Massive leak exposes 41,000 DHS workers and undercover ICE agents
2026-10-29

Massive leak exposes 41,000 DHS workers and undercover ICE agents

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
An unprecedented leak of Department of Homeland Security records has exposed details of more than 41,000 employees or contractors who work for the department and its constituent agencies. The records include email addresses, phone numbers, and office addresses, but also references to internal systems and sensitive information on undercover and surveillance programs. In all, the leak provides a map of how DHS is organized and how it operates. The records, which span DHS itself as well as seven component agencies, including Immigration and Customs Enforcement and US Customs and Border Protection, were last updated on August 19, 2026. The transparency nonprofit Distributed Denial of Secrets and cybersecurity researcher and journalist maia arson crimew previewed the data for WIRED, which they say they obtained from a group calling itself the Department of Peace. DDoSecrets published the archive, which it calls “Icepick,” on Wednesday. In a press release, DDoSecrets says the data is from DHS’s Okta login system. In a note with the files, the hackers wrote, “They have our information, so it’s only fair that we have theirs.” An Okta spokesperson tells WIRED that it is unaware of any breach of its systems. “We are aware of this report. Okta is secure, and we have no evidence our data or systems were compromised,” the spokesperson says. The leak comes as DHS has aggressively pursued its critics, seeking to unmask anonymous social media users, obtain customer records on protesters, and secure warrants for surveillance tools aimed at reporters. Since the start of the second Trump administration, DHS and the federal government have moved to hide DHS employees’ names and faces. The agency has also expanded its definition of “doxing”—a term that typically refers to publishing someone’s home address and other personal details for malicious purposes—to encompass the publication of basic facts about public employees, like their names and titles. The leak follows a breach in which a group of hackers known as ShinyHunters claimed to have stolen personal information on nearly every FBI employee and their families. Law enforcement agencies have arrested several suspects and detained another who is alleged to be part of the group. The DHS records are searchable on a website crimew built. “I don’t think we should let them scare us into not looking into them,” she tells WIRED. “DHS is aware of a potential breach and is actively working to address the situation swiftly and effectively,” a Homeland Security spokesperson tells WIRED. “We take all security threats against our employees and officers seriously and will work to ensure all are properly protected. We will update the public when more information is available.” The DOJ did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment. The FBI referred WIRED’s inquiry to DHS. According to a WIRED analysis of the data, CBP staffers account for more than half of the records. ICE accounts for about 10,000, roughly a third of them in Homeland Security Investigations, ICE’s investigative wing. Another 6,200 belong to US Citizenship and Immigration Services, and nearly 1,000 are tied to the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, more than half of them contractors. Altogether, the files cover about a seventh of DHS’s workforce, revealing how DHS is structured, from senior leadership down to individual immigration agents and detention contractors. Like most large organizations, DHS uses permission groups to control access to internal systems, according to the leaked records. Nearly everyone named in the leak has access to at least some of these systems. At HSI, 39 employees across more than a dozen field offices belong to groups whose names include “CUC,” which government court filings say stands for “certified undercover operation.” Another group, apparently based in HSI’s St. Paul, Minnesota, area of operations, references “Keyhole,” which federal court filings describe as a certified undercover operation that recently funded HSI’s undercover surveillance of Minneapolis protest groups earlier this year. Others reference a “Communications Intercept Center TechOps (CICTO) Program,” a “Title III Intercept Program” for wiretaps, and an “Illicit Sensor Intercept Program (ISIP).” The names of the groups—there are over 55,000 in all—appear to reference tools for accessing immigration court records, pulling data from seized phones, and conducting surveillance. These include Palantir’s Falcon investigative platform and PenLink, which analyzes phone records and social media. Two permission groups, named “Executive Leadership” and “AI Access,” appear to list people given exceptions to DHS’s web filter, which blocks some websites for employees. They contain the same 13 people, including former DHS head Kristi Noem; Antoine McCord, the recently departed DHS chief information officer who has returned to defense tech company Anduril; Philip Hegseth, listed as a senior adviser to the DHS secretary and the younger brother of Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth; and Jayce Krachenfels, listed as a policy adviser, who previously worked as a regional field director for Donald Trump’s 2024 presidential campaign, according to his LinkedIn profile. Noem, McCord, Hegseth, and Krachenfels did not respond to a request for comment Other groups named in the leak reference AI tools, including OpenAI’s ChatGPT, Google’s Gemini, and Microsoft’s Copilot. One, called “Grok.com,” includes eight DHS technology officials, including the department’s former chief information officer and chief data officer. “If authentic and current, this is an extraordinarily valuable intelligence resource for those seeking to penetrate DHS,” says Alexander Leslie, a national security expert at the threat intelligence company Recorded Future. “This dataset becomes way more valuable when correlated with previously compromised information, commercial data, and other intelligence collections. An adversary could spend months exploiting these relationships to identify vulnerable personnel, develop convincing impersonation attempts, or compromise ongoing investigations.” More than 700 accounts are associated with a group apparently referencing Signal, the encrypted messaging app, nearly all belonging to CBP personnel. More than 200 CBP accounts belong to a group referencing Mobile Fortify, a face-recognition app that immigration agents use to identify people. The group includes field supervisors, special response team operators, and tactical and intelligence agents. About half are in Texas, California, and Arizona. Roughly 1,300 ICE enforcement accounts have been created since July 4, 2025, when Trump signed the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which funded the hiring of 10,000 new ICE Enforcement and Removal officers by the end of 2025. Nearly 20 accounts appear to belong to personnel with the Department of Defense, which the administration styles as the Department of War; eight are in a group titled “ICE Tip Line DOW Surge.” About 170 accounts are listed as providing “military processing support” at ICE facilities, and 35 others explicitly identify National Guard members. Dozens of accounts have job titles like “Designated Immigration Officer” and “287(g) Officer,” which mark local law enforcement personnel authorized to carry out some immigration enforcement duties. Many list county sheriff’s offices and jails as their workplaces. The records also include roughly 4,400 “associates,” whose jobs range from IT developers and data scientists to nurses and detention officers. Of those, 145 are named as a “contractor” or “high risk contractor.” More than 300 records reference senior titles associated with DHS headquarters, from Noem down through deputy undersecretaries, assistant secretaries, and chiefs of staff to officials overseeing intelligence, technology, biometrics, and privacy. Most records also name a supervisor, which could allow anyone analyzing the dataset to reconstruct reporting relationships across the department. The Trump administration has prosecuted protesters for posting information about individual ICE officers and warned others for doing so. In June, 15 Minnesota residents were charged under laws related to stalking and threats of violence, which prosecutors linked to their participation in ICE-opposition activities. More than 100 HSI agents, including undercover operatives, took part in an operation that included widespread surveillance and infiltration of group chats. Adam Steinbaugh, a senior attorney at the nonprofit civil liberties group FIRE, says that the Trump administration has taken an expansive view of 18 USC 119, a law that prohibits people from sharing “restricted personal information” about federal officers or their families with the intent to threaten them. “This is information that would be on a business card,” says Steinbaugh of the names and basic work details included in the archive. “That’s not something the government can criminalize.” Despite the potential sensitivity of other records in the leaked data, the creators of Icepick do not consider it doxing. They see it as an accountability tool. “The DHS especially as an agency completely lacks accountability, despite many parts of it being incredibly unpopular. The people who work there know this too; for example, some of their LinkedIn bios simply say ‘stuff at US Government,’” says crimew. “If they weren’t hiding their involvement in the repressive arm of the US government, there would be no need for us to expose them in the first place.” This story originally appeared on wired.com.
Ars Technica - All content
SpaceX no longer needs NASA, and that's a problem for America's space agency
2026-10-29

SpaceX no longer needs NASA, and that's a problem for America's space agency

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
For nearly a quarter of a century, Elon Musk and his essential partner at SpaceX, Gwynne Shotwell, viewed the United States government as El Dorado, the mythical city of gold. Like machete-wielding treasure hunters, Musk and Shotwell hacked through a tangled jungle filled with lurking carnivores such as Boeing and Lockheed Martin, as well as bean counters and regulators. Over time, against long odds, the pair cleared a path for SpaceX to contracts at NASA and the US military. They sued when things did not go their way. They hollered about unfair playing fields. Most importantly, they delivered quality space products to the US government for less. And so, after a while, SpaceX found El Dorado and a city of contracts. Then they started winning everything. So much so that the relationship between the customer and contractor began to change. Slowly at first, but ever more quickly, SpaceX went from needing the US government as an indispensable customer to becoming an indispensable contractor for the nation. The pendulum has swung so far now that SpaceX seems increasingly disinterested in the US government for most contracts outside of data services, such as Starshield for military communications and observations. “I guess I am still in denial that there is a divorce pending,” said Lori Garver, a deputy NASA administrator during the Obama White House years who was instrumental in expanding the agency’s use of commercial contracts with companies like SpaceX. “I literally cannot wrap my head around a future yet where SpaceX stops flying Dragon and Falcon. Not yet.” But the signs point to at least a partial breakup. Consider the following data points: To be clear, SpaceX is still fulfilling its existing contracts with NASA, the US Space Force, and other government entities. And it continues to do so at a lower cost and generally higher level of reliability than pretty much anyone else. But the company is no longer signing many large new contracts for spaceflight services, at least in the traditional launch and in-space flight domains. The shift comes after 15 years in which the US government has grown accustomed to lower costs and rapid innovation that has revitalized the nation’s access to space. For SpaceX and its shareholders, as shocking as it may seem for a US company to leave billions of dollars on the table in government contracts, this may be the right decision. SpaceX sees the vast bulk of its future revenue coming from artificial intelligence. However, SpaceX’s retreat from the public square comes as both NASA and the Space Force confront growing challenges from a rising space program in China and an aging but still dangerous Russia in space. So what does it mean if the halcyon days of SpaceX riding to the rescue for America in space—such as in the saga of Butch Wilmore and Suni Williams—seem to be ending? In the early days, Musk and Shotwell sought revenue from various sources as SpaceX developed the scrappy Falcon 1 rocket. But it was NASA that stepped in at two crucial moments in the company’s first decade. The first came in 2006, with a $278 million award to develop a cargo delivery spacecraft for the International Space Station. It helped SpaceX expand rapidly when it was struggling to develop the Falcon 1 rocket. An even more consequential award arrived in December 2008, $1.6 billion for a dozen Dragon cargo flights to the space station. The funding arrived as SpaceX struggled to meet payroll. Both Musk and Shotwell have said this award probably saved SpaceX from bankruptcy. NASA and SpaceX then fell into an increasingly warm and mutually beneficial relationship. During the last two decades, NASA has awarded contracts to SpaceX worth approximately $20 billion, and Musk has been deeply appreciative. In 2017, he revealed that the online password for some of his accounts was “ILoveNASA.” In return, NASA got reliable cargo, eventually crew services into low-Earth orbit, and a revolution in US spaceflight capabilities. Musk and Shotwell fought to extend their relationship with the federal government in the mid-2010s. In 2014, they filed a lawsuit against the US Air Force for the right to bid for national security launch contracts. Musk contended that United Launch Alliance had a monopoly and said SpaceX should be eligible to bid. “We’re just protesting and saying these launches should be competed,” he said at the time. “And if we compete and lose, that’s fine, but why were they not even competed?” The effort proved successful. SpaceX won national security contracts, and launch costs for the military plunged. Over time, SpaceX became the dominant player. Even today, with United Launch Alliance and Blue Origin struggling to bring their large Vulcan and New Glenn rockets into service, SpaceX’s Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy rockets have become a single-point failure for national security launches. If the US military needed to get a dozen large satellites into orbit during the next 12 to 18 months, there is only one company that could credibly pull that off. The bottom line is that SpaceX, far more often than any other competitor over the last 15 years, delivered spaceflight services to the US government when other contractors were late or overbudget or simply failed. That no other commercial space company has risen alongside SpaceX during this period is a problem. NASA, certainly, sought to support competitors such as Blue Origin, Sierra Space, Rocket Lab, Orbital Sciences, Boeing, and others. The US military supported development of United Launch Alliance’s rocket with $2 billion in funding as an alternative to Falcon. And still, none have yet come remotely close to matching what SpaceX has done in launch, spacecraft, and in-space data services. The relationship dynamic began to change about half a decade ago. At the height of the pandemic, in mid-2020, SpaceX delivered a huge win for NASA by safely flying astronauts Doug Hurley and Bob Behnken to the space station. It is difficult to overstate the importance of this achievement for NASA. Since 2011, the space agency had swallowed its pride and gone, hat in hand, to Russia for transportation to the space station. Its astronauts spent months in Russia learning the language and training to ride the Soyuz spacecraft into orbit. With a single successful 64-day mission, SpaceX ended all of that. Less than a year later, NASA again turned to SpaceX. The space agency needed a Moon lander. But Congress had only appropriated beer money for a champagne project. NASA had three options for a privately developed lunar lander. None were ideal. SpaceX’s $2.89 billion proposal to develop Starship as a lunar lander and fly two missions was the only bid that fit NASA’s budget profile. Yes, Starship presented a complicated, oversized, and technically demanding solution. But in 2021, SpaceX was NASA’s only spaceflight contractor consistently delivering big results. Almost overnight, then, NASA’s two most important and costly programs, the Artemis Program to land humans on the Moon and the International Space Station, became utterly dependent on SpaceX. This power imbalance continued to grow, breaking into wider public view in June 2025 amid a temporary falling out between Musk and the president he helped elect. After Trump threatened to “terminate” SpaceX’s governmental contracts and subsidies, Musk’s response was essentially so what? In return, Musk threatened to decommission the Dragon spacecraft. He knew—correctly, it turns out—that SpaceX’s role in US government space programs could not be easily replaced. A Pentagon review in July 2025 found most of the company’s contracts with the US Department of Defense were essential. Trump could still have hurt Musk’s businesses through regulatory review, particularly with the Federal Communications Commission and Federal Aviation Administration. And the two made up. But one indisputable takeaway from this brouhaha is that the US government now needed SpaceX more than SpaceX needed the US government. “The dust-up with President Trump, when Musk threatened to take away Dragon, was utterly irresponsible,” said Chris Davenport, a professor at Arizona State University and author of Rocket Dreams. “If Falcon and Dragon go away, that is a huge disservice to the US government. NASA desperately needs SpaceX, and SpaceX knows it, and Elon knows it.” This represented a truly remarkable shift for everyone who watched SpaceX fight so hard, for so long, to get its foot in the door at NASA and the Space Force. But as jarring as it is, this shift is no longer deniable. Around the time of his dispute with Trump, Musk came to fully believe that artificial intelligence was not just the next big thing. Rather, it was the industrial revolution of his lifetime. He began vectoring his two biggest companies, Tesla and SpaceX, toward dominating AI software, AI hardware, and computing power on the ground and in space to run it all. This largely explains SpaceX’s acquisition of xAI and its subsequent IPO. It is also behind the potential merger of SpaceX and Tesla. This will give Musk more direct control over Tesla, something he values. But more critically, it gives him an immense pool of assets to deploy toward building data centers terrestrially and in space, autonomous vehicles, robots, massive rockets, and more. No other company on Earth has the potential, at least in the near term, to own an entire stack of artificial intelligence. Elon Musk wants to manufacture the chips and electricity infrastructure for data centers, he wants to build the data centers that will power the AI revolution, he wants to develop and train the AI models that will provide its intelligence, and he wants to build physical products that he believes will become the shaping force of the 21st century. Such a future, in his mind, does not include a dead-end technology like the Dragon spacecraft—even though Dragon is the only reliable way to get humans into space outside of China and Russia, and only took flight with humans six years ago. He does not want to fly the partially reusable Falcon 9 rocket—even though it is the best, cheapest, and only Western vehicle capable of expanding to meet the growing demand from government and commercial customers for access to space right now. Musk believes the government moves too slowly and simply isn’t ambitious enough when it comes to space. Often, in meetings at SpaceX, he will describe working with the US government as “like wearing lead shoes and walking through molasses.” Instead, Musk wants to build bigger things, like Starship. He wants to move fast and break things when necessary. This explains why he has invested something like $15 billion in the development of the Starship rocket, from its engines to a growing number of launch sites in Texas and Florida, to get the massive, fully reusable vehicle off the ground. For a time, this looked like a win-win situation for the US government and a growing commercial space industry counting on frequent, low-cost access to space. NASA built its Artemis Program around Starships landing on the Moon. The commercial space industry looked forward to abundant, cheap launches. The general sense was that pulling Dragon and Falcon 9 off the market would cause temporary upheaval, but these problems would soon be ameliorated by Starship and its promise of lower cost and even greater capability. In March 2020, SpaceX published its first “Starship Users Guide,” which included a 9-meter payload fairing with a “clamshell” structure that would open once in space to deploy payloads. The company said it would support Falcon-compatible payload adapters. But in recent years, there has been little evidence that SpaceX is actually developing such a deployment mechanism. Rather, the company has implemented a Pez-like dispenser that releases flattish satellites from a narrow door in the middle of the Starship vehicle. This serves SpaceX’s internal payloads of Starlink and future Starmind (data center) satellites. But it’s unsuitable for deploying more traditional satellites or big NASA payloads, such as large space telescopes. Multiple people Ars spoke with for this story said it would be incredibly beneficial if SpaceX publicly committed to developing a large payload door and provided a timeline for its readiness. Ars offered SpaceX the opportunity to make such a commitment, but the company didn’t take it. And it doesn’t have to. The reality is straightforward. This year’s IPO gave SpaceX a laser-like focus on its core business plan. Reaching stratospheric financial targets requires launching incredible numbers of Starlink and Starmind satellites, all of which can be dispensed like Pez candy into space. Launch services for third parties, which include NASA and other Moon Base customers, represent a rounding error in SpaceX’s revenue in the post-IPO world. In its recent third-quarter 10-Q financial filing, the company references these launch priorities for Starlink and artificial intelligence: “We allocate a significant amount of launch capacity to our Connectivity segment and expect to allocate a significant amount to our SI segment in the future.” An argument often made about SpaceX’s decision to end the Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy rocket is that these programs—which operate with unprecedented efficiency in the history of launch—consume valuable engineering time. This talented team pushed Falcon 9 above 150 launches a year and helped make SpaceX globally dominant. Musk has said he would like to redirect these talented engineers to bring similar launch-operation efficiency to the Starship program. Great engineers are certainly a finite resource. And SpaceX faces fierce competition in the US space industry for talented engineers, with higher salaries offered by Blue Origin, and attractive alternatives such as Stoke Space, Rocket Lab, Firefly, and Relativity Space all recruiting. The question is whether Musk would devote his Falcon engineering team solely to Starship. Perhaps not. During the company’s third-quarter earnings call in October, Musk made a striking comment while musing about how the company aimed to deploy 10 GW of compute (on Earth) in 2027. “What we’ve done at SpaceX is we’ve taken a team that has been able to achieve a market share in mass to orbit that is almost 90 percent,” Musk said. That team, he added, “has been able to achieve results that are an order of magnitude greater than the rest of all competitors in space combined.” Now, he said, SpaceX was applying that same team to the deployment of terrestrial compute. They would not be sent to Starbase for Starship, in other words, but to places like Memphis, Tennessee. “This is a bit like the New York Yankees playing in Little League,” Musk said. “It’s not fair. But the net result is being able to deploy power, cooling, and infrastructure at a rate that is vastly faster than anyone else and may at some point be faster than everyone else combined.” SpaceX can do what it wants. But there are consequences, and some people at NASA are starting to wonder how serious SpaceX is about the Human Landing System contract it won from NASA in 2021. Landing on the Moon is wholly tangential to the company’s near-term AI future, and fulfilling the contract will almost certainly be a net negative from a financial perspective. SpaceX, of course, says it is working assiduously on a lunar lander. And publicly, NASA officials say the company is a good partner. In an interview with Ars last week, the manager of the Artemis Program for NASA, Jeremy Parsons, said he regularly visits SpaceX facilities in Hawthorne, California, and Starbase, Texas. “Their management team is definitely committed to the lunar missions,” he said. Parsons credited the SpaceX engineers working on Starship as “some of the smartest individuals” he had ever met. No one I have spoken to at NASA, both on and off the record, says SpaceX has given them a reason to believe the company will not eventually meet its Human Landing System commitments. And to date, SpaceX has had a remarkably good track record of delivering for NASA on its contracts. Nevertheless, the road ahead may be rocky in an era when SpaceX is pulling back from government involvement. SpaceX has consistently told NASA it must get Starship flying orbital missions regularly. It has to get to full reusability, or nothing else matters. It has to do propellant transfer. All of that must come before finalizing the lander. And sure, it’s totally fair to say that the most important thing SpaceX can do for NASA right now is getting Starship to a very high flight rate. In SpaceX’s view, in a few years, Starship will be flying so often—the company recently told the Federal Aviation Administration it plans to launch 10,000 Starships a year by the 2030s—that fueling depots for missions to the Moon and Mars will be trivial. Such a launch cadence surely would change everything, but nothing in aerospace ever happens as fast as one thinks or wants. And for now, the real issue is just how committed SpaceX is to the lander project and NASA’s competition with China. At SpaceX, projects deemed most important by leadership get the most resources. That is, the best engineers, the most software support, the most funding, and so on. Those projects are currently things like Starmind, the Colossus data center project in Memphis, and Starship engineering. It’s not the Human Landing System, multiple sources told Ars. As SpaceX has struggled through the Starship test flight program, NASA has been waiting for an in-space fueling test, which has slipped its schedule many times. Testing propellant transfer and storage at a large scale is critical, and it has never been done in space. Successfully completing this test would reduce much of the risk for the kinds of refueling operations needed to send a fully loaded Starship to the Moon. The latest target for such a test is “early” 2027, according to NASA and SpaceX. But this date seems aspirational. According to Parsons, the test requires one Starship to launch from Starbase in Texas and another to launch from Launch Complex 39A at Kennedy Space Center in Florida. Starship has not yet flown from there. Presently, a debut Florida launch is not expected until at least January. According to Parsons, the in-orbit fueling test is the third launch planned from Florida. Once SpaceX demonstrates in-orbit refueling, it could begin planning a campaign to send an uncrewed Starship to land on the Moon as a demonstration. How many tanker launches would this take? The internal estimate at NASA’s Johnson Space Center is “about 22” Starship launches, Ars reported last week. Musk followed that up by saying the actual number “will be more like 6 to 8.” So what’s the truth? In the near term, given Starship’s lift capacity, a lunar landing will probably require about 20 launches, which is the most current publicly disclosed government planning figure. With improvements to Starship and Raptor engine performance, and as SpaceX gets a better handle on in-space refueling operations, that number will assuredly come down. So at some point in the future, it could be as low as six. NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman appeared to confirm this last week when he characterized Musk’s estimate of six to eight refueling flights as the “ultimate potential end state” of Starship. Unfortunately, to beat China back to the Moon by 2030, NASA can’t wait for Starship to reach its final form. If SpaceX were to fly a lunar demonstration in the next year or two, a necessary precursor to human landings, it probably would take something like 20 Starship launches. Starship has flown 14 times in the last three and a half years. Its flight rate will undoubtedly accelerate in the coming months, but even at a weekly launch cadence, fueling a Starship for a lunar landing would still require more than four months of dedicated launches. Frankly, it’s difficult to envision SpaceX dedicating 20 Starships to a NASA mission anytime soon. The opportunity cost is enormous. Those same rockets could launch hundreds of revenue-generating Starlink V3 satellites into orbit. A single Starlink launch on Starship delivers the same amount of bandwidth as 10 Falcon 9 launches—bandwidth SpaceX can sell to customers for years to come. And as SpaceX expands into artificial intelligence, the company has even greater incentives to reserve Starship’s capacity for its own growing constellation of satellites. And then there’s the financial burden. Let’s assume an unoptimized launch cost of $100 million for early Starship flights—still a phenomenally low number for a super heavy lift vehicle. At that price, a lunar landing campaign requiring 20 launches would cost approximately $2 billion in launch expenses alone. Recall that SpaceX’s original Human Landing System contract was valued at $2.89 billion and included both an uncrewed demo and crewed landing. The actual cost of a single lunar landing mission remains uncertain, and some of the investments needed to fulfill the contract will benefit Starship more broadly. But the larger point is clear: NASA is asking SpaceX to devote substantial resources to a program that offers comparatively little financial upside at a time when the company’s own commercial ambitions are growing exponentially. Imagine a world where SpaceX had gone all in on developing a lunar lander. Would the company be taking a different approach to the immense technical challenges of getting Starship to the Moon, perhaps with a stubby Starship variant? Would it be devoting more of its best engineers to the program? Would it consider using an expendable tanker Starship to reduce the total needed? SpaceX very well might still get to the Moon in the next five years, and the company has enormous technical capacity. Its history suggests that when Musk makes something an overriding priority, things happen quickly. All it would take for SpaceX to go full throttle on a lunar Starship would be for Musk to text a technical leader at the company something like, “Do HLS.” Last year, Ars asked Musk if it mattered to him that China gets back to the Moon before NASA with the Artemis Program. “I think the United States should be aiming for Mars, because we’ve already actually been to the Moon several times,” he replied. “Yeah, if China sort of equals that, I’m like, OK, sure, but that’s something that America did 56 years ago.” In November of last year, Ars posed a similar question to Blue Origin’s chief executive, Dave Limp. “We just want to help the US get to the Moon,” he said. “If NASA wants to go quicker, we would move heaven and Earth, pun intended, to try to get to the Moon sooner.” Multiple NASA sources said Blue really is doing whatever it takes to help NASA and that what once appeared as a rocky relationship is now flourishing. After SpaceX won a sole-source contract for the Human Landing System in 2021, Blue Origin sued NASA. This pressure, along with NASA’s desire for a second provider, led Congress to eventually add funding for a second lander. As with Starship, Blue Origin’s original design under this new contract also involved refueling operations in low-Earth orbit for the large Blue Moon Mark 2 vehicle. As it became increasingly clear that China was on course to land humans on the Moon by or before 2030, Blue Origin’s large and ambitious Blue Moon lander did not serve NASA’s more immediate needs. It would take too long. In 2025, Ars was first to report that Blue Origin might be able to develop a lunar landing system that involved a smaller version of the Blue Moon Mark 2 vehicle and multiple transfer stages based on the existing Mark 1 lander. This involved a significant redesign to the Blue Moon program, but critically, it removed the need for in-space refueling. Eventually, this became the new plan. Blue Origin, without any guarantee of new funding, changed its design when NASA needed something faster. “I will tell you, they are absolutely fantastic partners,” Parsons, NASA’s Artemis manager, said of Blue Origin. “They have leaned forward at every step, and they are all in on the mission.” NASA’s upcoming Artemis III mission is illustrative of the differences. The mission is intended to test both Blue Origin and SpaceX’s lunar landers in space. Blue Origin is building a special “test” version of its Blue Moon lander to demonstrate a host of capabilities for NASA. After docking, the crew will exit the Orion spacecraft and spend time aboard the Blue Moon vehicle. By contrast, SpaceX is pulling a “ship off the line” of its Starship program for the flight. Orion will rendezvous and dock with Starship, but there will be no ingress into the vehicle and hence no life support equipment. Earlier this year, NASA revealed a major new initiative to develop a Moon Base. Not long after, New Glenn exploded on its launch pad. In the aftermath, Blue Origin has moved with uncharacteristic speed to repair its launch pad to get New Glenn flying again, while investing $3 billion in Florida facilities to support a much higher launch cadence. This matters because New Glenn is superseding Starship as the essential rocket powering NASA’s lunar ambitions. Beyond the Human Landing System, there are myriad robotic elements of the Moon Base that need to get to the surface, and NASA has contracted with a host of US space companies to develop smaller cargo landers. Some of these providers are growing nervous about launch. At least two of these companies had “rough order of magnitude” estimates and other types of launch agreements with SpaceX for Falcon 9s to deliver elements of NASA’s Moon base later this decade. But in the last several weeks, SpaceX, the only company that can offer reliable launch dates, has told them it will no longer sell rides to the Moon. This has sent shockwaves through the Artemis community. It is not the behavior of a partner being broadly supportive of NASA’s lunar efforts. These smaller lander companies now must hope that New Glenn can pick up the slack or a company like Relativity Space can bring its Terran R rocket to readiness quickly. Ars pressed Parsons on how committed SpaceX really was to NASA’s lunar activities. For example, did he really believe SpaceX would be willing to sideline its profitable Starlink and Starmind launches for the extended period to support an Artemis landing? “It’s a fair question, especially as they have gone public,” he acknowledged. For more than a decade, NASA more or less turned its spaceflight fortunes over to one billionaire. That partnership now appears to be unraveling, and there seems to be a growing sense that if the space agency is to achieve its considerable lunar ambitions in the next decade, it may need another billionaire to bail it out. “This could end up being a very big opportunity for Blue Origin and Jeff Bezos, who would absolutely relish the role of coming to the country in its time of need,” said Davenport, the author who has written about the rocket billionaires. “Bezos has been public in saying he wanted to serve his country, particularly if it meant in doing it instead of SpaceX and Elon.” Bezos, of course, is not doing this out of the goodness of his heart or because he bleeds red, white, and blue. He stands to gain Artemis customers, national security launch contracts, and commercial business. He is chasing SpaceX’s enormous valuation with his own space company. But no one thinks Bezos would pull his rockets off the market if NASA were desperate to get to the Moon. The real question is whether Blue Origin can succeed. The company has a long, long list of things to achieve to make Artemis a reality, from rockets to landers to in-space operations. And for all the many billions of dollars Bezos has invested, Blue Origin has a short track record of execution. It has only ever launched three orbital missions. Now it is being called on to spearhead the landing of humans on the Moon. On a rocket that recently blew up. Aboard a lander that doesn’t yet exist. At the 11th hour.

ULA's Vulcan rocket is set to return to flight on Friday the 13th
2026-10-28

ULA's Vulcan rocket is set to return to flight on Friday the 13th

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
United Launch Alliance announced Wednesday it plans to return its Vulcan rocket to service on November 13, nine months after a solid rocket booster anomaly threw the company’s 2026 launch schedule into oblivion. The return-to-flight mission will launch 40 satellites for the Amazon Leo broadband constellation, the first of 38 Vulcan flights Amazon has booked to deploy the bulk of its network. The mission will lift off from Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida, and ULA said the launch date is pending range approval. The launch is critical for ULA after its new flagship rocket suffered nozzle failures on its solid rocket boosters on two of its first four missions, most recently during a flight in February. In both cases, the rocket overcame the nozzle failure and completed its mission, but officials at the US Space Force, ULA’s primary customer, lost confidence in Vulcan. Space Force officials will closely scrutinize Vulcan’s return-to-flight mission. The Pentagon has entrusted ULA’s Vulcan rocket for roughly half of its major launches through the early 2030s. The Space Force certified the Vulcan rocket for national security launches in 2025, then flew two missions on Vulcan before its grounding after the February booster incident. In a statement, United Launch Alliance said engineers “made great strides” in understanding the booster anomaly that occurred in February. Engineers “have identified several potential contributors, which have been mitigated through design and process improvements to the nozzle,” ULA said. “To support our customers, this upcoming launch will utilize the improved nozzle design, and additional testing and analysis will continue in parallel with the mission,” ULA said. “By flying the improved design, ULA gains valuable flight data that validates these improvements and is the most beneficial path in support of all our customers.” ULA and Northrop Grumman, the booster manufacturer, have not publicly identified the root cause of the nozzle malfunction in February. Investigators blamed the nozzle problem’s first occurrence in 2024 on a manufacturing defect in an insulator meant to protect the nozzle’s metallic structure from the rocket’s superheated exhaust. Neither company responded to questions from Ars on Wednesday. The Space Force is counting on United Launch Alliance, a 50-50 joint venture between Boeing and Lockheed Martin, to deliver into orbit a backlog of the military’s communications, navigation, missile warning, and surveillance satellites. Amazon Leo, a potential competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink network, also has an inventory of broadband satellites in storage waiting for rides on the Vulcan rocket. With ULA’s delays, the Space Force has moved six missions off the Vulcan rocket to expedite their launch on SpaceX Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy rockets. ULA received six future missions originally assigned to SpaceX, the military’s other certified launch provider. The swaps included three GPS satellites that moved off ULA’s Vulcan rocket for launches last year and earlier this year on SpaceX Falcon 9s. Space Systems Command, which oversees military launch acquisition and procurement, also swapped one ULA launch for the Space Development Agency with another on SpaceX. A launch carrying the Air Force Research Laboratory’s Oracle-M lunar mission will now fly on SpaceX’s Falcon Heavy rocket instead of ULA’s Vulcan, which will receive a future SpaceX-assigned launch in compensation. Space Systems Command completed a similar swap to move a large strategic missile warning satellite from Vulcan to Falcon Heavy. So far the swaps have had no effect on the number of missions the Space Force has awarded to ULA and SpaceX. The trades are separate from the Space Force’s assignment of unallocated future missions to launch providers, which may already be hamstrung by SpaceX’s plans to retire the Falcon rocket family. “These swaps are executed in coordination with and with the support of both launch service providers,” a Space Systems Command spokesperson said. “We are grateful for the flexibility shown by our launch service providers during these swaps to help us ensure we can deliver capabilities to the warfighter when and where they are needed.” Brig. Gen. Eric Zarybnisky, the chief rocket buyer at Space Systems Command, said the military wants to see data from commercial flights on the Vulcan rocket before clearing ULA to resume national security launches. At least two Amazon Leo missions are on the Vulcan launch schedule before it flies another national security mission, perhaps early next year. “Those flights will have a significant number of solids,” Zarybnisky said. “It’ll depend a lot on what we see, not only on the engineering and tests that we’re doing on the ground, but also what we see from the flight data from the commercial missions they fly.” The Vulcan launch next month will be the first to fly with a full complement of six solid rocket boosters. The flight will also debut an extended payload fairing to accommodate Amazon’s satellite stack and a shortened Centaur upper stage optimized for low-Earth orbit missions.
Ars Technica - All content
As US ties fray, Europe plans first undersea cable link to Greenland
2026-10-28

As US ties fray, Europe plans first undersea cable link to Greenland

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The European Union plans to create the first subsea Internet cable directly connecting Europe to Greenland. The Pan-Arctic Cable project could also extend to Canada and eventually link with Japan and South Korea, notably bypassing the United States. The Pan-Arctic Cable is described as a European Union flagship project in a European Commission joint communication published on October 20, 2026. The policy document describes a new European strategy for the Arctic that supports “a sovereign, secure and resilient submarine cable connection linking Greenland directly to the EU,” while also suggesting that “like-minded partners like Canada” could provide a stepping stone for future cable expansion toward Asia. “It is interesting that this project is even being considered, because five years ago it would have seemed like not a particularly commercially attractive proposition,” Elisabeth Braw, a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council who leads the Threats to the Global Maritime Order Initiative, told Ars. “But the fact that it’s being considered is very much on trend and a sign of our times.” The project has gained official support after repeated Trump administration attempts to claim Greenland were finally settled through a trilateral security agreement between the United States, Greenland, and Denmark in September 2026. The agreement preserves Greenland’s autonomous territory status within the Kingdom of Denmark while expanding US military access. But relations have grown increasingly strained between the United States and its allies in Europe and Canada since President Trump initiated trade wars with multiple countries and has taken an increasingly pro-Russia stance on the war in Ukraine. Europe has also been eyeing new Internet cable routes to Asia, because more than 90 percent of communications between Europe and Asia run through undersea cables in the Red Sea, according to TeleGeography. Maintaining such cables has become riskier since open warfare resumed between the Yemeni government and Houthi faction this year, with Houthi forces having fired missiles and drones at commercial ships in the Bab al-Mandeb Strait chokepoint and the broader Red Sea. The Pan-Arctic Cable took shape when European companies and research organizations joined forces with a Greenland telecom operator to sign an agreement on developing the project in 2025, according to Submarine Cable Networks. Since that time, the project has received European Union backing through the Connecting Europe Facility Digital program. The European Union has already invested $119 million (107 million euros) to “advance Greenland’s secure and sovereign connectivity” along with funding the Pan-Arctic Cable plan, according to the new European Commission policy document on Europe’s Arctic strategy. But the goal of eventually extending the undersea cable to Japan and South Korea by way of Canada faces considerable challenges, including requiring more European icebreaker ships to support the installation and maintenance of the cable in icy Arctic waters. Multiple pan-Arctic projects have been proposed in the past, and some are still in development. One earlier project, known as the Arctic Connect subsea cable, aimed to connect Europe and Asia using the Northern Sea Route running along Russia’s Arctic coastline—but that was eventually halted in May 2021 before the full-scale Russian invasion of Ukraine led to growing Russian isolation from Europe. Still, the Pan-Arctic Cable is not the only subsea cable project that could strengthen Europe’s ties to friendlier nations. In September 2026, Norwegian company Bulk Infrastructure Group AS began looking for a Canadian partner to take a majority ownership stake in a new transatlantic cable directly linking Europe to Canada, according to Bloomberg News. That proposed project has been named the Leif Erikson system. In previous decades, “geopolitics played virtually no role when companies considered where to put their cables and what countries to connect,” said Braw, whose book Undersea War explores why the world has become more dangerous for the Internet’s undersea backbone. But the focus on maximizing data connectivity and Internet traffic has given way to more complex planning that also accounts for international relations and the growing risk of adversaries carrying out surveillance or sabotage of cables. “We are going to see more cables that connect friendly countries, and then you have to figure out how you can connect friendly countries without going through unfriendly countries’ exclusive economic zones or territorial waters,” Braw told Ars. Growing international tensions have coincided with suspicious incidents in which Russian- and Chinese-linked commercial ships severed European undersea cables in the Baltic Sea by dragging their anchors for long periods of time. In spring 2026, NATO countries’ forces also confronted Russian submarines that were practicing how to disable undersea cables in Arctic waters near Norway, Reuters reported. Europe’s new Arctic strategy document addresses such security threats by stating that the European Union will establish an Arctic Cable Hub to “monitor, protect and support the repair of submarine cables in the North Atlantic-Arctic.” It also says the European Union will invest in “smart technologies on cables that act as early warning systems for potential threats.” Such risks for undersea infrastructure are not limited to the Baltic Sea or Atlantic Ocean. In the Pacific Ocean, Chinese-linked commercial ships have also severed undersea cables near Taiwan. Analysts have documented Chinese research vessels spending unusual amounts of time near Pacific undersea cables and warned of the rising risks for US-linked subsea cable projects that are often owned by Big Tech companies such as Google and Meta. Meanwhile, Iran has threatened to charge fees to subsea cable operators whose cables run through the Strait of Hormuz chokepoint. That action comes as part of a broader regional war in the Middle East that began when the United States and Israel attacked Iran in February 2026—and Iran has drawn up plans to potentially attack undersea cables if hostilities ramp up once again.

Peptide peddlers cheer as RFK Jr. unilaterally grants access, overruling FDA
2026-10-28

Peptide peddlers cheer as RFK Jr. unilaterally grants access, overruling FDA

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy has unilaterally passed a rule that will allow specialty pharmacies to sell six specific peptide drugs, overruling scientists at the Food and Drug Administration. The peptides are popular among members of Kennedy’s Make America Health Again movement, and many prominent MAHA figures and Kennedy allies sell them. Kennedy said he uses some of the peptides and called himself a “big fan.” But FDA scientists continue to say there is insufficient evidence to show if they’re effective, and they are potentially dangerous. In 2023, the safety concerns led the FDA to remove the six peptides from a list of drugs that can be made by specialty pharmacies called compounding pharmacies—which make customized formulations of drugs for patients, or, in cases such as this, specialty drugs in bulk. Kennedy’s new rule sidesteps the need to formally undo that FDA action. The rule is titled “Enforcement Discretion Policy Regarding 503A Compounding Using Certain Peptide Bulk Drug Substances” and was made available Wednesday on the Federal Register, with the final publication date set for October 30. The rule does not change the regulatory status of the peptide; instead, it states the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) will no longer take enforcement action against compounding pharmacies for making the peptides, essentially giving them a green light to freely produce and sell them. Kennedy had quietly proposed this rule earlier this month. Along with the notice in the Federal Register, HHS also posted a more detailed explanation of the policy today that includes more of Kennedy’s rationale for easing enforcement of the six select peptides, which are: BPC-157, Epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500. One reason cited for all the peptides was that an FDA advisory panel voted in July to add these six peptides back to the list of drugs compound pharmacies can make. However, prior to the vote, Kennedy stacked that 12-member committee with eight new members. At least six of Kennedy’s additions have ties to peptide-related businesses and clinics, raising serious conflict-of-interest concerns. Another explanation for allowing the peptides is that the safety concerns are mainly hypothetical or “inferential” and not based on observed harm in humans. But this is mainly because there are few to no studies involving humans that show either benefits or harms from the peptides. Still, Kennedy argues in the policy that making the peptides more available through compounding pharmacies will improve safety because it will keep people from buying them on the gray or black market or buying formulas not intended for patient use. “The Secretary of HHS has determined that this limited enforcement-discretion policy will discourage the continued, widespread use of certain research-use-only peptides supplied by foreign manufacturers that pose a safety risk to US patients,” the HHS policy document states. Calling the compounded peptides “a more controlled alternative” to illegal versions of the peptides, the document says that “the Secretary has determined that the policy advances patient choice and freedom while appropriately protecting patient safety.” Peptide sellers online were quick to celebrate the news. Brigham Buhler, who sells peptides in a wellness clinic he runs, cheered the news on X, thanking Kennedy, President Trump, Calley Means, and Joe Rogan. “Medical freedom just took a major step forward!” he wrote. Zachary Shurtleff, a marketing executive for a wellness-focused compounding pharmacy, also celebrated, writing, “Peptides are BACK like they never shoulda left!” Lee Rosebush, chair of the American Academy of Peptide Medicine, a group formed last year, released a statement thanking Kennedy and the Trump administration for “taking a forward-thinking, evidence-based approach to the regulation of peptides.” Critics have pushed back, noting that there are already reports of harms from peptides. In a statement released Tuesday, before the new rule was released, the Center for Science in the Public Interest laid out several reasons why making unproven peptides more accessible is dangerous. For one, reports of harms will “continue to mount,” CSPI said. And if any of the peptides actually showed promise of efficacy, reputable pharmaceutical companies will have less incentive to follow up with thorough clinical trials if the peptide is already widely available. Further, allowing access to peptides based on marketing demand could open the doors for other unapproved products, creating an incentive for illegal sales. Fred Mills, chief policy officer of Americans for Safe and Effective Medicines (ASEM), said in a statement Wednesday that the new rule “does not resolve problems with untested products, safety risks, questionable ingredients, or misleading claims. In fact, we believe this will worsen what we have already seen—the harms from mass compounding hitting communities hard due to a lack of oversight to rein in bad actors. We need to follow the science, and the reality is that FDA itself has continued to sound the alarm on lackluster data on these peptides. Safety and efficacy aren’t a nice-to-have; they are required.”
Ars Technica - All content
Trump admin sues Utah to save Kalshi from the state's total ban on gambling
2026-10-28

Trump admin sues Utah to save Kalshi from the state's total ban on gambling

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The Trump administration is trying to stop Utah from enforcing its gambling ban against Kalshi, making Utah the 10th state sued by the US government agency that regulates prediction markets. Utah, which prohibits gambling, sued Kalshi in a state court last week. Utah’s lawsuit seeks an order prohibiting Kalshi from offering bets on sports, elections, politics, and other events. “Kalshi runs an online casino in Utah and calls it a prediction market. That violates Utah law,” Utah Attorney General Derek Brown, a Republican, said last week. “Not only do our state laws ban gambling but Utah is one of only two states where gambling is also prohibited under the state constitution. For 130 years, Utah has been clear: gambling has no place here, and we will enforce our laws.” Utah already defeated Kalshi in federal court after the prediction market filed a preemptive lawsuit that sought to prevent the state from enforcing its gambling laws. Now that Utah is moving ahead in its attempt to shut down Kalshi, it must defend against a lawsuit filed by the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC). The CFTC “has filed a lawsuit against Utah to block the state’s efforts to shut down CFTC-registered contract markets using state laws,” the commission said yesterday. “The lawsuit follows Utah’s decision to file civil enforcement actions in state court against CFTC-regulated markets and other businesses. Utah’s effort to restrict the functioning of CFTC-registered exchanges obstructs Congress’s decision to federally preempt state law.” If Utah can shut down Kalshi, that could spell trouble for Kalshi’s chief prediction-market rival, Polymarket, and for sports betting sites like DraftKings and FanDuel. DraftKings and FanDuel advertise availability in all US states via a mix of products, including prediction-market platforms in states like Utah. Both are registered with the CFTC. The Utah attorney general’s office declined to comment today on the CFTC lawsuit. The CFTC previously filed lawsuits against Arizona, Connecticut, Illinois, Kentucky, Minnesota, New Mexico, New York, Rhode Island, and Wisconsin. The federal agency also filed briefs supporting Kalshi in appeals court cases. “Utah seeks to shut down prediction markets across a wide variety of markets,” CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said. “Event contracts do not just serve hedging and investment purposes—they also provide the public with information about the outcome of future events. Congress entrusted the CFTC to be the federal regulator with sole responsibility to oversee these markets. As I’ve said before, the CFTC has the expertise and responsibility to defend its exclusive jurisdiction over commodity derivatives, and that’s exactly what we’ll do.” The US Commodity Exchange Act (CEA) gives the CFTC authority to regulate swaps. The key legal questions being grappled with by courts throughout the US are whether event contracts offered by prediction markets qualify as swaps, and whether the CFTC jurisdiction over swaps preempts states from applying their gambling laws. The CFTC is suing Utah in the same US district court that previously ruled in Utah’s favor. Kalshi sued Utah in February in an attempt to stop the state from bringing an enforcement action against the prediction market. In August, US District Judge Robert Shelby granted Utah’s motion for summary judgment and denied Kalshi’s motion for a preliminary injunction. Shelby wrote, “[T]he court concludes the federal law relied upon by Kalshi does not preempt Utah’s ability to enforce its anti-gambling laws.” Shelby determined the text of US law makes it clear that state regulation is not forbidden. US law states that CFTC jurisdiction does not “supersede or limit the jurisdiction conferred on courts of the United States or any State,” and “explicitly provides for State regulation of its gaming laws in all but a few specified cases,” he wrote. “Congress is aware that some States permit gambling while others do not, and the CEA explicitly provides for State jurisdiction,” Shelby wrote. Kalshi is now appealing its loss to the US Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit but was unable to obtain an injunction preventing enforcement of the state gambling ban while that litigation continues. On September 8, Tenth Circuit judges denied Kalshi’s emergency motion for an injunction pending appeal. Utah filed its lawsuit seeking a shutdown of Kalshi about six weeks later. The CFTC lawsuit against Utah makes arguments similar to the ones already rejected by the district court, although some other courts have ruled otherwise. “Utah is not the first State that has attempted to invade the Commission’s exclusive jurisdiction over swaps,” the CFTC lawsuit said. “Already, several federal courts—including the Third Circuit—have swiftly responded by issuing or affirming temporary restraining orders and preliminary injunctions barring the States from enforcing their gambling laws against CFTC-regulated exchanges that offer sports-related or other event contracts.” The Third Circuit appeals court ruled that New Jersey cannot regulate sports bets on prediction markets. But Kalshi lost similar cases in the Sixth Circuit and Ninth Circuit in rulings that went in favor of Ohio, Tennessee, and Nevada. Because circuit appeals courts are divided on the issue, it is likely that the Supreme Court will take up the case and determine whether states can apply their gambling laws to prediction markets. New Jersey petitioned the Supreme Court to resolve the circuit split, and 39 other states and the District of Columbia filed a brief supporting New Jersey’s petition.
Fortune | FORTUNE
Can Southeast Asia pitch itself as an ‘all-in-one’ chip hub?
2026-10-29

Can Southeast Asia pitch itself as an ‘all-in-one’ chip hub?

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Southeast Asia is riding an investment boom, as a surge in chip exports pushes economies like Vietnam and the Philippines up the World Bank’s income ladder, as defined by the World Bank. Two forces are driving this surge: the AI wave and an intensifying rivalry between Washington and Beijing that has companies scrambling to diversify where they make things. Together, these trends are building a regional chip export industry worth roughly $258 billion. But Southeast Asian governments aren’t satisfied with just housing a chip foundry or a packaging facility. The region wants to be a single, integrated supply chain right as the U.S.-China rivalry threatens to split the sector in two. In 2025, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) launched the ASEAN Framework for an Integrated Semiconductor Supply Chain (AFISS). The initiative—a signature deliverable of Malaysia’s 2025 chairship—hinges on each country playing a specialized, complementary role to move up the chip value chain. Now Southeast Asia can cement its role in the global supply chain for semiconductors, especially as AI demand makes the region’s position more valuable than ever. “Cultivation of semiconductor supply chains has put the region in the right place at the right time,” says Robert Walker, a research fellow at the Lowy Institute’s Indo-Pacific Development Centre. Malaysia, Singapore, Vietnam, Thailand and the Philippines have all benefited from the chip surge: Exports of semiconductors and semiconductor parts and components from Southeast Asia to China and the U.S. reached $42.2 billion and $15.1 billion respectively in 2023. “Southeast Asia has emerged as a critical semiconductor manufacturing hub, particularly for assembly, testing and packaging. The region has attracted substantial investment since the pandemic as companies seek to diversify supply chains, manage exposure to U.S.-China tensions and build greater resilience,” Piriya Piriyakrit, senior analyst at BowerGroupAsia, says. Walker thinks AI supply chains are “hitting their productive limits”—and that the capacity crunch that will follow gives Southeast Asia an opening. “As demand outpaces supply, new production capacity will be required, and Southeast Asia is well placed to drive that expansion across semiconductor supply chains, helping the region move up the value chain and increase its exports,” he says. Southeast Asia is an obvious alternative for companies pursuing a “China plus one” strategy, diversifying their supply chains away from China. That is the gap AFISS is meant to fill. Walker argues that while the framework won’t establish ASEAN as a “unified production base or single investment destination,” it could still deepen regional integration and improve overall competitiveness in chips. Yet ASEAN is not the European Union. The bloc emphasizes national sovereignty and non-interference, which means any framework—even a well-intentioned one—is only as good as each member’s choice to acknowledge it, let alone implement it. Chandra Putra, a Jakarta-based economist who has researched the region’s semiconductor sector, calls AFISS a “fundamentally good idea” to harmonize national agendas and build a more transparent regional ecosystem. But he cautions that its feasibility “hinges on countries diverging from their national interests.” But Southeast Asian coordination may happen without ASEAN’s guidance, argues Hans Vriens, co-founder and managing partner of government affairs consultancy Vriens & Partners. “If you look at the different parts of the supply chain, they are present in different countries. In that sense, Southeast Asia is already taking care of the whole supply chain because the countries are complementary as investment destinations,” he says. Singapore, for example, focuses on research and development; assembly, testing, and packaging is more common in Malaysia and the Philippines. What matters more is driving down the costs companies face when they produce across borders within ASEAN, through faster customs clearance, mutual recognition of technical standards and professional qualifications, and easier movement of skilled workers, says Arata Kuno, professor of international economics at Asia University in Japan. The framework could work, Kuno suggests, if the aim is to create a network that links members’ strengths while staying plugged into global supply chains instead of building a self-sufficient bloc. “Insisting on covering every stage of chipmaking within ASEAN could backfire and erode competitiveness,” Kuno cautions. Japan is a warning. Its chipmakers once led the world in market share, yet it held onto a vertically-integrated business model, keeping everything from design to manufacturing in-house. “That model is often cited as one reason their share later declined: it made them slower to respond as demand shifted from memory to logic chips, and to keep up with soaring investment costs and the growing international division of labor,” Kuno says. Still, there is value in concerted regional coordination. Individually, Southeast Asian countries “cannot match the depth of capital, specialized talent and the proprietary technology in places such as the U.S., China, Europe, and Northeast Asia,” says Andrew Chan Yik Hong, former founding executive director of the Malaysia Semiconductor Industry Association. Singapore is the regional leader in semiconductors, hosting fabrication plants for companies like Micron Technology and GlobalFoundries. Now Malaysia and the Philippines want to follow the city-state’s lead and move up the value chain, after decades specializing in back-end manufacturing—assembly, testing and packaging. Malaysia has been transitioning toward front-end manufacturing and chip design since it launched its National Semiconductor Strategy in 2024. In September, Manila unveiled a roadmap with ambitious targets to grow its annual chips and electronics exports to $110 billion by 2030, and move beyond manufacturing and assembly into higher-end operations. Yet on-the-ground evidence of success is slim. Malaysia’s $250 million deal with British chip designer Arm brought the country closer to making its own chips, but is currently being probed by the country’s anti-corruption agency. A talent crunch is also a barrier. On Oct. 26, Reuters reported that Vietnam’s first semiconductor plant will likely miss its 2027 target to start a trial production run, with sources citing a lack of experience at the plant’s owner, Viettel. Fortunately, moving up the value chain “does not have to mean building cutting-edge front-end fabs, which require enormous capital, reliable power and water, and highly skilled workers,” Kuno says. The most promising opportunity is advanced packaging, which has become increasingly central to the performance of AI chips, Kuno adds. The old view that back-end activities in chipmaking are uniformly low value-added “is no longer accurate,” as advanced packaging commands much higher margins than conventional assembly and testing. Piriya argues that the region’s core advantage isn’t from trying to replicate Taiwan or South Korea, “but its role as a connector across global supply chains.” “Southeast Asia combines scale, manufacturing experience, strategic geography and a diverse set of investment destinations. Importantly, moving up the semiconductor value chain does not necessarily require countries to develop advanced chip fabrication capabilities,” she added. Yet protecting the region’s global chip market share is just the bare minimum, Chan says. While Micron, Intel, and other companies have had decades to grow their footprint in the region, the greater ambition for the region should be to build stronger Southeast Asian companies with their own technology and regional or global reach. “Multinational companies brought investment, technology and customer relationships, and provided a substantial portion of workforce training…But attracting FDI and bringing technology into a country does not automatically generate technological spillovers to local companies,” he says. “That requires substantial patient capital, sustained research and development, access to customers, and policies that survive changes in political leadership.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. ‘The inflation outlook has become more concerning’: Incomes declined 0.3% and consumer prices jumped 3.6% in September as midterms loom
2026-10-29

‘The inflation outlook has become more concerning’: Incomes declined 0.3% and consumer prices jumped 3.6% in September as midterms loom

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
An inflation gauge closely monitored by the Federal Reserve accelerated last month, showing that prices remain elevated less than a week before midterm elections in which the uncomfortably high cost of living for millions of Americans has become a central issue. Consumer prices rose 3.6% in September from a year earlier, the Commerce Department said Thursday, up from 3.4% in August. On a monthly basis, prices climbed 0.5%, up from 0.3% in August and the highest figure since May. Rising gas prices drove much of the inflation increase as renewed violence in the Middle East has kept oil prices above $100 a barrel, up from below $70 before the Iran war. But prices also rose outside of fuel costs. The cost of services such as shipping, hotel rooms, and utilities increased rapidly from August to September, a sign that inflation is spreading and growing more entrenched. “The inflation outlook has become more concerning,” Ksenia Bushmeneva, an economist at TD, a bank, wrote in a commentary on the report. “Price pressures are showing signs of heating up. Elevated gasoline prices are keeping headline inflation hot, but underlying pressures are also broadening.” Polls show that the rise in inflation has soured most voters on the economy, leading them to give President Donald Trump poor marks on handling rising prices. Trump campaigned in 2024 on lowering the cost of living, including bringing the cost of gas below $2 a gallon. It currently averages about $4.25 a gallon nationwide. Excluding the volatile energy and food categories, core inflation also picked up last month. It increased 3.1% from a year ago in September, up from 3% in August, the Commerce Department said. And from August to September, core prices rose 0.3%, the same as the previous month. Separately, the government also said the economy grew at a strong 3.4% annual rate in the July-September quarter, much faster than the 2.2% growth in the April-June quarter, as consumer spending jumped and business investment rose, fueled by spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure. Consumer spending climbed 3.8% at an annual rate in the third quarter, the second straight quarter of strong growth and far above the 0.7% increase in the first three months of the year. Yet there are signs that consumers may struggle to maintain that level of spending. Inflation is eroding their incomes: In September, inflation-adjusted incomes, after taxes, declined 0.3% from the previous month. Consumers appear to be dipping into savings or relying on credit to fuel much of their consumption. “Together with higher borrowing costs, these headwinds are likely to weigh on consumer demand in the coming quarters,” Bushmeneva said.” The reports come a day after the Federal Reserve, the agency tasked with combatting inflation, left its key interest rate unchanged. Yet strong economic growth and persistently higher prices could mean more pain for Americans when the Fed meets in December. The Fed is much more likely to hike its key interest rate, which could lift borrowing costs across the economy. Thursday’s inflation data comes from the personal consumption expenditures price index, which typically echoes the better-known consumer price index published earlier this month. The Fed more closely monitors the PCE index because it puts less weight on housing prices and allows for changes in consumers’ shopping habits. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Alaska’s governor accuses Dem Peltola’s campaign for ‘deliberately’ tricking voters after recruiting candidate ‘with the same name’ as her opponent
2026-10-29

Alaska’s governor accuses Dem Peltola’s campaign for ‘deliberately’ tricking voters after recruiting candidate ‘with the same name’ as her opponent

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Republican Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy has ordered an investigation into the Democratic candidate running for the U.S. Senate in the state after revelations that her campaign appeared to be involved in recruiting another candidate with the same name as Republican incumbent Dan Sullivan. Dunleavy’s order, posted overnight on social media, comes a day after President Donald Trump called for the criminal prosecution of Mary Peltola and her campaign. In a race that’s among a handful of Senate contests this year expected to determine control of the chamber, the Republican president had previously directed his Department of Justice to sue to block certification of it, demanding that Alaska be prevented from certifying a win by Peltola if Daniel J. Sullivan Jr. remains on the state’s ballots. Dunleavy wrote that reporting by The New York Times “reveals disturbing new evidence of an organized effort to manipulate Alaska’s elections by recruiting candidates — including one with the same name as an incumbent U.S. Senator — to deliberately confuse voters and misdirect their votes.” “Deliberately tricking voters into casting their ballots for someone else strikes at the integrity of our elections,” Dunleavy added, noting that the reporting “substantially supports” earlier findings by the state’s elections board into the matter. Peltola’s campaign didn’t immediately respond to a message early Thursday seeking comment on Dunleavy’s order. But during a campaign stop in Soldotna, Peltola, a former congresswoman, suggested the Times’ report was a “distraction” from issues such as Sen. Sullivan’s support of the war in Iran. “This is just another distraction. It’s another diversion,” she said in a video posted online. “This is all stuff to sell newspapers. This is all stuff to get subscriptions.” On Thursday, Vice President JD Vance didn’t go as far as the president did in calling for Peltola to be prosecuted but said he supported a probe into possible election fraud. “I mean, she is a person who, for months now, has pretended that she has had nothing to do with the fact that they have put a fraudulent person, a person explicitly designed to confuse Alaska voters, on the ballot,” Vance told reporters ahead of a campaign trip to Texas. “She is trying to steal a Senate seat from the voters of Alaska, and it’s disgraceful.” Earlier this year, a top state elections official ruled Daniel J. Sullivan Jr., a retired schoolteacher in the small fishing community of Petersburg, ineligible for the primary ballot, concluding that his declaration of candidacy was filed “with a purpose to confuse or mislead and to thereby compromise the ballot’s fairness or neutrality.” The case went to the Alaska Supreme Court, which ruled he was qualified to run. He has long denied he was running at Democrats’ behest and has said he had no contact with Peltola’s campaign: “Zero, none, zilch,” he said in June after announcing his run. Alaskan voters will see “Dan S. Sullivan” identified as a “registered Republican” and “incumbent” on their ballots, while “Daniel J. Sullivan Jr.” will not have a party affiliation. The New York Times reported that interviews, text messages and documents prepared for a potential lawsuit showed Peltola’s team aimed to coax candidates into the race, with the aim of splitting support for the GOP senator. Asked about the reporting on Wednesday, Trump told journalists, “I think they should be prosecuted.” He earlier had posted on social media that “Peltola should suffer the Legal and Political consequences for what she has done.” While controversial, the situation in Alaska is not that unusual. Political parties often support extra candidates on ballots to try to pull votes from their rivals. If state courts allow it, legal experts say, the federal government has no role challenging the results. In Ohio’s Senate race, Republicans have been tied to an effort to boost a socialist on the ballot who would pull votes from the Democratic candidate challenging Sen. Jon Husted.
