‘No one likes changing the clock’: Senate to hold on House-passed bill to make daylight saving time permanent after previous tries have been repealed
2026-10-29

‘No one likes changing the clock’: Senate to hold on House-passed bill to make daylight saving time permanent after previous tries have been repealed

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
The Senate is unlikely to act before the end of the year on a House-passed bill that would make daylight saving time permanent, defying President Donald Trump and dashing the hopes of Americans who don’t like having to change their clocks twice a year. As daylight saving time ends early Sunday and much of the country turns the clocks back an hour, several senators are holding up legislation that would eliminate the longtime U.S. practice. Supporters argue that ending the time change would provide more daylight during the afternoon, when Americans are most active, but opponents say commuters in some states wouldn’t see the sun until after 9 a.m. “No one likes changing the clock, me included, but Congress has enacted permanent Daylight Savings Time multiple times before and repealed it each time once Americans experienced absurdly late sunrises in the winter,” said Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark. Cotton, a member of GOP leadership, is the most vocal opponent of the change, notably drawing Trump’s ire earlier this month when the Republican president posted his personal cellphone number on social media and ordered him to “GET IT DONE!” But Cotton is not the only senator blocking the legislation, which would make daylight saving time the permanent national standard. Several other Republicans, particularly those from some Northern states that would see much later sunrises, say they also oppose it. “You’d be sending kids to school in the dark,” said South Dakota Sen. Mike Rounds. Trump has aggressively pushed the change, putting pressure on Senate Republicans to pass the House bill and send it to his desk. In the posts targeting Cotton earlier this month, Trump wrote that sunshine is better in the afternoon than the morning and “CRIMINALS DON’T LIKE WORKING IN THE LIGHT.” He renewed his call in a social media post this week, writing on Truth Social that “United States Senate is letting the Daylight Saving Bill die, when a vast Majority of the Country want it.” An avid golfer, Trump has made clear that adding daylight hours would mean more time to hit the links. He has argued that changing the time can be dangerous, especially when “you have high and dangerous steeples to reach, like on churches and municipal buildings.” Even as many Americans now rely on digital clocks that change on their own, Trump said that “nobody wants to spend their precious time running all over the place, turning Clocks back twice a year.” The president’s arguments don’t appear to have changed many votes in the Senate, however. Cotton has doubled down in response to Trump’s pressure, and several Republicans predicted in recent weeks that the bill won’t come up for a vote in the short time left when the Senate returns to Washington after the Nov. 3 midterm election. Sen. Todd Young, R-Ind., said that it’s his understanding that the House bill is a “non-starter” for Senate Republican leaders. Republican Sen. Bernie Moreno of Ohio was even more blunt when asked this month if he thinks the bill will pass the Senate anytime soon. Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., hasn’t weighed in publicly on the House bill, but he has voted against similar versions in the past. In the winter of 1973, amid an energy crisis, Congress passed a law instituting permanent daylight saving time for what was supposed to be a trial period. But it was repealed by that October as public support had sharply dropped, partly because of concern for children who were walking to school in the dark. Now more than 50 years later, fewer Americans recall that experiment. Like Trump, many want to ditch the twice-yearly time change and add more daylight hours later in the day. An AP-NORC poll last year found that only 12% of American adults were in favor of the current system, while almost half opposed it. Proponents of a single time include the American Medical Association and American Academy of Sleep Medicine. Florida Sen. Rick Scott signed legislation when he was governor in 2018 that would allow the state to remain on daylight saving time year round, pending federal approval. Now in the Senate, the Republican has led efforts to make that happen. “I hear from Americans constantly that they are sick and tired of changing their clocks twice a year — it’s an unnecessary, decades-old practice that’s more of an annoyance to families than benefit to them,” Scott said. “The head-spinning ritual of falling back and springing forward has a very real impact on our economy, our health, and our happiness,” said Democratic Sen. Ed Markey of Massachusetts, when Scott introduced his own bill last year. “Let’s say goodbye to 4pm winter sunsets.” Associated Press writer Darlene Superville contributed to this report. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. The neocloud shakeout is coming, and even the neoclouds seem to know it
2026-10-29

The neocloud shakeout is coming, and even the neoclouds seem to know it

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Welcome to Eye on AI. Beatrice Nolan here. In today’s issue: Is the end of the neocloud nigh? Elon Musk certainly seems to think so. Neoclouds are a new and increasingly popular type of business that has exploded alongside the AI boom. The business model is simple: buy up in-demand AI chips, build or lease the data centers to house them, and rent out the computing power to AI companies. As the demand for data centers has grown, so has the number of neoclouds promising to provide them. But on Sunday, Musk had a warning for the upstarts. In an X post, he predicted that more than 200 of them could face “guppy-level death and consolidation.” There are a few reasons for this. First, as Musk points out, lenders are reluctant to finance businesses that depend wholly on AI demand. Second, investors are already questioning the debt-fueled expansion of these companies. And with the Fed hinting at a December rate hike, that debt is only getting more expensive. Neoclouds are also just risky businesses. Rather than build all the capacity themselves, Big Tech and the AI companies sign contracts with neoclouds, which take on debt to build data centers and acquire the computer chips needed to run AI workloads. If demand holds, everyone wins. If it falters, the hyperscalers can, in theory, shift their capacity to other cloud work while the neoclouds are left with expensive data centers, rapidly aging chips, and loans to repay. There’s also concerns that Nvidia is propping up the industry in ways that might not be sustainable. The chipmaker has invested more than $7 billion directly in AI cloud providers and its latest quarterly filing shows up to $164.5 billion in guarantees and commitments tied to the data centers running its chips. It’s an effective way to keep GPU sales flowing, but it also means that if the neoclouds stumble, Nvidia may also suffer. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren dipped on Monday morning following Musk’s comments, though all three recovered by the close. Still, it was another knock for a sector that has faced some recent challenges. Musk also isn’t the only one saying the neocloud sector may be headed for trouble. I spent last week in Berlin at Nvidia’s GTC event where I met with several neocloud companies and, surprisingly, most agreed some kind of consolidation in the market is looming. “I don’t think it will be like a light consolidation. It will be a massive wave,” Raimund Riedl, Volta’s chief financial officer, told me. “We’re going to start to see it in the relatively near future. There will start to be M&A activity, and we’ll consolidate.” “We’re not worried about being one of those dead bodies, so to speak, because of the nature of this model,” he added. Broadly, there are two types of neoclouds. The first is the established players, like CoreWeave, Nebius, and Iren, which have data centers and big customer contracts, often funded with big loans. Some, like Iren and CoreWeave, are former bitcoin miners that already at least some power and sites. The second is the newer startups, which often have little more than a business plan. Volta is one of the new crowd. It came out of stealth in August and says it wants to lean less on venture capital and more on the huge pool of institutional money that funds roads, power grids and cell towers, though it did raise $300 million from investors including Andreessen Horowitz and Nvidia. It already has a $10 billion contract, reportedly with Anthropic, that it has used to help secure a $5 billion debt deal, partly underwritten by JPMorgan and Goldman Sachs. “It’s just like every other new technology sort of hyped area. Everybody piles in,” Paul Miller, a principal analyst at Forrester, said of the neocloud market. “They will not all survive. There’s a lot of dead wood that needs to be cut out.” Miller added he expected to see the start of some consolidation over the next year, but then much more after that. Gartner predicts that 80% of independent neoclouds will “cease independent operations” by 2030, while Bernstein analysts estimate that only 35% of the data center capacity that has been announced in North America will ever get built, according to a Bloomberg report. As access to the grid remains a bottleneck in many countries and public backlash against data center shows no signs of easing, getting a data center built and connected to power is no mean feat. To make matters worse, it also might be getting harder to see the demand. In Berlin, one European VC told me that most hyperscalers and larger AI companies are “hedging their bets” and overcommitting to data center deals—fearing many won’t make it off the ground. Is all this bad for the entire neocloud industry? Well, it depends on who you are. A shakeout would take some of the froth out of the sector, Miller said, but it doesn’t mean the AI buildout is over. Demand for computing power is still running hot. The problem is that too many companies have promised capacity they may never deliver. When those players fold, their customers won’t disappear, they’ll just go looking for someone who can actually deliver. That’s good news for the neoclouds with real data centers, real chips, and real customers, Miller says: “Now those companies get to tell customers: ‘I’ve got a data center. I’ve got chips. I’ve got capacity. I know how to run them. I might charge you a little bit more, but I can take you today.'” Beatrice Nolanbeatrice.nolan@fortune.com@beafreyanolan But first: Cyber threats don’t stand still—and neither do the startups working to stop them. From AI-powered deception to hidden vulnerabilities, businesses face an increasingly complex security landscape. Anthropic’s S-1 reveals two areas where OpenAI could have the upper hand, experts say — By Emily Forlini Waters’ new AI tool targets one of the pharmaceutical industry’s most tedious tasks — By John Kell Financial AI startup Casap raises $56 million to help banks stop an explosion of customer fraud — By Jeff John Roberts Parents sue OpenAI, alleging ChatGPT coached their daughter off her epilepsy medication. The parents of Bowen Wan, a 31-year-old Amazon software engineer with epilepsy, have sued OpenAI, alleging that ChatGPT’s GPT-4o model helped her taper off her seizure medication and hide it from her doctors. Wan died in February, the same week OpenAI retired GPT-4o from ChatGPT. According to the complaint, the chatbot drafted schedules to bring her lamotrigine dose down to zero and described her doctors’ advice as risk aversion. It also praised her for declining a blood test and played down symptoms including confusion and head pain as part of her recovery. The suit argues that OpenAI should strictly limit the ability of its AI models to provide medication advice, the way it already treats reproducing copyrighted text. OpenAI called the case “incredibly heartbreaking” and said newer models are better at recognizing when a situation needs urgent attention. Read more in the New York Times. Microsoft offers buyouts to a quarter of its workforce as it reshapes for AI. Microsoft is offering voluntary buyouts to about 60,000 employees, roughly a quarter of its workforce of around 223,000, as it cuts costs and reorganizes around AI. Chief people officer Amy Coleman announced the “Voluntary Exit Offer” in a memo. It covers staff at vice president level and below on select teams in the U.S., Canada, India, and the U.K. Xbox, Windows, Copilot, and Finance are reportedly among the participating divisions, and employees reportedly have until early December to decide. The program lets Microsoft shrink headcount without mass layoffs while it keeps spending heavily on data centers and custom chips. Read more here in Reuters and from Fortune's Sebastian Herrera here. OpenAI opens an election “war room” ahead of the midterms. OpenAI is launching an Election Operations Center to watch for misinformation and other attempts to disrupt next week’s U.S. midterms, especially misuse of its own tools such as ChatGPT. The center started operating this week and will run until all votes are counted. It is led by Josh Lawson, OpenAI’s head of civic and strategic risk. Staff will review flagged cases of potential abuse, reports of unexpected model behavior, and OpenAI’s election integrations with Democracy Works and the Associated Press. Earlier this month, OpenAI disrupted two Russian- and Iranian-run influence operations that used its models. Meta and other tech companies have run similar election centers before, but this is a first for a leading AI lab. Read more from Politico here. Elizabeth Warren presses Anthropic on existential risks ahead of its IPO. Massachusetts Senator Elizabeth Warren has sent Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei four pages of questions. She is demanding “exhaustive responses” by Nov. 3, before the company’s planned Nasdaq listing. The offering is expected to be the largest IPO in history, with a reported valuation somewhere between $1.5 trillion to $2 trillion. The Democratic senator points to risks Anthropic disclosed in its S-1 filing and to Amodei’s own public warnings about loss of control, misuse for cyberattacks and bioterrorism, and a commercial “race to the bottom.” She also argues that recent incidents in which frontier models, including Anthropic’s, broke through security controls make existing safety tests hard to trust. On the financial side, she warns that millions of Americans could quickly become shareholders through index funds and pensions. She calls the company’s projections “astronomical” and says first-day trading could roil tech stocks. Read more from the Wall Street Journal here. That’s how often professional analysts went along with whatever their AI agent was nudging them toward, according to a new Anthropic study. Researchers asked 50 experienced data analysts to use Claude Code to decide whether a fictional farming cooperative should expand a paid advisory program. The task hinged on four judgment calls with no single right answer, such as how to measure productivity and which farms to include. Without telling the analysts, the researchers secretly assigned the agent a position on each decision and told it to make the strongest honest case for it. Analysts sided with the agent’s randomly assigned preference 86% of the time, versus the roughly 50% you’d expect by chance. Of the 38 who stated a preference before the agent weighed in, 60% switched when it disagreed with them, compared with 6% when it agreed. Most analysts didn’t seem to notice. Some 77% repeated the agent’s arguments in their own explanations, sometimes verbatim, but only 15% credited it. The researchers say the study isn’t a case against AI. In fact, six of the 11 analysts who declined to use the agent made serious methodological errors, versus three of the 50 who used it. But they warn the findings undercut the idea that a “human in the loop” is an independent check, and that if many teams defer to the same model, apparent expert consensus could simply reflect one model’s biases. Women astronauts outnumber men in NASA’s new class—they’re now halfway through training and set to earn around $150K with no overtime or hazard pay
2026-10-29

Women astronauts outnumber men in NASA’s new class—they’re now halfway through training and set to earn around $150K with no overtime or hazard pay

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Women astronauts outnumber men in NASA’s new class—they’re now halfway through training, and set to earn around $150K with no overtime or hazard pay If billionaires like Elon Musk, Jeff Bezos and Sam Altman are right, your next job could be on the Moon. And the people lined up to get there first are already in training. NASA’s newest astronaut class, announced last year, is now just past the halfway point of its two-year training—and it’s doing the rounds on social media again for making history. For the first time, women outnumber men: six of the 10 candidates, chosen from more than 8,000 applicants. They’re currently learning “basic astronaut skills,” according to NASA’s website, like spacewalking, operating the space station, flying jet planes and controlling a robotic arm. Once qualified, they could be assigned to future missions in low Earth orbit and to the Moon, with some potentially playing a role in NASA’s longer-term plans for Mars. But there’s a catch: they’ll earn on par with a mid-career desk job or a skilled trade like electricians—and they won’t get paid overtime or hazard pay. The out-of-this-world job comes with a very down-to-earth paycheck. Candidates earn a government salary during training, and if they qualify, NASA’s website states the pay is around $152,000, as of 2024. NASA didn’t respond to Fortune’s request for comment on their updated pay. In comparison, psychotherapists, project managers and air traffic controllers can earn similar wages—without the added risk of leaving planet Earth. “When NASA astronauts are aboard the International Space Station, they receive regular 40-hour workweek salaries,” NASA previously told Fortune. “While in space, NASA astronauts are on official travel orders as federal employees, so their transportation, lodging, and meals are provided.” Not that they can clock off—the office is orbiting Earth. Astronauts Suni Williams and Butch Wilmore found that out the hard way. Their eight-day trip stretched to 286 days after problems with Boeing’s Starliner spacecraft, and they received just $5 a day in incidentals, about $1,430 each in extra pay. First, the odds. With an acceptance rate of about 0.125%, it’s harder to get into NASA’s astronaut class than Harvard, which accepts around 4%. The qualifications themselves are relatively straightforward. Applicants must: Military experience isn’t required, and neither is flying experience. There’s no age limit, with the average candidate being 34. But applicants must be free of any medical conditions that could impair space flight—and NASA physicians have the final say, with no waivers for medical requirements. And if you want a taste of what it takes to become an astronaut, look no further than Sara Sabry. She doesn’t work for NASA, but she became the first Egyptian, and the first Arab and African woman, to fly to space on Jeff Bezos’s Blue Origin in 2022, beating more than 7,000 other applicants. To get there, she woke at 4:30 a.m. to squeeze in training and research before her full-time job as CTO of a Berlin-based startup. “Back then it was, it was really, really, it was really tough,” she told Fortune. “You would wake up at night, and then you would go back at night, so you barely see the daylight ever.” “I see a lot of young people now; they’re wanting to take the easy route without working so hard. But the truth is, you have to make sacrifices. You have to put yourself through a lot of discomfort,” Sabry added. “Of course, it’s not easy to wake up at 4:30 a.m. every morning and be completely isolated from the world, right? But it goes to show that you can really transform your life—and you have so much control over your life.” Orianna Rosa Royle is the Success associate editor at Fortune, overseeing careers, leadership, and company culture coverage. She was previously the senior reporter at Management Today, Britain's longest-running publication for CEOs. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Finnish police suspect environmental failings at Google data centre site

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Google's Hamina data centre. Image: Google The project was ordered to be halted earlier this month over pending environmental assessments. Finnish police have launched a criminal investigation into suspected breaches of environmental law at the site of one of Google’s planned data centre projects in the country. In a statement today (29 October), authorities said they received five separate requests to probe the company’s recently announced Muhos data centre project in northern Finland. All of these requests are being handled under the same criminal investigation. Google’s data centre projects in the country are led by Tuike Finland Oy, a subsidiary business providing professional engineering services. According to the police, preliminary information found “ambiguities and shortcomings” in ditching and water management at the company’s data centre site. In a statement to Reuters, detective superintendent Kari Pakanen said: “We are investigating whether the company’s operations have breached the water act and resulted in environmental ​damage.” A guilty finding could lead to a corporate fine, he further told the publication. The criminal probe comes after the Finnish licensing authority ordered Tuike Finland Oy to urgently suspend its data centre projects in Muhos and Kajaani-Otanmäki earlier this month over pending environmental assessments. Based on available information, authorities said they couldn’t verify whether the project’s environment impact could be appropriately managed. Tuike Finland Oy suspended its preparatory work in both the projects on 14 October. Finnish police said today that the ensuing preliminary investigation will only be completed next year due to “the scope of the matter”. The Muhos data centre is part of a major €13bn commitment Google made in Finland last month. The massive spend also includes a number of other planned data centres, together estimated to create around 16,000 jobs in construction alone. The technology giant also announced a number of environmental initiatives last month, including promising to fund clean energy and nature restoration projects. Although, last week, it was reported that Google “illegally stripped” 330 hectares of forest near Muhos for its data centre project in the town. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. How quantum is moving out of the lab and into the boardroom
2026-10-29

How quantum is moving out of the lab and into the boardroom

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
PwC’s Leonard McAuliffe explores the changing quantum landscape and how organisations need to evolve in tandem. By its very nature, the quantum industry is transforming almost too quickly to keep pace with. As the movers and shakers continue to drive innovation and the skills and expectations evolve, organisations are finding themselves in an increasingly complex position. “The biggest shift we’ve seen this year is that quantum has moved out of the research lab and into the boardroom,” explained Leonard McAuliffe, a partner for cybersecurity and privacy at PwC Ireland. He told SiliconRepublic.com: “The hardware is improving faster than most people expected, particularly on error correction and qubit quality and quantum-inspired tools are already helping in areas like logistics and finance. “From a security perspective, the most important development is around standards. NIST has finalised its quantum-safe algorithms and the US, UK and EU are now setting timelines for moving to post-quantum cryptography. Quantum is no longer a watching brief for any organisation.” But how do you develop a workforce that is equipped to deal with the demands of an industry that is rapidly evolving, despite being in relative infancy? McAuliffe finds that in the broad scheme of things you don’t necessarily need the entire workforce to understand the physics of quantum. Rather, you need to ensure that there is the right level of understanding across the right teams and departments. He said: “Boards and senior leaders need to grasp the risk, in particular ‘harvest now, decrypt later’, where attackers steal encrypted data today and hold onto it until they can decrypt it. “For security and technology teams, it’s more practical. They need to know where cryptography is used across the organisation and how to plan the move to quantum-safe alternatives. The majority of organisations don’t have a clear picture of where their cryptography sits, so that is usually the first gap to close.” McAuliffe finds that, when it comes to advancing and innovating in the quantum era, Ireland’s economy is “both uniquely exposed and uniquely positioned”. He said: “Pharma, a core pillar of the Irish economy, could shorten drug discovery cycles, financial services could see breakthroughs in risk modelling, optimisation and fraud detection, while manufacturing and supply chain-intensive sectors stand to benefit from quantum-enabled optimisation. “However, the most significant impact is on security and that cuts across every industry. Quantum computers will be capable of breaking the public-key cryptography that underpins secure online banking, medical records, digital signatures, remote access and supply chain integrity.” PwC previously published the ‘2027 Global Digital Trust Insights Survey’ that found only 17pc of Irish business and information security leaders agree that cyber risk is integrated into their organisation’s enterprise risk management processes, compared with 41pc globally. In line with McAuliffe’s insights regarding organisational readiness, the report found that when it comes to quantum security, only 6pc of Irish respondents are implementing quantum-resistant measures, compared to 21pc globally. There is pressure, he finds, to ensure that the more heavily regulated sectors are prepared, as the failure to do so could lead to major issues down the line. “For heavily regulated sectors such as finance, healthcare, energy and telecoms, a quantum-readiness gap may soon constitute a compliance breach, not just a technical issue.” He concluded: “It’s a business continuity, legal, regulatory and reputational issue for every board. Early adopters will gain an advantage, while those who wait risk being exposed.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. DoorDash buys grocery delivery platform Instacart in $17bn deal
2026-10-29

DoorDash buys grocery delivery platform Instacart in $17bn deal

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
DoorDash shares jumped more than 8.5pc in pre-market trading today. DoorDash is acquiring Instacart at a $17bn equity value in a bid to scale its grocery delivery business as shoppers increasingly go online. The US food delivery giant is paying $65 per Instacart share, representing a 26pc premium on the grocery delivery platform’s closing price on 22 October. DoorDash also announced its third-quarter results today (29 October), reporting a 34pc year-on-year revenue growth to $4.6bn and total order growth of 28pc to 993m. DoorDash shares jumped more than 8.5pc in pre-market trading today. The acquisition targets the growing proportion of shoppers who are choosing to buy groceries online. Since exploding into popularity during the Covid-19 pandemic, online groceries now capture nearly 21pc of all grocery spending in the US, according to some reports. Groceries are still an emerging, logistically challenging category in the online delivery business. In its financial statement, DoorDash said that tapping Instacart will help scale its offerings, lower costs and improve its service, and that as things are, “consumers are still asked to pay a premium for imperfect delivery”. “Grocery is one of the hardest things to get right online. Someone has to pick a ripe avocado, find a substitute you’d choose yourself, and get the ice cream to you before it melts,” said Tony Xu, the co-founder and CEO of DoorDash. “Instacart has spent years learning to do that well for grocers, while DoorDash has built a delivery network and technology that local stores rely on every day. Together, we could give stores of every size more of those tools and unlock the potential of grocery delivery.” At the end of 2025, according to DoorDash, it had more than 56m monthly active users. Founded in 2012, Instacart’s online grocery delivery platform is used by millions globally. Its CEO Chris Rogers said that this past quarter was the company’s best performance as a public company, with a reported revenue of just more than $1bn, up 17pc year-over-year. The acquisition deal is expected to close between late 2027 and early 2028, until which both companies will continue to operate separately. DoorDash said it will keep Instacart’s brand, enterprise technology products and app after the transaction is closed. Last year, DoorDash entered Ireland after acquiring its UK rival Deliveroo for £2.9bn. It entered Europe in 2021 after buying Finnish food and grocery delivery start-up Wolt, its first acquisition in the region. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. File storage platform Dropbox is laying off around 200 people, or 9pc of its global workforce, across its engineering, product, design and administrative functions. SiliconRepublic.com has contacted Dropbox to ask if Ireland-based employees will be impacted. In a memo to staff yesterday (28 October), co-CEO Ashraf Alkarmi said the company was “making difficult choices about where we invest and how we organise Dropbox to achieve the product and growth priorities that will matter most for our long-term strategy”. “Our business is profitable and we’ve built real momentum this year. Our two-year strategy sets a clear direction for growth,” he said. “Delivering on that strategy requires concentrating our resources on the priorities that will have the greatest potential for customer impact and growth.” He added: “In order to be successful, we can’t take the same work and spread it across fewer people. We’ll streamline teams and concentrate support and resources against product areas that have the highest impact on our customers and Dropbox as a business.” In a filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission yesterday, Dropbox noted the layoffs would cost it “approximately $27m to $33m”. In his staff memo, Alkarmi said that impacted staff members would receive 16 weeks’ pay plus one additional week for each completed year at Dropbox, while country-specific practices and legal requirements would also be followed. Other aspects of compensation are also included in Dropbox’s offering to affected staff, including immigration support for impacted visa holders. The SEC filing noted that that the company “expects its third quarter 2026 financial results to be in line with or above its guidance ranges for revenue, constant currency revenue and non-GAAP operating margin”. Alkarmi’s memo also noted that the company’s CTO Ali Dasdan would be leaving in November. On this, the SEC filing said: “Mr Dasdan will receive a pro rata bonus equal to $515,000 as well as a lump sum cash payment equal to approximately $1.3m, as well as certain additional benefits consistent with those received by other departing employees.” Two years ago, Dropbox laid off more than 500 employees, while in April 2023, it cut a similar number of roles. Rise of AI not deterring parents from backing their child’s STEM interests

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
The research found that a range of factors can impact why a parent may support a child’s STEM ambitions, including their own career and level of AI use. A report published today (29 October) by STEM South West has found that, despite the concerns around artificial intelligence, eight out of 10 (81pc) parents are still encouraging their school-aged children to pursue careers in science, technology, engineering and maths (STEM). STEM South West is an industry-led initiative that brings together Irish tech, pharmaceutical, science and engineering companies, alongside SMEs and stakeholders, to identify and address problems facing the STEM sector in the south-west. The report gathered information from 1,000 adults nationwide and discovered that, of those who contributed to the research, almost one-quarter are likely to encourage a child to engage with STEM even more than they would have prior to the rise of AI. Comparatively, only 8pc said they are less likely to and only 12pc said they wouldn’t encourage a child into STEM at all. According to the report, people’s own use of AI had some bearing on how likely they would be to support their child’s foray into the AI space. Life-changing career opportunities for you Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Among frequent or daily AI users, 36pc said they would encourage a child towards STEM more than before, compared to 18pc of non-AI users. In contrast, 25pc of non-AI users said they wouldn’t encourage a child towards STEM, compared to 7pc of frequent or daily users. ICT professionals were also found to be particularly supportive, as 41pc said they would encourage a child towards STEM more than before, while 94pc said they would encourage a child at least as much as they would have before recent advances in AI. Commenting on the research, Aine McCarthy, the chair of STEM South West said: “It’s really encouraging that people continue to believe strongly in the opportunities a career in STEM can offer young people. There has understandably been a lot of discussion about what AI could mean for future jobs, but these findings show that confidence in STEM remains strong. “What is particularly interesting is the difference we see among people who use AI regularly, where greater familiarity with the technology is associated with a more positive outlook on STEM careers. That reinforces the importance of giving young people opportunities not simply to use AI, but to understand it and develop the skills to work alongside it.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Ireland’s Vectigo unveils new road safety platform in the US
2026-10-29

Ireland’s Vectigo unveils new road safety platform in the US

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Image: © David S. Swierczek/Stock.adobe.com Vectigo’s platform is ‘designed to reduce crashes and save lives for everyday drivers first’, company chair Brendan Lundy said. Irish start-up Vectigo’s US division is launching a new safety platform for autonomous vehicles in Colorado that can be embedded directly onto roadways. Vectigo, headquartered out of Dublin, was founded in 2024 by Sinéad O’Sullivan and Brendan Lundy. The business is heading towards a $2.2m raise this year after securing $1.9m in total funds since its founding. Vectigo’s technology offers solar-powered and grid-independent on-road sensors designed to improve road safety and support human and autonomous driving. The data infrastructure company uses AI to conduct predictive analysis to help manage traffic, and detect any potential anomalies on the road. Its technology has been shown to reduce human-driver accidents by 70pc, the company said. “This platform is designed to reduce crashes and save lives for everyday drivers first,” said Lundy, the company’s executive chair. “Every winter, Colorado drivers face black ice they cannot see, wrong-way drivers they cannot anticipate and closures they cannot avoid. “Vectigo detects those dangers in the lane and gets that information to the drivers, first responders and truckers who need it, in time to change course. The same data gives autonomous vehicles the redundancy they need to operate safely.” The company’s Colorado-based US division delivered its first in-ground pilot with RoadSafe Traffic Systems earlier this year. According to the company, the sensors light up in heavy snow, send warnings before black ice and alert drivers about a wrong-way driver. The platform works with every vehicle on the road today, with no special equipment, app or new car required, Vectigo said. The technology is particularly useful for autonomous vehicles, which use advanced sensors to detect obstacles on the road, though they cannot directly sense surface conditions such as ice or standing water and can be degraded by snow, glare and faded lane markings. RFK Jr Opens the Floodgates on Experimental Peptides, Alarming Scientists
2026-10-29

RFK Jr Opens the Floodgates on Experimental Peptides, Alarming Scientists

Futurism | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
It’s open season for untested peptide injections. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. declared on Wednesday that patients will now be allowed broad access to untested and unproven peptide drugs popularized by health influencers. The move has already drawn immediate and intense concern from scientists and medical professionals, who are wary that the decision will result in confusion and harm to consumers. In a sweeping and seemingly unilateral decision by Kennedy, the HHS has directed the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to not take action against pharmacies that sell six peptides including BPC-157, KPV, and TB-500. According to sources who spoke to the Associated Press, the decision was made without input from scientists at the FDA, who generally oppose the change. “Based on the available evidence, including the recommendations of a recent Pharmacy Compounding Advisory Committee, the Secretary has determined that the policy advances patient choice and freedom while appropriately protecting patient safety,” reads the decision. Kennedy, a fervent supporter of the false conspiracy theory that vaccines cause autism and who has been known to recover and eat roadkill, has praised the compounded meds. Online influencers have touted the compounded drugs — with little to no scientific evidence — as metabolism, energy, and muscle-recovery boosters, among other claims. The peptide boom was kicked off by the explosive success of GLP-1 drugs, the FDA-approved weight loss medication. However, their soaring popularity has given way to a whole new realm of experimental substances that don’t have the rigor of regulated safety testing and clinical trials behind them. The trend has created a confusing and dangerous marketplace in which peptide takers are buying the drugs via sketchy online dealers, in bodegas, and at gas stations — and unsurprisingly, the substances in the vials are often not at all what’s being marketed and some takers have claimed strange and dangerous side effects. In its surprising announcement, the HHS notes that “compounded drugs are not FDA-approved, which means FDA does not review their safety, effectiveness, or quality before they are marketed.” The agency even notes that the drugs continue to be “experimental,” and says that the action is designed to “expand and improve patient access while FDA reviews these substances.” Put simply, the decision doesn’t mean these drugs are FDA-approved, and you’re still taking peptides at your own risk. “Although compounded drugs can serve an important medical need for certain patients, they also may pose risks to patients,” reads the decision. “However, based on the Pharmacy Compounding Advisory Committee (PCAC) meeting and data regarding utilization of peptides, HHS believes that there is significant interest in the use of these peptides in compounding.” FDA approvals aren’t just bureaucratic red tape. The regulator’s tight guidelines are designed to ensure that drugs don’t cause severe outcomes like birth defects or cancer, as well as confirm that the drugs are actually effective. In a statement, HHS told the New York Times that the new policy requires “patient-specific prescriptions, traceable ingredients, quality testing and safety reporting to hold compounders accountable and give patients an alternative to unregulated sellers.” She added that the goal of the policy is in fact to confront “the risks of black-market peptides” and that it “does not grant FDA approval or establish clinical safety and effectiveness.” Former FDA official Peter Lurie, who now serves as the president of the Center for Science in the Public Interest, told the AP that the decision may result in people being “diverted from” FDA-approved medicines “toward this stuff.” As a result, he added, “there will be more unnecessary pain and suffering.” “It does undermine the idea,” Janet Woodcock, a physician and former head of the FDA, told the NYT, “that you would test drugs for safety and effectiveness before they were widely made available.” The pro-unapproved peptide crowd, meanwhile, is predictably thrilled. “This is a big win for patients, providers and medical freedom!” Lee Rosebush, who chairs the American Academy of Peptide Medicine, said in a LinkedIn post. Fuel prices have exploded throughout the US since Donald Trump began his war on Iran. Though the rise in fuel costs throughout the world can be traced back to retaliatory fuel embargoes imposed by the Iranian military in the Strait of Hormuz and Houthi militants in the Bab el-Mandeb Strait, Trump and his cronies have another explanation for the American people: environmental regulations. During a political rally in Lubbock, Texas on Tuesday, Trump announced yet another massive rollback of environmental regulations, originally imposed by the Biden administration to clamp down on polluting methane corporations. “Tonight, I’m officially terminating Biden’s expensive and unnecessary methane regulations to save consumers and energy producers,” Trump told throngs of reporters, according to the Guardian. In doing so, the president says he’s ending the “crippling regulations on methane that caused prices to skyrocket” across the nation. Environmental Protection Agency administrator Lee Zeldin swiftly put out an effusive statement praising his bosses’ decision to “end the war on oil and natural gas,” referring to the Biden administration’s oil and natural gas regulations passed under the Clean Air Act. The real kicker is how little Trump’s rollbacks will actually do for American consumers, while simultaneously enabling the worst excesses of the oil and gas corporations. In his press release, Zeldin noted the move is expected to “unleash an estimated 210 million barrels of oil,” which the Guardian notes is equivalent to just ten days of US petroleum consumption. In other words, any financial relief from the deregulatory move will be short lived for American consumers. The revision is also opening doors for some of the worst air polluters operating in the US, as the publication Grist observed. According to the outlet, the kind of methane emissions represented by Trump’s rollbacks make up about 12 percent of the greenhouse gases generated by the US, which is around 80 times more potent than carbon dioxide when it comes to its impact on global warming over a 20-year span. “Cutting methane emissions from the oil and gas industry is the fastest way to fight climate change,” project manager at the Environmental Defense Fund Tsion Amare told Grist. “This rule could severely set back the advances we’ve made over the years.” More on Trump: Trump Says He Called the CEO of Google to Rename the Atlantic Ocean

NASA Slams the Accelerator to Get to the Moon Before Trump’s Term Is Over

Futurism | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
NASA is gunning the engine so it can reach the Moon — and soon build a base there — at incredible speed. On Friday, the space agency’s administrator Jared Isaacman doubled down on its ambitious timeline to send astronauts to the Moon in just two years, which would be before the end of president Donald Trump’s term. In a lengthy speech at the Kennedy Space Center last week, Isaacman reaffirmed that the space agency is on track to launch Artemis 3 next summer, a mission that won’t send astronauts to the Moon but will have them practice docking their spacecraft with lunar landers made either by Elon Musk’s SpaceX or Jeff Bezos’ Blue Origin in Earth’s orbit. (The space agency still hasn’t finalized its decision on which vehicle it will be using for the landing, a major point of uncertainty.) Artemis IV, still slated for 2028 at the time of writing, is when the astronauts will fly to the Moon and land on it for the first time in over half a century, where they’ll spend a week on the surface conducting scientific missions. That is, if everything goes according to plan. Isaacman stressed, per the Associated Press, that NASA is now accelerating its pace by cutting down on meetings, “bureaucratic inertia, and endless deliberation.” It’s a striking comment to make, raising questions of if pursuing speed could be coming at the expense of thoroughness and safety. Isaacman, though, drew on NASA’s glorious past, saying that its strength won’t come from “tech industry pixie dust or some new management philosophy.” “It’s about our own history,” he continued. “It’s a playbook this agency knows really well, the same playbook that went from Apollo 7 splashdown to Apollo 11 in just nine months.” There’s no denying NASA’s legendary lunar history. From 1968 to 1974, its Apollo program landed on the Moon six separate times, a pace that seems unthinkable today. But never has any space agency attempted to build a Moon base, the next stage of its lunar ambitions after Artemis IV. Equally unprecedented is the planned base’s location near the Moon’s south pole, when all of the Apollo missions landed near the Moon’s equator. There’s also doubts over whether SpaceX’s Starship, one of the spacecraft that NASA has selected to carry out the Moon landing and be used in the docking exercise, will be ready in time. The world’s largest rocket recently accomplished its first orbit around the planet, but that flight, which was originally intended to have Starship fly around the Earth six times, was cut short because of an engine failure. SpaceX has yet to prove the rocket is safe enough for a proper mission, on top of other capabilities such as refueling in space. “It is incredibly ambitious,” NASA astronaut Frank Rubio, who was selected for the Artemis III mission, recently told the AP. “One thing I’ve always believed and learned is don’t bet against America.” More on spaceflight: 11-Foot Chunk of Chinese Rocket Crashes Down in Minnesota Cornfield

2026-10-28

The Hinge on Apple’s Constantly Breaking New Foldable iPhone Was “Built With AI”

Futurism | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The success of Apple’s new folding iPhone Duo hinged on its, well, hinge. But sadly for the multi trillion dollar company, some of the larger, inner screens on its $2,000-plus devices are already cracking, and early postmortems point to their faulty hinge design. In light of that fiasco, now feels like a good time to bring up how the hinge was designed: with the help of AI. Here’s what Johny Srouji, the company’s chief hardware officer, said during an event last month. Per TechCrunch’s coverage: “During manufacturing, we use AI algorithms to precisely match each individual hinge with its best-fit housing to ensure perfect alignment,” Srouji explained, “and a confocal laser progressively scans the topology of every single unit and 3D prints up to 25 micro layers of a custom photopolymer to eliminate residual waviness.” To be clear, “AI algorithms” could mean a lot of things. A machine learning design tool is not the same thing as Claude or ChatGPT, and these algorithms have had a place in industrial applications for years. Apple itself has long boasted about using machine learning to help design and improve its software and devices. For instance, machine learning, or “AI” if you will, powers its iPad’s palm rejection technology that lets you rest your hand on the screen while using a stylus. Still, Srouji made the conscious decision to conflate machine learning algorithms — if that’s what’s going on here — with what people take “AI” to mean today: a catchall term for chatbots, autonomous agents, and other large language model powered systems that appear intelligent. Maybe he thought it’d sound more impressive to investors and the general public. Whether the hinge was vibe-designed or not, it’s clearly causing some notable issues, with a small but sizable minority of customers complaining that their screens are cracking along it just hours after unboxing. Shahram Mokhtari, the lead teardown technician at iFixit explained to The Verge that part of the “extremely sensitive” flexible inner screen is pushing up against the hinge. It appears that a center support bar that makes space for the hinge to fold without crease isn’t retracting far enough and creating stress on the screen. Apple, at least, acknowledged there was an “issue” Monday and said it’s replacing the damaged units. More on AI: Nike’s AI Designed World Cup Jerseys Are a Disaster

Visit Original Website
In the troubling view of machine learning researcher Nate Soares, AI is more likely than not to wipe out humanity within the next decade. At least, that’s what Soares said during a recent episode of the Atlantic’s podcast “The Most Interesting Thing in AI.” During the taping, the AI researcher and president of the Machine Intelligence Research Institute even said that he’s so confident in the coming AI apocalypse that he’s stopped contributing to his retirement account. “I do not save for retirement right now,” Soares explained during the interview. “I have a 401(k) from when I worked at Google, but I haven’t contributed for a decade.” The AI guru evidently pegs the possibility of AI-driven extinction at over 50 percent over the next ten years, a remarkably bold prediction of a future which Soares is evidently working hard to prevent. “I do everything I can to stop this eventuality,” he said. “I don’t think it’s inevitable.” Soares is well-known in the AI research space as a prolific doom-monger — a schtick which is starting to play on a loop. As critics have noted, though both Soares and Yudkowsky have spent much time fretting about an AI apocalypse, there’s no consensus that such a future is even possible — let alone probable. There are plenty of reasons to be pessimistic about AI. The technology has already been used to bomb elementary school students, scam seniors, and drive dozens of vulnerable users to suicide. AI doesn’t need to be superintelligent to do any of that — and if we all focus on the harms technology may cause down the road, it becomes easy to overlook the harms it’s causing in the here and now. As for saving your pennies for your retirement? Well, it’s like some AI doomers say — it’s probably better to play it safe and contribute to your savings, rather than take the chance at being sorry you didn’t. Ex-Palantir execs launch Dream with $17M from a16z to build AI world models for construction

Ex-Palantir execs launch Dream with $17M from a16z to build AI world models for construction

TechCrunch | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Andy Elder and Arjun Mathur were Stanford roommates who both studied computer science before spending a decade at Palantir. Elder led product design for a team of 800 people building software for U.S. defense and intelligence agencies, while Mathur served as a senior vice president in the company’s commercial practice. When the friends decided to build a startup early last year, there was no question they would do it together. Despite having no direct background in architecture, Elder and Mathur were troubled by a growing problem: fewer buildings are constructed in the U.S. today than a few decades ago, as development costs have surged. “We’ve gone from dial-up internet to super intelligent AI, but our ability to build the physical world around us has actually decreased,” Mathur told TechCrunch. Believing AI can boost productivity across the architecture, engineering, and construction (AEC) industry, they set out to build Dream, a startup developing world models that automate building design and engineering. Dream has emerged from stealth with $17 million in seed funding led by Andreessen Horowitz, alongside other backers including SV Angel, XYZ, and Zigg Capital. The startup aims to replace cumbersome, outdated AEC tools like Autodesk’s AutoCAD and Revit. If you sit next to an architect or a structural engineer, you’ll notice that they are still “drawing” every piece of their designs part-by-part on legacy software, Mathur said. “AI really plays no role in that equation.” While a standard LLM like ChatGPT can generate an image of a building, the result won’t be structurally sound. Dream, by contrast, is grounded in real physics and construction standards. Just as LLMs learn from text, Dream’s world model is trained on more than 100 million square feet of building data gathered from permit archives across the United States. “Our model actually understands what’s within those walls. What materials are used, what the beams are made of, and how much weight they can support,” Elder said. The startup calls its approach “structural cognition” because its model generates structurally precise designs that account for internal building elements like plumbing and HVAC. Dream has already begun selling its product to AEC professionals and developers, who use the model to conceptualize building designs and estimate overall costs, according to the founders. The platform is already deployed across 10 active projects in Texas and California. While Dream isn’t the only company attempting to update AEC software with AI – other startups working on this include Motif, Snaptrude and Arcol—Elder and Mathur claim that they are the only ones that developed a built world model that instantly designs whole buildings. Kimberly Tan, a partner at Andreessen Horowitz, said that while the firm first invested in Dream over a year ago, when the startup model was just an idea, she has been extremely impressed with their progress. “I have not seen anybody take the technical approach,” she said. “They’re doing everything based on their own models.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. SuperMe raises $20M for its network of AI expert profiles
2026-10-29

SuperMe raises $20M for its network of AI expert profiles

TechCrunch | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. When people have career or job questions, they usually tap into their network to find experts who’ve faced a similar predicament and can offer answers. Increasingly, that job is falling to AI. SuperMe is a network of AI profiles built from the knowledge of real-life experts. When you ask a question, the platform determines which experts are best suited to answer and creates a group chat with their AI profiles. To scale that network on both sides, meaning more users asking questions and more experts supplying knowledge, the startup announced today that it is raising $20 million in funding led by Kirsten Green at Forerunner, with participation from Greylock Partners. Casey Winters and Ludo Antonov founded the startup in 2024. Both have experience at large consumer companies. Winters held exec roles at Grubhub, Pinterest, and Eventbrite and has advised companies like Airbnb and Canva part-time. Antonov was head of engineering at multiple startups, including Pinterest, Lyft, and Whatnot. The two got connected when Winters was advising Whatnot. ChatGPT was becoming popular around the same time, and they decided to build a network business using AI. “There are these hidden networks in Silicon Valley of who the best people are for niche skills. Our idea was: Could we make that network of who the absolute best people are more transparent, and could we take all of the key insights that are locked inside their heads and make them more readily available?” Winters told TechCrunch over a call. Users can go to the SuperMe website and type their question into the chatbot to start a group of experts. They can also return to a group they have formed or joined and ask follow-up questions. You can ask a question like “Analyze my pitch deck. Which parts of the story are the weakest,and which questions should I be most prepared to answer?” and get answers from AI profiles of longtime VCs like Mercedes Bent, Sarah Tavel, and Josh Elman. Users can also go to these people’s profiles and ask questions directly rather than in a group chat. To collect knowledge from users, SuperMe scans public sources such as LinkedIn, YouTube, Substack, and blogs. It also draws on connected apps such as Google Drive, Notion, Gmail, and meeting notetaking tools like Granola. SuperMe said some of this data will include personal information and trade secrets, but it makes sure that none of it appears in answers. The network is currently free, but the startup plans to cap the number of questions users can ask, after which they will have to cough up some money. It is also considering how to give experts incentives to add more knowledge. SuperMe also works with MCP (Model Context Protocol, a standard that lets AI chatbots connect to outside tools and data), so users can ask questions from within chatbots such as Claude and ChatGPT. The startup has about 13 employees. It is hiring on the tech side to improve its AI models for conversation and for matching questions to experts, and on the network side for someone to find experts in subject areas the platform doesn’t yet cover. Forerunner’s Green said that SuperMe is well positioned to build a platform where AI collaborates with humans. “I think it’s one of the reasons why this is very timely, and it’s good to have something like this establishing itself when we’re relatively early in this journey,” she told TechCrunch over a call. “Because there’s going to be so many lessons that are going to be learned, and they need to be shared so that people can come along in a productive way. I think that the balance here is to build a network so that you do have a true sharing component where there are incentives and benefits for everybody to contribute what they know.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Ivan covers global consumer tech developments at TechCrunch. He is based out of India and has previously worked at publications including Huffington Post and The Next Web. Agent identity startup Mintive lands Cowboy Ventures, Coatue, angels Jeff Dean, Andrej Karpathy

TechCrunch | 2026-10-29

Here’s another early-stage, agent-management startup to watch because of the backgrounds of the founders and the sheer number of big names it has already attracted as investors: Mintive. Mintive has raised $11 million total, including a newly announced $7 million seed led by Cowboy Ventures. Additional investors include Coatue, Operator Collective, and Hustle Fund; angels include Okta co-founder Frederic Kerrest, Discovery Loop co-founder and former Google Chief Scientist Jeff Dean, and OpenAI co-founder Andrej Karpathy, among others. Mintive was founded by Jiquan Ngiam (pictured above, far right) and Vijay Vasudevan (pictured above far left). Ngiam, Mintive’s CEO, was the founding engineer of Coursera (employee No. 1, hired by Andrew Ng). Ngiam later worked on neural networks at Google Brain. Vasudevan, the company’s CTO, is best known as one of the co-creators of TensorFlow while he was at Google Brain. That was the OG AI training platform that predated PyTorch. Mintive is joining a crowded field of products that promise to govern, provision, and offer identity management to enterprise AI agents. Its platform includes an MCP gateway that allows IT professionals to authorize what the cloud services agents may access and what actions they can perform on them. It also offers an LLM gateway that routes to different models and can cap costs; handles agent identities and authorizations that may differ from the employees that they serve; and an agent monitor that tracks all agents across the company. The company also allows enterprises to create agents that proactively perform long-running tasks. Still, it’s hard to imagine a field with more competition. Agent management systems are offered by Amazon, Microsoft, OpenAI, Anthropic, plus as point products galore (from companies as diverse as LangChain, New Relic, and Rippling, to name a few). Then there are countless startups working on the idea (40+ from Y Combinator alone). So, what’s Mintive’s secret sauce? Given the founders’ long history in building AI for enterprises, they have designed their system to be especially easy to use, Ngiam says. Set-up amounts to clicking a few buttons, the startup promises. “I think what our customers look at and they are a bit shocked at, is how quick it is and easy to deploy,” Ngiam tells TechCrunch. “We have really thought through all the ways this can be done, all the access control, permissioning layer, to make it so easy for them to deploy it, that all the complexities are hidden away. You don’t think about sandboxes, you don’t think about harnesses, you don’t think about memory because we manage that for you.” That pitch has won over 50 enterprise customers so far, Mintive says, since the startup launched its product in February. These include Ro, Modern Treasury, the University of Michigan, Coursera, Apax, and Stability AI. But it was the founders’ industry experience that won over Cowboy Ventures managing partner Aileen Lee. “AI is going to be able to power incredible things, but we need more infrastructure that’s going to give us governance and visibility and control,” Lee told TechCrunch. Lee also says she particularly respects that Ngiam has been careful by only raising $11 million. This compares to other agentic-building startups raising vast amounts at unicorn valuations. As just one example: Prime Intellect raised $130 million for its Series A at $1 billion valuation. “I feel really lucky to have won the chance to work with him,” said Lee. “He is a grown-up and so he was not a person chasing a ‘vanity’ round.” Lee added: “I think a lot of companies are being set up for failure by chasing round size, rather than, ‘how much do I need to actually really move the needle for the company?’ And so, yes, in a time when people are raising unprecedented amounts of money, we’re just kind of doing it the brick by brick, old-fashioned way.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. The race to build the winning AI assistant is on, and investors are spreading out their bets. On Thursday, another AI app, Wabi, now describing itself as a “personal agent,” said it has raised $55 million at a $1.25 billion valuation. Founded by Eugenia Kuyda, who previously founded the AI companion startup Replika, Wabi originally focused on allowing anyone to build apps using prompts. Last month, however, the startup announced a pivot of sorts to become an AI messenger. The idea now is that the new Wabi — or Wabi 2.0, as it was called — could build the interfaces you need to get things done as part of its role as your personal AI agent. The company suggests Wabi could be used to build mini apps for things like meal planning, tracking local restaurants, planning date nights, creating fitness plans, designing specialized trivia games, and more. The additional funding indicates that Wabi’s investors agree that its new agentic angle has some potential, especially given the market around AI agents is hot right now, with viral AI agent Instinct pulling in another billion at a $10 billion valuation in September. Bluesky blocks links to leaked DHS employee data as government seeks takedowns
2026-10-29

Bluesky blocks links to leaked DHS employee data as government seeks takedowns

TechCrunch | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Following a sizable leak of personnel data about employees who work for the U.S. Department of Homeland Security, social network Bluesky is now blocking its users from accessing a nonprofit’s website that hosts the leaked dataset. Users reported that the links to nonprofit transparency collective DDoSecrets were marked as “malicious” soon after TechCrunch and other media outlets broke the news that Homeland Security had been hacked. TechCrunch reported on Wednesday that a hacker obtained over 40,000 user account records from Homeland Security’s login system, including the names and contact information, such as work-issued phone numbers and email addresses, of employees and agents working for its various agencies like Customs and Border Protection (CBP) and Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE). The data included information that was similar to that on an employee’s business card. DDoSecrets, which publishes leaked datasets in the public interest, also made the hacked data publicly available. Researchers who are investigating abuses by the Trump administration say that the data will be invaluable for seeking records from the government under public records laws. A spokesperson for Homeland Security told TechCrunch on Wednesday that it was “aware of a potential breach and is actively working to address the situation swiftly and effectively.” In a statement, Bluesky chief operating officer Rose Wang told TechCrunch: “Our Community Guidelines protect people’s safety and privacy by prohibiting the unauthorized sharing of personal information and other content that could pose safety risks. Because the files include the email addresses and phone numbers of individuals, shared without their authorization, we are blocking links to the dataset.” “When deciding between blocking a link and removing individual posts, we chose to block the links to avoid limiting public discussion or restricting broader access to reporting and commentary,” said Wang. In a post on Bluesky, Wang said that the company plans to change the wording on the block message. “We should have changed the wording last night. Changes incoming shortly,” said Wang. DDoSecrets co-founder and journalist Emma Best told TechCrunch that while Bluesky’s terms of service prohibit sharing home addresses and personal contact information, Best said that neither of these is in the leaked dataset. “The data contains professional contact information for government employees at a time when the public interest in that exact information has never been higher. By requiring the public to provide it for [Freedom of Information Act] requests, the government has made that exact information a crucial piece of the transparency channels they want the public to engage in,” said Best. Best told TechCrunch that a website hosting a searchable copy of the stolen data was taken down by its domain registrar, effectively knocking the website offline for now. A search site, dubbed Icepick, made by journalist maia arson crimew no longer loads as of Thursday morning. An internet record associated with the domain says it has been placed on “serverHold,” which generally means that the domain registrar locked or closed the domain for some reason. Homeland Security filed a takedown notice via a security vendor called ZeroFox, which has authority to act on the department’s behalf until 2028 to seek the removal of data from the internet. TechCrunch has seen a copy of the takedown notice for the search site, which asks that the domain registrar “stop serving the personal contact and location data of individuals who did not consent to its publication.” CentralNic, which hosts the domain name, did not respond to a request for comment from TechCrunch about the takedown or whether it was instructed to do so by a government demand. ZeroFox also did not respond to a request for comment. The Homeland Security spokesperson did not respond to a follow-up regarding the takedown notice affecting Icepick’s website. Crimew also made a locally hosted version of the data, allowing anyone to access it on their own computer. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Zack Whittaker is the security editor at TechCrunch. He also authors the weekly cybersecurity newsletter, this week in security. Alaska Governor Orders Probe Into Peltola Campaign Over Alleged Spoiler Scheme

Alaska Governor Orders Probe Into Peltola Campaign Over Alleged Spoiler Scheme

TIME | 2026-10-29

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Alaska’s Republican Governor Mike Dunleavy ordered an investigation into Democrat Mary Peltola’s Senate campaign following a report that her team helped recruit candidates to draw votes from her Republican opponent, including a man with the same name. The announcement, posted on social media overnight, followed President Donald Trump’s call for Peltola’s prosecution and escalated Republican efforts to challenge her candidacy just days before an election that could help determine control of the Senate. Read More: Republican Panic Sets In Over Prospects of a Massive Blue Wave On Thursday, Trump declined to say whether Peltola should be seated if she won. “Well, you’re going to have to see what happens,” he told reporters before leaving for a rally in South Carolina. “It’s hard to believe that she could even be allowed to run.” Peltola is challenging Republican Senator Dan S. Sullivan in the Nov. 3 election. Another candidate, Daniel J. Sullivan Jr., a retired teacher from Petersburg, has denied running at Democrats’ behest. Alaska’s Supreme Court upheld his eligibility for the ballot earlier this year. The dispute intensified Wednesday after a New York Times report found that Peltola's team worked with people to recruit men named Dan Sullivan to run for Senate, as well as other candidates who could draw votes away from the incumbent. Dunleavy said the reporting provided new evidence of an effort to mislead voters and supported state election officials’ earlier findings about Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy. “Deliberately tricking voters into casting their ballots for someone else strikes at the integrity of our elections,” the governor wrote. Peltola, a former congresswoman, dismissed the report, arguing that it diverted attention from issues including the incumbent Sullivan’s support for the war in Iran. “This is just another distraction. It’s another diversion,” she said during a campaign stop in Soldotna on Wednesday. “This is all stuff to sell newspapers. This is all stuff to get subscriptions.” A spokesperson for Peltola’s campaign, Alaskans for Mary, said in an email to TIME that it “has no involvement with either Sullivan campaign.” The campaign also said it had made no assertions in federal court about how Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy came about or who was involved in his decision to run. The Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee (DSCC), which was also reportedly involved in the plan, told TIME in a statement that it was “not involved with Dan J. Sullivan.” Vice President JD Vance also backed an investigation Thursday, accusing Peltola of trying to mislead voters about her campaign’s involvement. “She is trying to steal a Senate seat from the voters of Alaska, and it’s disgraceful,” he told reporters before a campaign trip to Texas. Trump had accused Peltola and her campaign of voter fraud in a series of Truth Social posts Wednesday, calling for her removal from the ballot and prosecution. “It was just determined that the Dumocrats in Alaska, despite their sworn statements to the contrary, put a man named Dan Sullivan on the Ballot in order to confuse the Public with respect to Highly Esteemed Senator Dan Sullivan,” Trump posted. “This is Voter Fraud at the Highest Level, and the Dumocrat Candidate, who was involved in the scheme, Mary Peltola, should be removed from the Ballot and prosecuted—So should the FAKE Dan Sullivan. Nothing else will do!” The Times noted that ballot-stacking tactics are generally considered legal and have been used by both parties. At Trump’s direction, the Justice Department had already filed a lawsuit seeking to block certification of the election results under specific conditions if Senator Sullivan loses, citing the potential for “voter confusion.” The department wants the results recalculated as though votes for Daniel J. Sullivan had gone to the incumbent, and certification blocked if that would change the outcome. TIME reached out to the Justice Department for comment on how the report affects its case. Attorney General Todd Blanche told Fox News on Wednesday that he expected to “hear more” from the department this week as officials receive new information. “They are cheating,” Blanche said. “They are trying to take votes away from the incumbent, not by running an election, not by running on issues, but by putting up a person with the same name, so that confuses Alaskans.” According to the Times’ report, which was based on interviews, text messages, and documents prepared for a possible lawsuit, the Peltola campaign pursued a project known internally as “ballot composition,” which involved campaign lawyers and senior aides at the DSCC, which directs Democratic campaign strategy for Senate races nationwide. One of the Times’ sources, Elisa Rios, Peltola’s former campaign manager who was fired during an August staff shake-up, said the operation to recruit potential spoiler candidates for the Alaska ballot began in the summer of 2025. The Times reported that Peltola’s campaign worked with a “veteran Democratic operative” named Jerid Kurtz, whom it described as having expertise in ballot maneuvering. The Times reported that a Peltola staffer received instructions to “find as many people named Dan Sullivan in Alaska” as possible. Kurtz reportedly found a man in Petersburg named Dan Sullivan who was open to running for Senate. Rios said Peltola wasn’t directly involved in the project but was “definitely aware.” The Times also reported that Rios later wanted to recant her statements but did not explain why. Alaska has a nonpartisan primary system, in which voters may choose a candidate regardless of party affiliation, and the top four advance to the general election. Voters then rank candidates in order of preference. If no candidate wins an initial majority, the lowest-ranking candidates are eliminated in successive rounds and their ballots transfer to voters’ next remaining choices. Alaska’s other Senator, Lisa Murkowski, who endorsed Sullivan but supported Peltola years ago, issued a statement Wednesday demanding that Peltola tell the “honest truth” about the apparent ballot-stacking effort. “While some may try to write this off as just how politics works now, ballot-stacking should have no place in our electoral process,” Murkowski wrote. “Run and win on your merits: your views, your policy ideas, and your accomplishments. By contrast, recruiting candidates to manipulate the field and influence voting outcomes is simply wrong.” Nate Adams, a spokesperson for Sullivan’s campaign, also said in a statement that “not one player in this operation has acted in good faith with Alaska voters.” Republicans have also used similar ballot-stacking tactics. In this year’s Alaska primary, a GOP-aligned political action committee reportedly spent money to boost left-leaning fringe candidates in an attempt to pull votes from Peltola. When Peltola ran for re-election to the House in 2024, Alaska’s Republican Party supported an effort to help a federally imprisoned Democrat who had never lived in Alaska stay on the ballot. A GOP-linked political action committee also spent over $1 million this election cycle to boost a third-party candidate in Ohio in a bid to undermine former Senator Sherrod Brown’s comeback bid. In Minnesota in 2020, Legal Marijuana Now Party candidate Adam Weeks told a close friend before his death that he was recruited by unnamed Republicans to pull votes away from Democrats.
