How Europe Is Defying Trump by Rallying Around the ICC
2026-10-29

How Europe Is Defying Trump by Rallying Around the ICC

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump takes a meeting at Turnberry, south west Scotland, on July 27, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings As the Trump Administration forges ahead with its campaign to dismantle the International Criminal Court (ICC) “brick by brick,” Europe is publicly showing a steady stream of support for the institution. The E.U. last week awarded the court and its officials the Sakharov human rights prize, commending “the important work ​of the court in upholding international law and justice.” The prestigious recognition came after former ICC judge Navanethem 'Navi' Pillay on Oct. 9 was awarded the 2026 Nobel Peace Prize—an honor long coveted by President Donald Trump. Jørgen Watne Frydnes, chair of the Norwegian Nobel Committee, acknowledged the award was intended to send a “message to those who are attacking, undermin[ing]... the international rules-based system.” Hours after Pillay’s win, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio announced sweeping new sanctions against the ICC. While previous U.S. sanctions had targeted the institution's prosecutors and judges, the new measures were aimed at “cutting off [the court’s] resources” by severing U.S. transactions with the court. Experts warn that the sanctions could have “extremely disruptive” implications for the court, especially in regards to financial support for case work, travel to the U.S. for judges, and the core functions of the institution itself. “Leaving the political dimension to one side, the rule of law and the just resolution of disputes depends, to a large extent, on the body in question having all available evidence,” says Nabil Khabirpour, an affiliated lecturer in E.U. law and jurisprudence at Cambridge University. “Organizations that are involved in working with witnesses, both on the ground and within the legal infrastructure, are likely to be affected.” The latest sanctions, which were announced with a six-month initial grace period, are part of a broader push by the Trump Administration to dismantle the institution it accuses of unfairly targeting the U.S. and Israel. The Trump Administration has staunchly criticized the ICC over arrest warrants issued for Israeli leaders, including ​Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, and former investigations into the actions of American soldiers in Afghanistan. The ICC is recognized as the world’s highest criminal court. It draws its jurisdiction from the Rome Statute, a treaty that went into effect in 2002 and is ratified by 125 countries. Neither the U.S. nor Israel is a state party to the Rome Statute, and therefore they do not recognize the jurisdiction of the court. Trump has long complained that the ICC oversteps its authority and should not claim jurisdiction over U.S. citizens. He amplified his campaign against the organization at the United Nations General Assembly in New York last month, taking time out of his 45-minute speech to publicly call on “all nations that are members of the ICC to officially resign from this rogue institution, immediately.” He said the U.S. is “opposed to the out-of-control institution known as the ICC” and “will never allow U.S. service members or anyone else to be investigated or given show trials by an anti-American tribunal with no jurisdiction over us.” His call to action was swiftly rejected by European leaders. German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul, shortly after the speech, said: "We will, of course, not be doing that." In an interview with Germany's Deutschlandfunk radio station, he described the court as "an important institution” and said the issue is "one of the areas where we do indeed have to acknowledge a regrettable difference in policy from the U.S. Administration." Netherlands Prime Minister Rob Jetten also defended the ICC, asking how can anyone “possibly be opposed to prosecuting the very worst crimes?” “​I ⁠believe there can be only one response,” he continued, in his own U.N. address. “To say: 'hands off' the ICC, and all those other institutions that protect the international legal order.” The remarks drew loud applause from attending delegates. Similarly, European Council president Antonio Costa stressed that the E.U. stands “firmly behind” the ICC and argued it’s “unacceptable to threaten or attack the International Criminal Court, its officials, and its staff.” The European Union as an organization has since strongly criticized the U.S. sanctions against the ICC. In a speech on Oct. 20, E.U. policy chief Kaja Kallas said the most recent “misguided” measures were “against” European interests. “The U.S. has already adopted individual sanctions, including against nine judges and the chief prosecutor of the ICC. The latest U.S. announcement [on Oct. 9] to impose institutional sanctions puts the entire court at risk,” she argued, adding that Brussels will use the next six months "to open dialogue and look for solutions.” The European Commission’s choice to take time to consider how to counter the U.S. sanctions has fallen short of what many European lawmakers have called for. Several leading MEPs, including Germany's David McAllister, chair of the Committee on Foreign Affairs, and France's Mounir Satouri, chair of the Subcommittee on Human Rights, issued a public statement on Oct. 9 calling for the Commission to activate a legislative shield referred to as the “blocking statute,” which would essentially prevent E.U. firms from complying with U.S. sanctions. “The European Union must now demonstrate unwavering solidarity with the ICC and its personnel—not only with words, but also with concrete acts,” the leading Members of the European Parliament (MEPs) said in a joint statement. “The European Commission must immediately trigger the E.U. blocking statute, shielding the court and European actors from the extraterritorial reach of these sanctions. Europe must stand firm in defence of international law and the rule of law." This request was bolstered as 58 MEPs reportedly sent a letter to European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen for the Commission to activate the measure. According to the Commission, the purpose of the European Union’s blocking statute is to “protect E.U. operators from the extra-territorial application of third country laws.” The blocking statute came into force in 1996 in direct response to the U.S. sanctions against Cuba. The aim of the measure was to protect economic operators within the E.U. from the effects of such extraterritorial sanctions, according to the Norton Rose Fulbright law firm. As such, the blocking statute shields European economic operators from the application of the U.S. legislation and sanctions specified in the Annex to the E.U. blocking statute. More recently, the E.U.'s updated blocking statute entered into force in response to U.S. sanctions on Iran after the U.S. withdrew from the nuclear agreement in 2018. Kallas has expressed an openness to the measure, but also advised caution when delivering her statement on European support for the ICC. “We should keep all options on the table. If we use the blocking statute, we have one shot and we should do it well. So, it should be well prepared," Kallas said, urging companies “not to over comply” with the sanctions, as they are not yet in effect. ICC prosecutor Nazhat Shameem Khan told the Guardian last week that some ICC staff are already facing "banking difficulties," despite the grace period before the sanctions come into effect. Khan said the banks were “over complying” to minimize risk amid uncertainty over the scope of the measures. She said the court's work has not been hindered. TIME has reached out to the ICC for comment. If the E.U. were to activate the blocking statute, it would be a “very important gesture” that could show the Trump Administration how “Europe is serious about protecting the court and that it is prepared to take real steps in that direction,” says Sergey Vasiliev, a professor of international law at Open University Netherlands. But he warns that some may view it as a “very extreme or radical measure, that could perhaps anger the Trump Administration even more” at a time where U.S.-Europe relations are already strained. Meanwhile, some ICC officials have suggested alternative measures in an effort to mitigate the expected impact of the U.S. sanctions. Osvaldo Zavala Giler, the court's registrar, told Politico that “comfort letters” could be sent out, reassuring firms that they needn’t comply with the U.S. measures. But Vasiliev argues that this “will not necessarily solve the problem, because businesses are expecting clear assurances of protection… the only such assurance can be provided by the actual triggering of the blocking statute.” Relations between Europe and the U.S. have been under increasing strain since Trump returned to office in January 2025. Trump has introduced various waves of tariffs on international trading partners, and has threatened further levies on the E.U. and member states in response to divisive issues. When Trump’s pursuit of acquiring Greenland as a U.S. territory reached a critical point in January, Trump threatened to tariff European countries that opposed his campaign—a move that earned him strong rebuke from within his own party. Last month, when von der Leyen invited Canada to work toward becoming the European Union’s first “associate member,” in what she described as an “urgent” reimagining of partnerships, Trump again threatened economic pressure. He called the proposal “laughable” and threatened to “put very serious ​tariffs or stop trading with Europe on many things” if such an idea were to go ahead. The Trump Administration has also clashed with its NATO allies. Earlier this year, Trump refused to rule out leaving the alliance over Greenland disputes, and since the start of the Iran war, the President has hit out at NATO countries for refusing to get actively involved in the conflict. Should Brussels and Washington continue to clash on the ICC sanctions, “this could risk accelerating certain destabilizations, such as what we've seen with the trade wars,” says Khabirpour. International organizations will ultimately stand to suffer if the two remain at odds, he adds.

Ohio’s Data-Center Revolt Is Upending the Midterms
2026-10-29

Ohio’s Data-Center Revolt Is Upending the Midterms

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Patty Spillman’s farm in Grove City, Ohio, abuts the site of a proposed 310-acre data center Patty Spillman and Jen Belt drive through the outskirts of Grove City, Ohio, mapping the battle lines of a war they’ve waged for months. Pointing out the window of Belt’s blue Chrysler mini-van, Spillman notes the precise borders of a proposed 310-acre data-center campus. She singles out the electrical substation and towering power lines, and recounts the personal histories of neighbors who got offers to sell their land to Headwaters Site Development, the Texas-based company proposing to construct a cloud-computing storage facility on this patch of rural farmland about 10 miles outside Columbus. In between fields of drying corn-stalks and short rows of soybeans, some farmers have planted “no data center” signs on their lawns. Spillman’s 165-year-old farm sits across the street from the proposed complex. A soft-spoken retired bus driver, Spillman, 63, is a Republican who voted for President Donald Trump three times. Belt, a 51-year-old artist and marketing contractor, is a Democrat. For months, they’ve co-led a group called Protect Grove City, which mobilized a community effort to thwart the proposed data center. The residents’ concerns are legion: water usage, noise and environmental concerns, energy costs, the cloak of secrecy hanging over the project. “This is our community. What if we don’t want you here?” says Spillman, whose mother-in-law turned down an above-market offer from Headwaters of around $1.5 million for 30 acres of land. Community pushback prompted the Grove City council to pass a yearlong moratorium on data-center construction. In November, Grove City residents will vote on a proposal to go even further, making all large developments subject to voter approval. The issue has upended the intimate politics of this conservative community of 45,180. Community Facebook groups are filled with bickering, angry residents have filled council meetings, and activists filed freedom-of-information requests seeking answers about city officials’ communications about the referendum. Grove City’s longtime mayor, Richard “Ike” Stage, and other local leaders have fought against their efforts, saying the ballot initiative to restrict development would hurt economic growth. TIME illustration (Source image: Wong Yu Liang–Getty Images) The fervor against data centers is spreading across the state. Ohio has some 200 data centers, making it one of the biggest hubs in the country. In a former swing state that went red in the Trump era, Republicans admit the groundswell of political opposition to data centers has created a political opening for Ohio Democrats in the upcoming midterm elections. “I’m as Republican as they get, but it’s going to be a tough year for Republicans in the state of Ohio,” says Nick Owens, a former county GOP chair and Trump delegate at the 2024 Republican National Convention. Owens is now working with the anti-data-center group Conserve Ohio. “I can get on my phone right now and text or call 10 or 12 people from 10 different corners of the state, from all different political backgrounds, from all different socio-economic backgrounds,” Owens says, “because we’re all united about this.” A national Gallup poll published in May found that more than 70% of voters oppose building an AI data center in their area. In Ohio, where the average household’s electric bill climbed some 15% in the past year and more than 66% over the past decade, the sentiment is even more negative. A September survey by Bowling Green State University found that nearly 80% of Ohioans support a moratorium on data centers. More than 100 local governments in Ohio have reportedly enacted data-center moratoriums; in November, at least 10 will vote on adding a data-center ban to their charters, while two others will decide whether to recall mayors who cut data-center deals. In the eyes of opponents, the facilities—and the powerful AI industry that backs them—have come to represent an attack on their safety, jobs, pocketbooks, and quality of life. It’s not clear just how potent the issue will be at the polls in November. By themselves, data centers don’t rank as a top-tier national campaign issue, according to voters—in a recent Pew Research Center survey, the economy, the future of democracy, health care, education, and immigration made up the top five. But Democratic candidates have sought to connect data centers to many of those themes. They’re promising to ease affordability concerns by cracking down on high electricity bills through data-center restrictions, pledging to strengthen democracy by fighting Silicon Valley’s opaque deals to get facilities built, casting AI companies as puppet masters in an unequal economy, and painting their political rivals as corrupt leaders seeking to curry favor from powerful companies. This election cycle, $137.6 million has been spent on broadcast ads about data centers, with Democrats making up 77% of that estimated spending, according to the analytics firm AdImpact. Some Democrats believe data centers could be a decisive issue in tight races, especially in red states like Texas, Iowa, and Ohio. In those states and others, voters have told pollsters they trust Democrats more on the issue. President Donald Trump has positioned himself as one of the industry’s biggest champions, deriding opponents of data centers as “backwards and poor.” The wave of populist anger has “become the one thing that unites Americans right now,” says Megan Mullin, a UCLA political-science professor. “They connect to bigger themes that are becoming really important in American politics—around affordability, around energy affordability, around suspicion and discomfort about AI, about populist resistance to the accumulation of wealth and tech titans.” In early October, Don Leonard, the Democratic candidate in Ohio’s 15th Congressional District, arrives at the Schmidlapp Free School Library in Piqua. The space is decorated for fall with artificial flowers, and filled with activists and community leaders ready to detail the latest data-center controversy. Meta, which has been rapidly building out its data-center infrastructure to fuel its AI push, made plans to erect a $1 billion campus in the area. The company hashed out the deal in secret last year, according to city documents and news reports, using a private LLC and nondisclosure agreements with local officials. After Meta was outed as the company behind the data center, the social media giant and Piqua government officials said the project would bring economic investment to the region. In a statement, Meta told TIME the company is reviewing its use of nondisclosure agreements. Leonard heard from Alisha Lange, 42, who worried about contamination of her well water. Lange, a registered Republican, was among those angered by the secrecy surrounding the project and revelations that several city officials had been awarded $3,000 bonuses by the city after winning Meta’s business. In response, outraged residents successfully petitioned to add a measure to the November ballot designed to recall Piqua’s mayor and other elected officials. A former city and regional planning professor at The Ohio State University, Leonard, 48, sports a stubbly beard and speaks with a deliberate unpretentiousness. Normally, a progressive candidate wouldn’t stand much of a shot in a congressional district that’s been represented by Republicans for the past 15 years. The incumbent, former coal-industry lobbyist Mike Carey, won by 13 points two years ago. But by the time Leonard won the primary in May, residents in the district, which includes Grove City, were aflame over data centers. Leonard pushed his team to add a data-center question to their polling. They found more than 70% of likely voters in the 15th District wanted greater say over where data centers are located and how they operate. “If we were going to message on it, we had to confirm quantitatively what we knew from listening to the constituents,” Leonard explains in an interview at a modern co-working space in Columbus. “Which is that data centers are one of the most bipartisan issues in Central Ohio right now.” Leonard sees data centers as “the mascot for predatory capitalism,” forcing communities to absorb increased electricity costs and environmental threats as their local landscapes are remade by huge companies based far away. He says the developments have exacerbated the anxieties of residents who watched neighbors lose solid factory jobs to outsourcing and suspect they’ll once again be the losers in an AI-driven economy. “There’s a feeling like we didn’t even govern the last revolution—globalization—and now they’re hoisting another one on us,” Leonard says. “They’re doing it in our backyard. They’re attacking our way of life.” It’s not clear whether the backlash will be enough to catapult Leonard into contention in a solidly Republican district. There has been no non-partisan polling in the race, nor any public debates, and Carey holds a clear fundraising advantage, raking in $4 million to Leonard’s $1.2 million, according to campaign filings, though a recent influx of spending against Leonard is a sign Republicans may be a little nervous. But in other races in Ohio, Democrats spy a clear opportunity. On a crisp fall day, Sherrod Brown takes the microphone during a small rally outside a Franklin County strip mall. He begins with talking points about economic inequality that he could have fed this crowd of Democratic organizers and voters at any point during his 18 years representing the state in the Senate. But then Brown pivots to an attack line that might have landed with a thud even a year ago. “These data centers are in Ohio now thanks to Jon Husted,” Brown says of his Republican opponent. “They come into communities. They gulp up the land. They swallow the water. Then they get these huge tax breaks.” After three terms in Washington, Brown was defeated in 2024, when Trump won Ohio for the third straight time. The cornerstone of his comeback bid this year has been the data-center issue. In stump speeches, social media posts, and advertisements blaring across television, Brown blames Husted for the role he played as lieutenant governor in fostering data-center growth in the state, which gave data centers an estimated $2.5 billion in incentives, including breaks on sales and property taxes, from 2017 to 2024, according to a report by the Ohio Chamber of Commerce. (The report also estimates that the centers brought in twice as much in revenue to the state’s coffers.) Back then, Husted, like other political leaders in both parties, often celebrated tech companies like Google and Amazon, which built the sprawling campuses, as a boost to Ohio’s economy. Husted campaign spokesperson Amy Natoce called Brown’s critiques hypocritical, noting that in 2015 the former Senator himself praised an Amazon Web Services data-center project in Ohio, calling it “great news.” Now Husted is playing defense on the issue, sponsoring a Senate bill designed to get data centers to pay for electrical-system upgrades that was ultimately blocked by Democrats. Data centers should be a local decision, he says. Polls show the race as a toss-up. But a recent New York Times/Siena poll found that Ohio was one of five right-leaning states—Alaska, Iowa, Kansas, and Texas are the others—where Democrats are outperforming expectations in Senate races, in part because voters said they thought the Democratic candidate would do a better job on data centers. The party is eager to press that advantage. Dr. Amy Acton, the Democratic candidate for Ohio governor, has called for a conditional moratorium on data centers until they meet certain requirements, such as covering the cost of their own electricity, meeting environmental standards, and participating in community-benefit agreements. Vivek Ramaswamy, the Republican nominee, wants to force hyperscale data centers to cover electricity costs and abide by air- and water-quality standards. Acton has assailed Ramaswamy for not committing to selling off his investments in tech and AI stocks, calling these holdings a conflict of interest. A similar pattern is playing out in other states. In Texas, both Democratic Senate nominee James Talarico and his Republican opponent Ken Paxton have proposed tighter restrictions on data centers and accused each other of being supported by tech-aligned donors. The state’s Republican governor Greg Abbott, who is locked in a surprisingly tight race of his own, has imposed a temporary moratorium on new data centers until an audit is complete. In Iowa, data centers have been a flash point in the race between Democratic Senate nominee Josh Turek and Republican nominee Ashley Hinson, with Turek proposing restrictions and Hinson emphasizing the need for local control. Ike Stage first became the mayor of Grove City in 1988. A retired banker who helped set up the local chamber of commerce, he credits cheapish land, a convenient location, and a business-friendly climate for the steady stream of calls he’s fielded from developers over the years. Companies like medical manufacturer American Nitrile have moved in, along with three hospitals and two scientific-research facilities. “Not many cities can say that,” Stage says in an interview at city hall, where brightly colored maps of the area adorn the walls. Stage has aspirations for Grove City to grow further. In 2018, it adopted a vision that included better transportation options, more parks, and mixed-use developments. Officials also eyed more than 9,000 acres of agricultural and vacant land they thought could be turned into “light industrial-style development.” Seven years later, Headwaters Site Development, founded by former Google data-center infrastructure leader Michael Lebow, had a similar thought. It contacted property owners in a rural area just south of Grove City’s limits. One of the homesteads belonged to Patty Spillman’s family, which rejected the deal. “Most of us turned them down,” Spillman says. But not all. By this spring, Headwaters had found enough homeowners willing to sell to cobble together the necessary land. During two information sessions at a local food bank in April, Headwaters representatives pitched locals on what they called Project Maple. Headwaters says it plans to sell the $6.9 billion project to an unidentified tech company, and pledged to support community-friendly zoning requirements that would be assumed by the buyer, such as a 100-ft. height limit, concealing landscaping, and noise levels that aren’t louder than a dishwasher. Headwaters says the project would bring 250 permanent jobs, pay for its own energy costs, and take precautions to keep water usage low. But according to advocates, Head-waters’ refusal to say which tech company would ultimately buy the facility was unnerving. “They just skirted around giving us straight answers about anything,” Jen Belt recalls. “A lot of people left mad.” The anger emboldened the activists. Belt circulated a petition calling for the city council to reject any upcoming data-center applications. The group posted negative information about the facilities on their personal social media pages, as well as in popular Facebook groups, and put up no data centers signs near the proposed site. The following month, Grove City councilman Mohamed Omar, 26, announced he was working on legislation to impose a 12-month moratorium on local data centers. Headwaters responded by sending a letter to city council members, arguing that such a moratorium could violate its due process rights. (Lebow says the company has no plans to sue.) Yet the implied legal threat intensified the community pushback against the project on the eve of the moratorium vote. In June, the moratorium passed 6-1. Emboldened, the anti-data-center advocates with Protect Grove City decided the one-year moratorium wasn’t enough, and wrote a proposed amendment to the city’s charter, which functions like a governing constitution. It would prohibit the city from approving zoning applications for developments that occupy at least 50 acres, use more than 20 megawatts of power at peak capacity, or consume more than 500,000 gallons of water per day unless a majority of voters have backed the project in a public referendum. To drum up support, they canvassed local farmers’ markets, parks, and sporting events to ask people to sign a petition to put the measure on the ballot in November. Within a few weeks, they secured enough signatures. Councilman Mohamed Omar, who opposes data center development, in Grove City Maddie McGarvey for TIME City officials nervously tracked news coverage of the data-center backlash across Ohio and beyond, according to emails obtained by TIME. The charter amendment wouldn’t just inhibit the Headwaters project, they warned. It would fundamentally change how any future development deals are approved, and perhaps inhibit existing organizations, including churches, a hospital, and schools, from expanding by imposing overly burdensome processes. Critics contend residents already have opportunities to weigh in on upcoming developments through the normal city approval process. “It’s going to affect business. It’s going to affect our schools. It’s going to affect our taxes,” says Steve Funk, 70, who runs a car dealership in Grove City. The city has been sending mailers to residents explaining the amendment’s potential effects. Residents have gotten into bitter personal disputes over the amendment in Facebook groups. “It’s bad,” says Omar. “It almost feels like the city is torn.” To Stage, tech companies have done a poor job trumpeting the benefits the facilities bring to local communities, allowing residents’ anxieties about the industry to flourish. He likens the backlash to the fears Americans had about railroad expansion during the 19th century, when rural farmers worried about the effect the technology might have on their livestock. Americans eventually got used to trains. But Stage also recognizes that he’s on the short side of public opinion. Stage is up for re-election next year. “I’ve won most elections pretty handily, and I’ve been through 11 of them,” he says. “But you never, never make that assumption.” —With reporting by Leslie Dickstein

Trump’s Approval Is at a New Low. Here’s Where Other Presidents’ Stood Before the Midterms—and How Their Parties Fared
2026-10-29

Trump’s Approval Is at a New Low. Here’s Where Other Presidents’ Stood Before the Midterms—and How Their Parties Fared

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump waves goodbye after speaking during a midterm campaign rally at Lubbock Memorial Civic Center in Lubbock, Texas, on Oct. 27, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With the pivotal November elections looming, President Donald Trump’s approval rating earlier this month dropped to the lowest point of his second term—and the lowest level any President’s has reached heading into the midterms in decades. Trump’s approval rating fell to 30% in early October, according to polling from the Pew Research Center. The last time an American President’s approval rating was at a comparable low this close to the midterms was Harry Truman in 1946. “This is historically low approval ratings for a President,” says Peter Enns, a professor in the department of government at Cornell University and co-founder of the survey research company Verasight. Data indicates that much of Americans’ dissatisfaction with the Trump Administration is being driven by the economy; according to polling from The Associated Press-NORC Center for Public Affairs Research, released earlier this month, only 26% of U.S. adults express support for how Trump is tackling it. Similarly, just 17% of adults in the country say they approve of how the President is addressing the cost of living, according to the AP-NORC poll. Many adults also disagree with the Trump Administration’s war in Iran: the same AP-NORC poll found that only 28% expressed support for how Trump is handling the issue. Read More: The 7 Key Governor Races to Watch in the Midterm Elections “Part of the reason Trump’s approval ratings are so low is he’s pushing policies and pushing positions that are very unpopular, and so the conflict in Iran is a great example; the public understands that this was Trump’s decision, and they understand this connects to extremely high gas prices,” Enns says. “It’s very unusual for a President to position himself on the side of so many unpopular positions.” Those issues could be critical ones for voters as they go to the polls. The Pew Research Center found that the vast majority of registered voters—84%—say that the economy is a “very important issue” for them heading into the midterms. A smaller majority of 57% also deemed foreign policy “very important” to their vote in the upcoming elections. Even without a historically low approval rating, midterm elections are a challenging environment for the sitting President’s party. And political experts say that this year’s election looks like a difficult one for Republicans. Typically, the party in the White House loses congressional seats in the midterms. Enns says this is a “well-known phenomenon” that is likely a result of frustration from both a President’s supporters, who may feel that there hasn’t been enough progress on the policies they wanted to see coming out of the Administration, and those who didn’t vote for the President, who may be even more frustrated because they disagree with the Administration’s actions. “That doesn’t mean everybody’s changing their vote,” Enns points out. “It just means the backlash, the tides, are working against the incumbent President.” Costas Panagopoulos, a professor of political science at Northeastern University, says that a possible explanation for this historical trend is that voters may be seeking “partisan balance in Washington.” When voters aren’t satisfied by policies coming out of the Administration, they may try supporting the opposition party in the midterms—particularly when one party controls the White House and both chambers of Congress, as is the case with Republicans right now, Panagopoulos says. “There’s simply nobody else to blame for government actions and current policies if voters are unhappy with those besides the Republican Party [right now],” he continues. “It’s very easy for [voters] to point the finger to the Republicans if they’re not happy with what they’re seeing.” Jim Watson, Chip Somodevilla, Saul Loeb, pool (3), and Mark Wilson—Getty Images There are, though, exceptions to this trend. In 2002, then-President George W. Bush’s Republican Party actually gained seats in both the House and the Senate in the midterm elections. “It bucked the historical trend,” Panagopoulos says. “It’s one of the rare examples [of that].” Political experts like Panagopoulos largely attribute the GOP’s success in the 2002 midterms to Bush’s high approval rating—63% heading into the midterms, according to Gallup data—in the aftermath of the Sept. 11, 2001, terrorist attacks. There have also been some instances when the opposition party was expected to win big in the midterms but fell short of those predictions. Going into the 2022 midterms, for instance, then-President Joe Biden’s approval rating sat at 40%, according to Gallup polling, and Republicans were widely expected to see major victories in the election. And while the GOP gained nine seats in the House, that wasn’t as substantial a win as many had predicted—and Democrats actually gained a seat in the Senate. Panagopoulos suggests that part of the reason for that could be because Biden’s approval rating wasn’t at a historic low. He also points out that 2022 was the first election cycle with newly drawn district lines, which also could have affected the results. Michele Swers, a professor of American government at Georgetown University, puts forth another explanation: the 2022 midterms took place just a few months after the Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade, which may have harmed Republicans' advantage in the election. “Sometimes, the political environment can intervene,” Swers says. Swers likens the midterm elections to “a referendum on how things are going with the presidency.” “The President is the one figure that everybody in the nation knows,” she says. While the President himself isn’t on the ballot in the midterms, “in general, the President loses seats because people are angry with the President, and they take that out on the President’s party because the President is also the leader of their party.” Panagopoulos says that a President’s approval rating is “among the best predictors of outcomes in midterm election cycles” because it encapsulates “a wide range of sentiments about how voters perceive the performance of the incumbent party or president.” And Panagopoulos adds that the lower the President’s approval rating is, the greater the likelihood that his party will lose more congressional seats in the midterms. Read More: What Early Voting Data Is Showing About the Midterm Elections Political experts agree that Republicans are grappling with a difficult political climate right now. The vast majority of generic ballot polls—surveys that ask respondents which party they would back in the midterms or which party they would like to hold the majority in Congress after Election Day—show that Democrats are outpacing Republicans. Polling also indicates that Democratic voters are more enthusiastic about this midterm election than Republicans are; about 91% of Democrats compared to 86% of Republicans, according to a recent PBS News/NPR/Marist poll. Republicans are even struggling to maintain their usual advantage in staunchly red states, including in Texas, where Democratic Senate candidate James Talarico holds a small lead in the polls over his Republican opponent Ken Paxton. “I think we can expect Republicans to be facing an uphill battle at the polls,” Panagopoulos says. “We can expect that they will likely lose seats in the House and also the Senate. And the question is will they lose enough seats to lose the majority in either or both those chambers. That is entirely within the realm of possibility. And that has to do both with Trump’s relatively low approval rating, historically speaking, but also the constellation of candidates in the mix.” Panagopoulos points out that, while the party in the White House typically loses seats in the midterms, that doesn’t necessarily mean that the opposition party gains enough seats to win the majority in the House or the Senate; flipping the majority in both chambers can be challenging to do. Enns also notes that polling—both presidential approval ratings and vote intentions—is “a snapshot in time” and is “a representative sample” of likely or registered voters. “We don’t know until Election Day who is for sure going to vote,” Enns says. “The election outcome may not map onto exactly what we’d expect from the polls because who votes, we don’t know until Election Day.”

Halloween Is a Chance to Rethink Your Relationship With Fear
2026-10-29

Halloween Is a Chance to Rethink Your Relationship With Fear

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Whether you’re watching a scary movie or tiptoeing through a haunted house, Halloween can stir up spooky feelings. A gory scene may force you to cover your eyes or jump out of your seat. What drives these reactions is fear, a powerful core emotion. Being afraid can feel like getting stuck in your own horror show—your heart pounds, your pupils dilate, and you may gasp or scream. These reactions are not under conscious control; they’re rooted in biology. Like all emotions, fear plays an important role. It helps you survive and bolt for safety. Halloween gives you a chance to experiment with fear. It’s a time when the fright is real, but the threat is not. As psychotherapists who specialize in Accelerated Experiential Dynamic Psychotherapy (AEDP), we know that being able to name and work with fear is good for health. Acknowledging your emotions makes you less likely to suppress or avoid them, drink too much, or become numb to what you’re feeling. While these behaviors take the edge off temporarily, research shows that stuffing down your emotions can lead to mental-health concerns, such as depression and anxiety, which have become more common among U.S. children and adults in recent years. If you’d like to safely explore your relationship with fear this Halloween season, here are five exercises we recommend. Have you ever tried to outsmart fear? Perhaps you scolded yourself for being a scaredy-cat or tried to stop the feeling with logic. Messages from society and our families often promote a mind-over-matter approach, reinforcing the belief that we can think our way out of an emotion. A healthier approach is to get out of your head and tune into your body. During a frightening moment, notice the telltale signs of alarm, like trembling hands, a racing heart, or feeling cold. These physical sensations are important messengers that clue you into what core emotions you’re experiencing. Once you identify and name them, their intensity will likely diminish. It may sound like a small act, but research shows that putting language on your emotions can help regulate and tame distress. Gripping, painful emotions can seem permanent. But watching them come and go can remind your brain that even the most frightening experiences are temporary. During a spooky scene, you may want to cover your face or turn away. Rather than immediately distracting yourself, approach the emotion with curiosity. Observe how fear builds, peaks, and eventually subsides. Curiosity helps us embrace a sense of wonder. Research shows that a curious mindset can cultivate positive emotions and reduce worry. When fright takes hold, breathing techniques like 4-7-8 breathing or box breathing can soothe your nervous system. Taking long breaths instead of shallow ones sends oxygen to your muscles, which can help relieve tension. You can also practice what eye movement desensitization and reprocessing (EMDR) therapists call “dual attention.” Keep your eyes glued to the frightening scene and stay with the sensations in your body as you take deep belly breaths. Research shows that belly breathing increases parasympathetic regulation of the heart through vagal pathways, helping the body settle into a calmer state. When a ghoul jumps out or a frightening sound catches you off guard, pay attention to what your body wants to do. Do you feel an urge to flee, freeze, duck, or reach for someone’s hand? In AEDP, we call these “adaptive actions,” and they offer clues about how your nervous system responds to perceived danger. All core emotions, including fear, have impulses—energy in the body that makes us want to take action. When someone responds to our emotions with shame instead of empathy, we learn to block these impulses, creating a pressure-cooker effect. This can lead to symptoms like insomnia, muscular tension, and rumination that can hurt your health. Noticing fear’s impulses expands your emotional awareness and sets the stage for you to work through other core emotions with greater wisdom. Sharing a scary moment with another person can make it easier to tolerate. That’s because having someone by your side doesn’t just provide reassurance; it calms the amygdala, the brain’s emotional center, and helps you heal past wounds and traumas. We recommend this experiment: Watch a horror movie alone, and then with someone you trust. Or visit a haunted house with a trusted friend or partner. Notice what happens when you share the experience. A little emotions education goes a long way towards nurturing your well-being. By learning to stay with fear, you teach your brain that you can handle challenging emotions. And when we know emotions aren’t something to fear, we’re better equipped to handle life’s challenges with authenticity, flexibility, and courage. Spanish Transport Minister Blames Train Crash That Killed 69 on ‘Inexplicable’ Error

Spanish Transport Minister Blames Train Crash That Killed 69 on ‘Inexplicable’ Error

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Emergency services at the scene of a train crash near Urda, Toledo, Spain, on Oct. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Spanish authorities are investigating the cause behind a high-speed train collision Wednesday morning that killed at least 69 people and injured dozens of others. The country’s transport minister suggested that a combination of “inexplicable” human error and a computer failure was likely behind the tragedy, which occurred near Urda, Toledo, more than 80 miles south of Madrid. Transport Minister Óscar Puente said in a press conference that the state-operated Renfe train slamming into the rear of a stationary private train operated by Iryo was due to an “inexplicable” order from a command center for Spain’s trains. Puente said the center had a computer malfunction before the order. “It was the control center, through its operators, that made the decision to resume traffic, under circumstances that, from our point of view, are inexplicable,” Puente said. He added later: “Clearly, it was a wrong decision.” Puente said that they have identified the person who decided to let the Renfe train continue moving even though the Iryo one had already stopped, but he has not spoken to the control center operator. “This person is subject to whatever the investigation determines,” Puente said in the press conference. The regional government of Castilla-La Mancha, where the Toledo town is, said in a social media post that 53 people are still receiving medical treatment in healthcare facilities. The number of people hospitalized reached 107 earlier in the day. Renfe said train services between Madrid, Ciudad Real, and Andalusia have been suspended. The Wednesday collision is now the deadliest train crash in the country since a 2013 incident in Santiago de Compostela that killed 80 people. But the latest collision follows a similarly deadly train crash in January, which involved high-speed trains operated by the same two companies. In that crash, which occurred in Adamuz, Córdoba, 46 people died and 122 sustained injuries. Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, who visited the crash site, announced that his government would declare three days of mourning in response and vowed an investigation. “What is unacceptable for a country like Spain is to suffer two high-speed rail accidents in the same year,” Sánchez said, though he noted that it “is highly likely that the two accidents are unrelated.” Despite the accident, Puente insisted that the Spanish rail system is “safe and comparable to that of other countries in our region.” However, while there are “very clear” regulations and protocols in place, he admitted, “failures can obviously occur.” The crashes involved a Renfe high-speed train bound for Seville with 393 people on board, and an Iryo train carrying 151 passengers. The Renfe train departed Madrid at 8:00 a.m. local time, while the Iryo train left a little earlier at 7:54 a.m., plying the same route. Spain’s rail infrastructure uses the German LZB (Linienzugbeeinflussung) system, a railway safety technology that monitors train speeds and sends traffic instructions to the driver’s cab. Operations are meant to revert to manual control when the automated safety system fails. Puente, in the press conference, said that at around 8:30 a.m., three computers on the railway network experienced a communication failure, taking the LZB control system offline along a 63 km (39 mi) stretch of the track. This automatically stopped about six trains traveling on that section, Puente said. The central command center would assume manual management of rail traffic. By 8:36 a.m., the command center authorized the Renfe train to continue its journey “normally,” even though Puente says internal protocols dictate otherwise and that a “red signal” should have prevented the journey from resuming. The Renfe train had 25 km (15.5 mi) to travel before it struck the Iryo train, roughly 17 minutes later, at 8:53 a.m. The speed it reached before the impact is still unknown, but it could reach up to 200 km/h (124 mph) for that stretch of track. Spain is among the world leaders in investing in domestic high-speed rail infrastructure—defined as rails for trains that can travel 250 km/h (155 mph) and over, according to the European Commission’s Ninth Rail Market Monitoring report last year. The report shows that of European Union countries, Spain allocated the most to high-speed rail between 2018 and 2022, averaging €1.5 billion (around $1.67 billion) per year. Latest data from the International Union of Railways shows that Spain has laid nearly 4,000 km (2,400 mi) of high-speed track, second only to China, which has laid more than 47,000 km (28,000 mi). The European country only legally opened its domestic commercial high-speed passenger rail market to private competitors in December 2020, and Iryo became one of the commercial high-speed operators in Spain in 2022. Spanish news outlet El Debate reported, citing data from the State Railway Safety Agency, that the number of “significant accidents” on the country’s rail network has increased over the years.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Why There’s a Real Risk of a Replay of the 2020 Election Come Tuesday
2026-10-29

Why There’s a Real Risk of a Replay of the 2020 Election Come Tuesday

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The midterm elections are now underway across the country and as voters decide who should hold the levers of power in Washington, all eyes are on President Donald Trump. He’s spent the last decade baselessly questioning the integrity of U.S. elections—despite plenty of evidence finding they are safe and secure—with things coming to a violent head on Jan. 6, 2021. This year, as more and more polling is suggesting Democrats will sweep Republicans, even in solidly red districts, it raises the question: Will the president accept the results of critical House and Senate races, or won’t he? “I will, if they’re honest elections,” Trump ominously responded when asked this very question on Tuesday. Since Trump began his second term, he’s been laying the groundwork to try to nationalize U.S. elections and go after “voter fraud” through executive action and lawsuits against states, though most of these efforts have been struck down in court. Yet, the Justice Department has not given up. In fact, in recent weeks the agency filed at least two more dubious election lawsuits against California and Alaska, plus a series of cases against immigrants for alleged noncitizen voting. The legal challenges suggest the administration is planning ahead this time around, planting lawsuits that might create a foothold in the courts for the GOP to seize on if the midterms don’t end up going their way. To Charles Stewart III, a political scientist and professor at MIT who has spent decades studying American congressional politics, elections, and political development, the current situation created by Trump is a test of America’s democracy. “Not primarily because of fundamental shortcomings in American election administration, but because political actors associated with the Administration will attempt to delegitimize or overturn its work,” he wrote in a newly published paper, “The Day After: Threats and Guardrails in the 2026 Election.” Stewart believes that Trump’s efforts to thwart this year’s midterms should be viewed in three distinct phases: Before Election Day, during the vote counting and certification process, and when the new Congress convenes on Jan. 3, 2027. In practice, this looks like Trump beginning to seed baseless claims that losing Republicans can make after Election Day: mail ballots are suspect, voter rolls are contaminated with noncitizens, slow ballot counts are evidence of fraud, and any Democratic victories in close races shouldn’t be accepted. After Election Day, the lawsuits begin, harkening back to the days and weeks following Trump’s 2020 loss to Joe Biden, and finally things might culminate at the Capitol, where lawmakers could be pressured not to certify election winners. I spoke with Stewart to understand how this framework can help us see important red flags in the hours immediately following Election Day, plus the chances that Trump incites yet another angry mob to take over the Capitol. And is there any silver lining to hold on to? Here’s our conversation, lightly edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: In your paper, you drill down on the president’s efforts to undermine the midterms into three flashpoints: Before Election Day, during the vote counting and certification process, and when the new Congress convenes. Can you explain what each of these are? Charles Stewart III: The ideas behind those flashpoints come from what we’ve seen the Trump administration working on in the second term: two executive orders, activities at the Justice Department and Homeland Security, actions that are beginning to nibble around the edges of the election administration process. Particularly before Election Day, putting pressures on mail voting, highlighted by the executive order from this past March that basically directed the U.S. Postal Service to create a new series of procedures for handling mail voting, marrying that together with directives to DHS to create these lists of citizens. The second flashpoint has to do with pressures on election administration, especially election administrators and the institutions themselves. There, I think the pressures go beyond even what the administration has been doing. Certainly, a lot of the activity of the administration has been focused on the possibility that if election officials don’t do what the administration wants, they will not only bring civil complaints against them, but they may very well bring criminal complaints against them. Then after Election Day, that’s where we’re thinking about getting the final count in. We shift into the formal process of canvassing the vote, certifying it, and making sure that we’ve named the winners. I’m thinking broadly about a potential replay of what happened after the 2020 election, where the Trump campaign lodged a number of lawsuits in battleground states like Wisconsin and Pennsylvania. I’m still anticipating, even more now, that coming true. The final flashpoint is what happens when the 120th Congress convenes on Jan. 3, 2027, particularly the House. And there the question is whether there will be efforts to bar the seating of senators or members of the House who’ve been elected, specifically Democrats who would be the pivotal members of the two chambers that would flip control of the chamber to the Democrats. There’s another obvious parallel with 2020, when there were efforts to deny the certification of the presidential election. How do the Justice Department’s lawsuits against California and Alaska fit into this? Those are kind of jumping the gun on the timeline that I had suspected, because they’re really almost preempting post-election challenges and making them pre-election challenges. About a week ago I was saying, “Well, they’ve filed this lawsuit against California, it’s getting kind of close to the election. If there’s just California, it’s kind of a weak rule in terms of applying formal pressure on the states.” Then came Alaska, and this one makes me wonder what else the DOJ is going to do in the next week to bring early lawsuits against states and kind of do a shot across the bow. The California lawsuit is the sort of thing that we that we’ve long expected the Trump administration to do, and in fact in the first administration, there were rumors that they were trying to put together a national voter database and merge it with immigration records to begin to go after states for having noncitizens on the rolls. This has been a long dream of the administration and people associated with the administration, and it’s taken them basically a decade in order to act on it. It doesn’t look like they’ve done a particularly sophisticated job, both in terms of the actual job of database matching or on the legal side. The Alaska case seems to be the most frivolous election challenge that I can imagine, and in fact it’s kind of orthogonal. It’s unrelated to the things I had anticipated, because the lawsuit they’re bringing is the sort of lawsuit that you would expect a candidate to bring. Really, it’s up to Sen. Sullivan to bring these challenges under Alaska law and then potentially to bring a contest to the U.S. Senate. I only have to believe that the Alaska case is the classic case of pleasing an audience of one; Donald Trump wanted it and he got it. There’s just no way in the world this is going to go forward. It’s sort of like going after James Comey or going after the members of Congress who reminded service members they didn’t have to follow illegal orders. It’s really performative, to please the boss, and hard to take seriously, except the degree to which it’s predictive of what we’re likely to see after the election: the federal government putting the thumb on the scales of candidate-oriented post-election litigation. Next week, immediately after Election Day is over, what are you going to be watching for? I think there will be two things. One will be legitimate, and the other will be illegitimate. The legitimate thing is that we may very well see more losing candidates contest their elections and begin taking legal actions to try to save evidence, within the normal contest laws. Some of it may very well be performative to garner support from the administration or local political actors, but those are the ill-fated sorts of things that there’s a legal framework for doing. Plus there’s a legal framework for judges throwing these cases out, right? And that’s the way it should happen. What I think we need to be prepared for, and I don’t think it’s going to be widespread, are candidates supported by the administration going into court to attempt to impound voting machines and ballots and to remove them from where they’re normally housed. Basically, a repeat of the Fulton County event a year ago where the FBI removed ballots. Those are the things I think we should anticipate, and I think that the Department of Justice may very well take matters into their own hands that are quasi-independent of the candidates themselves. For instance, the Alaska lawsuit was just something that the DOJ did because the president wanted it. That’s a disturbing thing that I think we need to be prepared for after the election because we’ve already seen it happen before the election. That is the DOJ just going into places they don’t like and filing lawsuits. In your paper, you wrote, “The greatest danger is not that even a few challenges will succeed; it is that a small number of delayed certifications could matter greatly if control of either chamber is in doubt.” What did you mean by that? If you think about Jan. 6, 2021, in Congress and leading up to that, there was a whole lot of noise in the political system about whether the vice president could refuse to accept electors and whether Congress could impose their own view on what state electors they should accept, and all those sorts of things. In retrospect, traditional constitutional election lawyers had it right all along in terms of the law. The correct person was elected president, but the political ramifications of that whole episode and Jan. 6 itself were just catastrophic for American politics and certainly transformative in many ways. The law held, the right guy was elected president, but antidemocratic elements were energized and we had a riot at the Capitol. A social movement has arisen in the country that makes millions of dollars based on stoking a view that elections in America are illegitimate. It’s an extreme view, and a minority view even within the Republican Party at that level of extremity, but it’s really been politically consequential over the last six years. I think we could be in an Act 2 of that, and that’s what I’m getting at here. Imagine that this was a decade ago and we’re coming into a Congress where the Democrats were going to take over the chamber and have about a 20-seat lead. You wouldn’t have imagined anything major to come out of any contest, any seat. There might have been a couple of contests and it might have been highly politicized, but they would have been handled in the regular order. Imagine right now if Democrats have that same margin of success. I think we have to assume there will be at least a few members of the House on the Republican side who will make a lot of hay around refusing their being seated, refusing to allow them to vote for the speaker, pressuring the clerk not to accept their certificates of election, those sorts of things. All of those would be illegitimate. The rules of the House are very clear about the clerk of the House accepting the properly given certificates of election for members of the House for the purpose of the roll-call vote at the beginning and electing the speaker. There is a very prescribed way of handling contests in the House of Representatives, and there is no legitimate, legal way for the current Congress to be the judge of those elections. Now, one of the things I think will stoke the political fires, though, is the investigations into any contest that any member would want to bring in the 119th Congress. There will be pressures in the current Congress for House Speaker Mike Johnson to highly politicize those investigations, use them as opportunities to shine a spotlight on California and other states that are not cooperating with the administration over noncitizens on the rolls, for instance. Or on states with high levels of mail voting. It’s going to unleash this political theater in hopes of blowing up regular procedures that are very clear, much more clear than the Electoral Count Act was in 2020, into something that looks like a rejection of this election. And the closer it is, the higher the stakes and the higher the temperature. Despite having serious concerns about what the Trump administration will do in the event Republicans lose control of Congress, you’re optimistic that the 2026 midterms will be resilient enough to withstand a Trump bashing. Why? It starts with the decentralized nature of election administration. When I’ve been asked about whether the Trump administration is going to be successful in overthrowing this election, I’ve generally responded with two words: Feckless—they’ve not been very good at what they’ve done; and capacity—there are just not enough lawyers left in the Department of Justice, or enough immigration agents, there’s not enough of anything to take over the American electoral system. If it’s a matter of mobilizing the federal government, there are more local election officials running elections than there’s the capacity of the Trump administration to go after enough of them. That’s decentralization. The second thing is that we continue to see evidence that the facts-based institutions of our electoral system continue to hold. That includes state and local election officials who are nothing if not careful about following the law. And there are the county boards of elections; we’ve seen general election boards stand by the canvassed results. There have been several states that have passed laws over the last few years reinforcing the notion that the certifications of elections at the local level are ministerial and that the proper place for conducting contests, if something is seen to be amiss, is in the court system, not in the election boards. Finally, I think we continue to see the courts holding to what the law is, holding to the precedents that have been established in the past about what do you do when someone contests the results of an election. There’s established law there, and trial courts have, on the whole, held, and when they haven’t, they’ve been admonished or overturned on appeal. It would be nice if we didn’t have to say, “Well, at the end of the day, we’re relying on the Alaska Supreme Court.” It would be much better if it didn’t have to get that far, but we do still have a robust and independent judiciary that has been willing to stand up to nonsense. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! If Democrats gain control of the House and Senate, this will be one of the first issues they will need to grapple with, and Frank Bowman, law professor and former federal and state prosecutor, tells Dahlia that Trump’s actions are definitely impeachable. However, Congress is likely to face obstacles that may just be insurmountable. States that have struggled to enforce strict abortion laws have found a new avenue to force their agenda, and it focuses on controlling speech. Mary Ziegler, law professor at the University of California, Davis, writes in Slate about a new frontier around reproductive rights that’s brewing for after the midterms. Dale Margolin Cecka, associate professor of law at Albany Law School, explains in Slate the statistics around child sexual abuse and how Ramaswamy turned Acton’s vulnerability into a campaign tactic.
