My State’s Democratic Candidate Has Zero Rizz. He Could Decide Control of the Senate. Good!
2026-10-29

My State’s Democratic Candidate Has Zero Rizz. He Could Decide Control of the Senate. Good!

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Less than half an hour into the first debate between the two candidates for New Hampshire’s open Senate seat, the moderator put his foot in his mouth. “Do you think that’s important, Chris Sununu?” George Epstein, of the Conway Daily Sun, tried to ask one candidate, before immediately course-correcting: “Excuse me—Chris Sununu is definitely the wrong name to use.” It was a mistake that said more about the race than any of the candidates’ answers. Yes, John Sununu, Republican, is running against Chris Pappas, Democrat. But Epstein’s error wasn’t an unfortunate mashup of the two candidates’ names: It was a reference to John’s younger—and significantly more popular—brother, Chris Sununu, also the state’s most recent former governor. (John Sununu has served on New Hampshire’s behalf in both the U.S. House and Senate, but he’d been out of office—and out of politics—for almost 20 years, until he joined the race this year.) If you’ve read anything about the New Hampshire Senate race, you might have heard that this is the one where the Republican is trying to ride his brother’s coattails to flipping a seat. This is reflected in his campaign’s perhaps oversimplified yard signs, the majority of which carry only two words “Sununu, Senator,” prompting plenty of residents to wonder if he’s deliberately skipping listing his first name. Otherwise, on paper, the New Hampshire Senate race has been a bit of a sleeper, despite the fact that it’s a competitive contest in an every-seat-counts struggle for control of Congress’ upper chamber. There are plenty of reasons for that: The September primaries were extremely boring compared to other Senate primaries that demanded national headlines. And unlike other primaries that seemed to point toward where the Democratic Party might be heading in 2028, Pappas’ win hardly felt like a bellwether. He did face a challenger from the left, but Karishma Manzur never put up numbers that made her a real threat, and Pappas beat her handily. Sununu did the same with his further-right challenger, Scott Brown, the former senator who had previously (and briefly) represented Massachusetts. That brought Pappas and Sununu to face off against one another in a Senate race that feels almost like it could be happening back in 2012, with classic establishment candidates from each party promising Granite Staters that they’re the ones who will have New Hampshire’s back in Washington. By many measures, it should be Pappas’ race. New Hampshire may be purple, but it’s reliably voted Democrat for president every year since 2000, and the delegation it sends to D.C. has been blue over the past several cycles too. Despite a bit of panic earlier this fall (“New Hampshire Democrats Sound an Alarm: We’re Losing” blared a New York Times headline in September), Pappas has pulled ahead in polling in recent weeks. But I have become particularly interested in New Hampshire’s Senate race, in large part because I live here—I moved a couple of weeks after Trump retook the presidency, soon enough that my town hall was still displaying the 2024 vote count, when 800 residents went for Kamala Harris and 792 went for Donald Trump. And I have come to think of this race not as a sleeper, but as the flip side of the coin to whatever happens with Abdul El-Sayed’s bid for Michigan’s seat or James Talarico’s impressive attempt to flip Texas. This is a race of establishment candidates in a moment when both the Republican establishment and the Democratic establishment are beleaguered. I wanted to find out what the establishment version of either political party even looks like these days, and whether the most boring Democrat in a midterms year of stars might have something to teach the rest of his party. I also needed to understand whether anyone actually votes a split ticket in our wildly polarized times. Given my own progressive tendencies, I came away surprised by how much close observation of the contest changed my mind. It’s not that this race feels like it’s happening specifically in 2012, it’s just that it has a retro feeling about it, particularly thanks to the candidates themselves. Jason Barabas, a government professor and director of the Rockefeller Center for Public Policy at Dartmouth College, put it succinctly: “You have the first open Senate race since 2010, but you have two incumbents squaring off.” Chris Pappas served in the House of Representatives for four terms before the Dems nominated him for Senate this year, and Sununu, a former congressman and senator, comes from the state’s biggest political dynasty. (It’s not just John Sununu’s brother Chris—the boys’ father, also John, also served as governor of the state, and as chief of staff for George H. W. Bush, with a memorable legacy.) “If his name wasn’t Sununu, I don’t think he’d be in the position he’s in,” Nicholas Germana, a government professor at Keene State University, told me. Germana knows what he’s talking about not only because he studies government (primarily German politics, not American ones), but because, like so many New Hampshirites, he serves in the state government himself. New Hampshire’s House of Representatives has 400 members (though apparently no one sits in seat No. 13), making it the second biggest legislative body in the country, just after the U.S. House, and one of the largest governing bodies in the world. This is particularly silly when you consider the small size of the state—with under 1.5 million residents, it’s split into just two congressional districts. But it’s also a huge source of pride and a cause of political engagement here. While reporting this story, multiple state reps reminded me that New Hampshire was the first state to declare independence from England (six months before the country did), and even if the state is losing its first-in-the-nation primary status (Iowa was always a caucus), being demoted from second to third (South Carolina and Nevada are moving up for Democrats) in this upcoming cycle, New Hampshire will still take its task extremely seriously. Is it people’s political involvement that has given the state its purple nature, with a push and pull back and forth between the parties? Not really. Independents outnumber registered Republicans and Democrats. For years now, the state government has been red—and currently, it’s a united Republican governorship, Senate, and House—while the state votes blue for president and sends a full blue delegation to D.C. Which means that people are splitting their tickets: red for the state, blue for the country. “That’s something we talk about all the time—marvel at, frankly—is how much ticket splitting there is,” Germana told me. That’s partly because you have a much higher likelihood of actually knowing your House representative, which might get you to vote across party lines. But it’s also because there’s some cultural state pride in “balancing things out,” even in these times. New Hampshire had one of the highest rates of ticket splitters in the 2022 midterms, Barabas told me, and this year, 13 percent of voters have already declared a preference for splitting their ticket. (The likely way this will lean is to send Pappas to Washington while the current governor, Kelly Ayotte, stays in power.) But the times are making it harder and harder to do that. These midterms are seeing an active effort from Democrats across the state as they respond to some of the more extreme moves made by the united Republican state government. “Republicans in the state had been able to maintain a brand identity that is different than the national Republican Party—rooted in that traditional view of what a Yankee Republican used to be,” Germana told me. But as the Republican agenda has lurched right, that distance has evaporated. In recent sessions, the GOP here has passed culture-war legislation like banning trans athletes from sports. They’ve also taken more aggressive steps to throttle public schools, including voting in a school voucher program that has activated plenty of independent voters. These days, there’s “no daylight between the Republican brand in the state and the Republican Party nationally,” Germana told me, though it’s worth keeping in mind that Germana is not just an observer—he’s technically a member of the opposition party. All of this makes Sununu’s bid to flip a Senate seat a tough effort, and that’s before you take into account his weaknesses as a candidate. While Sununu says he’s not at all trying to fool anyone into thinking he’s Chris, multiple people told me they only realized which brother was running when the primary came around in September. (Their reaction was not positive—Chris had a lot of cross-party appeal thanks to being “a phenomenal retail politician,” Germana said, which John lacks.) John Sununu’s been out of politics since he lost to Jeanne Shaheen in 2008, and it’s Shaheen he’s looking to replace now. While his absence from politics affords him a certain distance from Trump, when he lost his Senate seat, Sununu resorted to a classic move for the establishment and politicians of both parties: He went into lobbying, something the Pappas campaign has made hay of in both ads and debates. John Sununu was a Never-Trump Republican who supported John Kasich in 2016 and Nikki Haley in 2024. And yet, he finds himself in a difficult position this race, as D.C. Republicans personally recruited him to run, after failing to get his brother to do so. It feels a bit like he’s trying to run this race as if he could be New Hampshire’s very own Susan Collins, a Republican, sure, but a reasonable one who would hold his party to account. The problem is, Donald Trump endorsed him during his primary race, and here, like everywhere, Trump’s popularity is plummeting. Recent polling found that the number of Granite Staters who approve of the president was the lowest ever—just 19 percent said they approved of Trump in September of this year (compared to the 41 percent who approved of Trump in 2018, during his first in-office midterms). People are mad about tariffs and the Iran war just like they are everywhere—but New Hampshirites get to be extra mad because we share a border with Canada and are heading into winter, where fuel costs will matter more. All in all, Collins and Sununu seem to be the last individuals trying to claim that it’s possible to be something of a New England Republican—conservative on fiscal issues while being socially liberal—as Trump’s presence and his overwhelming grip on the Republican Party make the viability of that brand more and more difficult. But even Collins is struggling with this message this cycle, and Sununu is not campaigning in the relentless way she is in neighboring Maine. “Sununu is running a low-key campaign with almost no public rallies, few appearances from Republican surrogates, and limited engagement with national media,” the Dispatch reported recently, and he dropped out of what was supposed to be the final debate. I myself tried to engage with his campaign in multiple ways—attempting to RSVP to a meet-and-greet I was told was closed to the press, emailing the campaign, and even eventually texting his campaign spokesman—all to no avail. The main action seems to be in the form of the millions of dollars pouring into the state, where the GOP does have a clear advantage. (In early October, Republicans had spent $39 million compared to Dems’ $23 million, though both campaigns said they have about $14 million on hand for the final few weeks.) The latest ad features Sununu trying to distance himself from Trump, repeating his line that he’s “never met the man.” Pappas is doing the exact opposite of Sununu. He’s been crisscrossing the state (well, driving up and down—you can’t really drive across in New Hampshire) with bigwig Democrats who are happy to add a stop here in anticipation of 2028. Raphael Warnock, Pete Buttigieg, and Gavin Newsom all came through, and late this month, Pappas was joined by Kamala Harris herself. Which is part of what has gotten Pappas into trouble with members of the left flank of his party: Donald Trump may be toxic, but the Democrats are also dealing with their own intra-party feud. Manzur, his primary challenger, took aim at Pappas’ establishment connections and big money—her signs said “Powered by People, Not Billionaires.” And like Abdul El-Sayed, she tried to make her weak fundraising (in the low hundreds of thousands, not millions) an asset rather than a liability. It didn’t work—Pappas beat her by a ratio of just about 2-to-1—but it doesn’t mean her messaging didn’t land somewhere. I spoke with a group of students at Colby-Sawyer College in New London who were all required by a class to attend a political event on campus, and when I asked what they thought of Pappas, they all groaned and told me he was a “corporate Dem”—basically as bad as Sununu, who they didn’t want to vote for either, mind you. Several were still deciding whether they’d bother to change their state registration to vote here this fall (one knew she wouldn’t because she was “excited to vote against Susan Collins”). College students will be college students, but they weren’t the only ones I heard lamenting that Pappas might not have what it takes. An AARP poll that found Pappas has the edge also showed that both candidates have underwater favorability ratings. And I have to admit, for a long time, I felt the same way. On primary day, I was personally conflicted about who I’d vote for. (I’m a registered Democrat.) Ultimately, I watched about 30 minutes of the debate between Pappas and Manzur, and decided that Manzur wouldn’t have a chance of making it out of our purple state. I pulled the ballot for Pappas, but really it was a vote for pragmatism, driven by concerns over the ultimate balance of power in the Senate. Since then, though, I’ve been following him much more closely, and I’ve come to feel differently about my pragmatism vote. That’s largely thanks to the actual campaign that Pappas has knit together—one that has closed the initial gap he faced and looks solid going into November. At one stop I watched him tour a small manufacturing plant, nothing bright or flashy, but Pappas was the picture of politeness, gamely asking questions about how they’d gotten a competitive edge in the niche grips they made. The visit demonstrated what a layup Dems are getting this time around: Tariffs causing this small business problems—check. The war in Iran increasing expenses—check. Healthcare premiums going up—check. Pappas, whose family has long owned the Puritan, an event and conference space with a restaurant that claims to have invented chicken fingers, was able to engage in the minutiae of their business, from a place of true empathy. An on-floor engineer remarked that they sell their scrap metal, and the price has skyrocketed—something they take as a sure indication that the economy is going down the tubes. “Just like when peanut butter sales go up,” the chicken-finger heir nodded solemnly. At that stop, I spoke with an employee who told me she’s never been into politics herself, but that she’s estranged from both her parents on account of Trump. She stopped talking with her mother the day Roe v. Wade was overturned, she said—she couldn’t take it anymore, and she wants to set an example for her son about how people should behave. Her story was specific, but the distaste for Trump was second only to “affordability” on the trail, and luckily for Democrats this cycle, the two are intertwined now. You can’t go to a campaign event in New Hampshire without running into at least a few state reps (or candidates for state rep), and many of these people told me that they’re hearing from a lot of independents who are fed up with Trump and prices and are voting blue, and even a few registered Republicans here and there. Many offered praise for Pappas, who is skilled at listening to all sides of an issue and takes his representation of a mixed district seriously, they said—he’s made it easy for right-leaning folks to switch over. “He’s a guy who knows how to win a purple district,” Germana had told me, and he’s calibrated his public persona and strategy around that. One thing that’s stood out to me is the way Pappas seems to actually decenter himself in these settings, which you don’t need me to tell you is unusual for a politician. If elected, he will be the first openly gay senator in American history. This is not something he ever brings up himself, which, sure, maybe is a nod to the fact that identity politics is truly dead, but I think is more of a representation of his style as a legislator. Also: You’d think the man whose family claims they invented chicken fingers, of all things—what is more American and universally beloved than a chicken finger—would toot that at least a little bit on the campaign trail. But it’s all pretty straight “as a family business owner, I understand the struggle” type stuff. (On a recent visit to the Puritan Backroom, I was struck by two things: how quintessentially New Hampshire it was, which I’ve realized is my code for “caters to Trump voters,” and how delightfully large their Puritan Backroom–branded sauces are—Pappas should be running on his record of ranch portion sizes.) He’s a politician who has done things methodically, which in this day and age means mainly that he paid his dues in both the New Hampshire House and the U.S. House, along with a stint on the state’s executive council, before he went for a bigger office. In his mid-40s, he seems young for the Senate, but that’s just because he has a boyish look and our standards for how old lawmakers should reasonably be are broken. He has a stick-to-the-message-ness that can make him seem rehearsed—or can be interpreted as discipline from someone who is taking his race dead seriously. At a canvass kickoff event on Oct. 17, the one where Gavin Newsom joined the New Hampshire delegates running for office, I heard more practical praise for Pappas. A state House rep who described herself as far more progressive than he is explained why she voted for him in the primary over Manzur: Pappas pledged to endorse his opponent if he was bested, and Manzur did not. This was not the moment for such games, the rep had concluded. There was a couple who had come up from Massachusetts to canvass who constituted the first real Chris Pappas fans I met in the wild (“I’ve always liked him,” the woman told me, with an almost mothering quality). OK, fine, it wasn’t really the wild—these were people who were giving up their Saturday to knock on doors for Democrats, and would most likely end up at a “No Kings” rally later in the afternoon. Just two days before, the Pappas campaign had announced a “Country Before Party” steering committee of more than 50 Republicans and independents who were endorsing Pappas for Senate. The big coup on that list was Don Bolduc, the Republican who has been defeated for that exact Senate seat in the previous cycle. “I think that one was kind of surprising, to tell you the truth, and that can be quite powerful,” Barabas, the Dartmouth professor, told me. At the canvass launch, Pappas attributed Bolduc’s endorsement to his military experience, and his recognition of the nightmare the military is currently facing, led by Pete Hegseth into the Iran war. It set Pappas up perfectly for his line that “everyone is welcome on this team: progressives, moderates, independents, Republicans.” It was this plain appeal that made me start to realize what feels good—almost calming—about Chris Pappas’ campaign. There was something fundamentally decent going on here—a representative who truly seemed to understand that the job of an elected official is to represent all of his constituents, not just the ones who agree with him. One of the most memorable pieces of political journalism I’ve read this midterms cycle was a New York Times Magazine feature from Robert Draper that reported out, via interviews with legislators in both national houses, why Congress is so broken. It turned the entire idea of the midterms on its head for me: “On Nov. 3, American voters will decide which party controls the House and the Senate for the remainder of Trump’s presidency. In theory, at least, the outcome of their decision should be enormously consequential.” In practice, though, Draper is not sure how much any of it really matters, as he goes on to outline exactly how much power the legislative branch has ceded during the mind-bending times of President Donald Trump. There are many reasons for that, including Trump’s astonishing willingness to break norms (see: setting tariffs, something Congress is supposed to have control over). But Trump is also the avatar for how our broader politics have broken over the past decade-plus. Members from both parties explained that the quality of those who choose to join the government has declined, by which they mean that they’re all narcissists now. “What you see now are too many people who come in viewing Congress as their green screen,” David Joyce, a Republican from Ohio, told Draper. “And they’re going to make a name for themselves and don’t give a damn about anything else.” I’m not trying to suggest that the old Congress was all sunshine and daisies. Bogged down by our resolutely two-party system, we Americans can end up fetishizing Washington bipartisanship (that is ultimately out of step with what most regular people want—there’s a reason both sides are turning on the establishment this cycle). But even compromise is getting a bad name these days. Work too hard to actually get things done, and you might end up primaried by an inexperienced challenger like Manzur who may have plenty of good ideas, but no ability to even make it to D.C. to try them. It’s not that Congress should be populated by only normies, but it’s good to have a solid bloc in the mix. They aren’t exciting, but they’re a decent bulwark against the kind of “exciting” we’ve been living through, with brief interruptions, for a decade now. In the absence of a Congress that passes legislation, we have a Congress full of grandstanders. Forgive me for my Pollyannaishness, but as I’ve tried to figure out what is happening in my own state, why this tight race isn’t getting as much attention as I think it should, I came to a pretty simple conclusion. Chris Pappas is running to legislate. In 2026, that’s pretty boring. But I found myself convinced by his pitch—that he is actually going to Washington to attempt to solve our problems, even if it means reaching across the aisle or compromising in favor of getting something, anything, done. He won’t do it with a lot of fanfare, and he certainly won’t do it in a tight black V-neck. But he’ll try. Let’s just hope it’s still possible. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. How to Lose Your Party a Senate Seat: Alaska Edition
2026-10-28

How to Lose Your Party a Senate Seat: Alaska Edition

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Next week’s U.S. Senate election in Alaska could come down to one letter. Under the state’s unique balloting system, three candidates are running to unseat Sen. Dan S. Sullivan: Democratic former Rep. Mary Peltola, a bandana-wearing gadfly named Gerald Heikes, and … another Dan Sullivan. That other Dan Sullivan, whose middle initial is J, is a retired teacher who has previously voted Democrat, so his presence in the race quickly attracted suspicion. On Wednesday, the New York Times officially linked Dan J. Sullivan’s candidacy to Democrats, reporting that Peltola aides helped recruit him in a bid to siphon votes from the incumbent Republican. The subterfuge appears to be a blatant case of Democrats stooping to dirty tricks in a competitive race that will help decide which party controls the Senate. Yet as seamy as those tactics may be, they still don’t justify the Trump administration’s attempts to use the power of the federal government to help the original Dan Sullivan (the one who’s already a Republican senator, that is) win reelection. So like a salmon swimming up an icy Alaskan stream, let’s dive in. Last year, Peltola allies started looking for Alaskans named Dan Sullivan who might be open to running for Senate. The reported scheme, referred to internally as “the ballot composition project,” involved a Democratic operative named Jerid Kurtz clandestinely meeting with potential contenders. But Kurtz’s skullduggery appears to have paid off. In the small fishing village of Petersburg, he found Dan J. Sullivan, who apparently agreed to run if it would help Peltola win. (Dan J. Sullivan has previously denied any associations with the Peltola campaign, which in turn continues to deny any involvement with his candidacy.) Some of the Times’ information came from Elisa Rios, Peltola’s former campaign manager, who spoke to the paper after she was fired in August. Rios first claimed that Peltola knew about the gimmick before trying to retract her comments. Yet the Times also uncovered other evidence that the recruitment plot was real, including text exchanges going back months. Could having another Dan Sullivan on the ballot really help Peltola win? The theory goes like this: Confused voters intending to throw their support behind the Republican Dan Sullivan (who has a chance of beating Peltola) may accidentally vote for Dan J. Sullivan (who has no chance at all). Trump’s Justice Department cited that possibility in a lawsuit it filed last week at the president’s behest that aimed to block Alaska election officials from certifying a Peltola victory. Sounds like Democrats got caught in a pretty ugly attempt to confuse voters. Does that mean the administration’s suit has merit? Although the DOJ sued before the Times’ story broke, there are a few reasons to doubt that the Peltola campaign’s gambit merits federal intervention. For one thing, Alaska Republicans have engaged in similar hijinks. In 2024, the state party helped keep a federal inmate who had never been to Alaska on the U.S. House ballot in a bid to undermine Peltola, who ended up losing. The DOJ’s case also rests on some questionable assumptions. The feds framed the suit as a bid to “protect the integrity of the electoral process,” but the Constitution gives states the power to set election rules, and the Alaska Supreme Court decided months ago that the other Sullivan could stay on the ballot. The ballots also clearly label Sen. Sullivan as an incumbent Republican, reducing the chances of confusion. Attorney General Todd Blanche, a staunch Trump toady, has nonetheless argued that a narrow defeat for Sen. Sullivan could constitute evidence that voter confusion tipped the race, hence the preemptive lawsuit. “There are a number of scenarios where it could be obvious that a voter was confused by the way they wrote down candidates,” Blanche told CNN on Sunday. Except the U.S. guarantees the right to a secret ballot, which means it’s pretty much impossible to tell what someone was thinking when they bubbled in their preferred candidates. Rather than evidence of confusion, a narrow Sullivan defeat could just mean that Alaskans narrowly preferred Peltola as their next senator. Assuming a judge rejects the DOJ suit, how might Alaska voters respond to all this? One possibility is with a shrug. Without minimizing what the Peltola campaign reportedly did, there’s a difference between political trickery and lawbreaking election interference, and the Times story doesn’t assert that Peltola’s allies crossed that line. (Not that that’s stopped the president from claiming otherwise: On Wednesday, Trump cited the Times story in a Truth Social post arguing that Peltola “shouldn’t be allowed to run, and neither should the FAKE Dan Sullivan!”) Many voters may also have long assumed that the other Sullivan was a Democratic plant. Since June, when Alaska’s top court ruled he could stay on the ballot, most polls have shown Peltola narrowly winning. On the other hand, voters may resent the Peltola campaign’s attempt to confuse them, which is both underhanded and more than a little condescending. In what’s projected to be a close race in a state with fewer than 1 million inhabitants, turning off even a few voters could be a big problem—and a really stupid way to blow a Senate seat. Let’s turn our attention to my colleagues’ ideas about what to read and (not) watch this evening: A great book, a bad movie: Kazuo Ishiguro’s novel Klara and the Sun is strange and beautiful—but it might also be unfilmable. Laura Miller explains why Taika Waititi’s adaptation is shaping up to be a legendary box-office bomb. A reconsideration of Meta: It’s been five years since Facebook rebranded itself as Meta as part of Mark Zuckerberg’s pivot to the metaverse. That dream may be dead, but Meta lives on. Chris Stokel-Walker argues that the rebrand was the right decision—but made for the wrong reason. A new birth control option: Copper IUDs are a long-acting and nonhormonal form of birth control, but they do have some drawbacks. Luckily, if you hate your current birth control, Mia Armstrong-López is here to tell you about a new option that might be a better fit. The case for boring men: Dating in the modern era is a minefield, requiring you to deftly navigate the apps, tune out bad TikTok advice, and contend with the threat of ghosting. It’s not for the faint of heart. In a culture that pushes the promise of an exciting whirlwind romance, Alley Horn makes the case that boring men are a better bet. As a somewhat boring guy myself, I can’t say I disagree! See you back here tomorrow. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here

I Wanted to Understand What Israelis Think of This Election. What I Learned Was Chilling.
2026-10-28

I Wanted to Understand What Israelis Think of This Election. What I Learned Was Chilling.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. ISRAEL—In Tel Aviv, one of Israel’s more liberal hubs, Ben Friedhaim sits with his young daughter in HaKnesset Square as waves gently lap onto the nearby beach just days before the election. Even during Shabbat, the boardwalk here is full of families basking in the sunshine, electric scooters whizzing past, and bars blasting American hits. Ice cream melts down his daughter’s hand as he tells me about his family’s recent return to the country. He came to vote. Friedhaim, who now lives in Prague, is one of more than an estimated 80,000 Israelis who returned to the country this week, having left after Oct. 7, when, he says, things got “scary.” Without the option of absentee voting in most cases, this cohort signals how important this election is. For Friedhaim, he wants to make Israel feel safe for his daughter to grow up here; this safety, in his mind, is provided by the Israeli military. “I think the army is very important when she turns 18,” he said about his toddler. The military is crucial to many here, given the country’s embattled status. “It is an important election, but Israel is always going to be at war,” he said. “There is no diplomatic solution.” Indeed, even as the election seems so critical that people like Friedhaim are returning home to vote in it, there’s a certain stasis to what’s to come. A largely right-wing Zionist vision appears entrenched across Israel’s political bench, and most candidates hoping to unseat Benjamin Netanyahu in this election offer little clarity on the future facing Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank, or the broader region. The field has been crowded: 36 parties initially in the running. The main challengers to Netanyahu include Gadi Eisenkot of the Yashar party, the centrist former army chief of staff who opposes a two-state solution and backs some settlements in the West Bank. Former Defense Minister Avigdor Lieberman, of the right-wing Yisrael Beitenu party, is also in the running, and previously said the two-state solution is dead. And there’s Naftali Bennett, a nationalist businessman who rejects Palestinian self-determination—that’s the candidate Friedhaim is voting for because “he comes from a right-wing background.” There’s also the country’s left-leaning Democrats party, fronted by Yair Golan, who is more open to ending wars and reaching a two-state solution, though he believes that it’s not immediately realistic. Eisenkot—who also lost a son in Gaza—is essentially fronting this coalition bloc, hoping to oust Netanyahu. Then there are a handful of ultra-far-right figures, Orthodox parties, and the country’s Arab parties, none of which had a chance to win the majority outright. Just after 10 local time here on election night, the first exit polls were published: Many showed Eisenkot’s coalition initially holding a narrow lead over Netanyahu’s bloc, but no clear majority. By morning the results had already shifted toward Netanyahu, casting uncertainty over what comes next. Regardless of who wins the count, these factions will still have to work together to form a government and determine the next leader. Notably, it was a historic night for the Arab parties—but even now, it’s too early to tell what the next government will look like. The backdrop to this election has been one of tension, dissatisfaction, and fear. Even if a new coalition ushers in top-level change, it might do little to resolve the much deeper fractures beneath it. “Things have become very polarized here,” Friedhaim had said that Saturday before the election. “They’re turning people against each other.” In the time I spent in Israel in the week leading up to the vote, the election mood became visible, as did furious sentiment over what’s at stake. Looking out across a sea of Israeli flags during a massive anti-Netanyahu demonstration, one bystander bluntly says: “This country has been on the verge of civil war for years.” Still, this time feels different from past elections. The country is angry: some at Netanyahu’s cultish leadership, which they feel is running Israel into the ground; others convinced he’s the only one who can steer the country amid an increasingly hostile region. And yet, the options at the ballot box do little to address what lies ahead for Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank, for Syrians living in the embattled Golan Heights, or for the residents of southern Lebanon who have been forced from their homes by yet another illegal Israeli expansion. Despite the differences in opinion on domestic policy, there is a pervasive sense that the Israeli identity is under threat and that its enemies must be defeated at all costs. Which means that real change in Israel—and the idea of peace—has been the hardest to envision. Hours after I spoke with Friedhaim, I witnessed a tense exchange break out among strangers at a bar discussing the need to protect Israel’s borders. They were debating the unnecessary loss of life—not of civilians from neighboring countries, but of the Israeli soldiers stationed in those occupied territories—with one person arguing that sacrifices were necessary. That same day, crowds carrying homemade signs marched to Hostage Square (“change is going to come,” said one), with attendees largely backing one of the opposition candidates listed above. Rallying for an inquiry into Oct. 7, the crowd suddenly erupted as Eisenkot gleefully passed supporters, shaking hands with potential voters eager to see a new direction for the country. Dani Schrire and his wife work in academia and had traveled from Jerusalem just to attend the Tel Aviv demonstration. “The division is not between Israelis and Palestinians,” Schrire said, though not everyone would agree. “It’s crazy fanatics who are Israeli and Palestinian on the one hand—and those who have some kind of vision of mutual acceptance, whether it’s a two-state, one-state, doesn’t matter.” The treatment of the Palestinians shapes the couple’s vote as well, though it wasn’t necessarily the only factor. As for their thoughts on Netanyahu after his latest clampdown in the West Bank, Schrire said: “I think the guy is a total nut.” Palestinians in the occupied territories—some 5.6 million people—cannot vote in Israel’s election. Among them is hostel owner Issa Nazzal, who sits in his office in the picturesque Palestinian town of Ramallah in the occupied West Bank, looking out the window at the encroaching settlement on the hilltop. For many Palestinians here, the focus hinges on surviving day by day under a terrifying occupation that has ramped up its violence in the run-up to the election. In recent days, the situation has reached a breaking point—violent settlers attack anyone in their way, including Palestinians and international journalists. People are particularly despondent about what plays out beyond the 30-foot concrete barrier surrounding the territory in the Israeli election. “I didn’t see anything that makes me feel they can change,” Nazzal said. Within Israel, change means different things depending on whom you ask. Not far from Israel’s seat of government, the Knesset, in Jerusalem, last-minute canvassing was prominent. A group of teenagers, not old enough to vote, pass flyers in support of Bezalel Smotrich, a far-right ultranationalist politician who models himself on policies including the total destruction of Gaza. Military fatigues are worn like a badge of honor in Jerusalem’s historic center, where young recruits sling automatic weapons over their shoulders. Trumplike “Make Israel Great Again” caps are flogged in tourist shops across the Old City, one of the world’s oldest continuously inhabited cities. Standing outside the family-run carpet business, Yal and his father—who didn’t share their last names—perch near a table featuring a poster of Netanyahu alongside Donald Trump, framed by fighter jets and Israeli flags. There’s no question about where Yal stands: “The Arabs here are the problem,” he says. “Israel is a small country, and everyone wants to kill Israelis.” Netanyahu’s party appeared to have been prepping the ground for claims of election fraud, and Israelis across the political spectrum raised concerns in passing about whether the country will accept whatever results emerge. At the time of writing, the mood in Jerusalem was calm, but Netanyahu tried to extend voting hours, which the elections committee rejected. It’s hard to ignore what Yal had told me days before: “I don’t want to think what’s going to happen [if Netanyahu doesn’t win].” Propaganda has undoubtedly framed this election, with the country’s most popular TV channels portraying contrasting polls over who will win. Few here appear to see—or want to see—the devastating images that continue to emerge from places like Gaza, footage that international audiences are more exposed to. Instead, campaigning hinges on the threats facing Israel, and the urgent need for more secure borders—even if that means expanding into other countries. More than 850 polling stations were set up in occupied outposts for Israeli soldiers currently serving in Palestine, Syria, and Lebanon, up from 710 in 2022. The highway heading north along the country’s Mediterranean coastline has been littered with campaign billboards in a recognizable Israeli blue for weeks. There’s also an image of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani alongside Iran’s supreme leader, the chief of Hezbollah, and Turkey President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan. It’s an illustration of the alleged enemy, provided by Netanyahu’s Likud party. The shape of the new government could take weeks to sort out. Because of Israel’s proportional representation system, no party has ever won 61 seats outright to achieve a Knesset majority, meaning that the election will be followed by potentially long, drawn-out negotiations. Israel’s election committee tried to bar the country’s Arab parties from running, but the move was overturned. They might play a decisive role in who secures the Knesset—though few Israeli politicians in the opposition bloc currently appear open to working with the Arab parties to secure a majority government. Official election results, however, won’t be published until Nov. 4. In the oceanside city of Haifa, streets are quieter than in Israel’s major cities, but the mood in one of the Arab parties’ campaign offices was anything but. Some people here cling on to hope that Israeli politicians will have to pay attention to the Arab parties this time around. Historically, the Arab-led parties have only ever secured a record high of 15 seats, and only once did they join the governing coalition (in 2021). Then and now, they face considerable right-wing pushback. Before the election, I met with political activist Reem Hazzan as she was working on last-ditch canvassing for a party called Hadash. Here, she’s also running the campaign for the Joint List, an alliance of Arab parties that could have some determining influence in the overall election outcome. I ask her what makes this election different from others. Her answer is immediate: “The necessity to get rid of Netanyahu, Smotrich, and [far-right leader Itamar] Ben-Gvir.” She added: “If we do not get rid of them, apartheid will continue and be deepened, ethnic cleansing and Jewish terrorism in the West Bank will continue. The very slow genocide in Gaza will continue.” Hazzan worries that even of the Arabs eligible to vote, not everyone plans to: “A lot of people are worried that whatever we do, we will not be able to change anything; we will not be able to actually influence. “I wish people were much angrier, really, and much more furious about the situation, about what they are willing to sacrifice in order to change the situation,” she adds. “But I must also say that I understand why. We have been struggling for decades … and things are only getting worse.” The fear is different among this community, more visceral. Arabs in Israel are disproportionately affected by violent crime, and accounts of Muslims being targeted just for wearing hijabs or speaking Arabic are rife. “I’m afraid, regardless of these elections,” says Sally Abed, a Palestinian City Council member in Haifa. “I became a mother two years ago. I gave birth during a very active war. I am very worried as a woman, as a Palestinian, as a mother. … I really feel unsafe.” After spending a week in Israel—and witnessing a vote that seems as if it will not provide a definitive outcome and instead require more negotiation for a government to even form—I worried most about people like Abed. Israelis will argue over who actually takes charge in the weeks ahead. Palestinians will continue to live through the consequences. The fissures here run dangerously deep, and massive questions remain over whether the next government can pull people together—or push them even further apart. BattMan aims to turbocharge US batteries without robbin' critical minerals
2026-10-29

BattMan aims to turbocharge US batteries without robbin' critical minerals

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
DoE-backed consortium hoping to scale, says orgs can apply to become, literally, part of BattMan America's national laboratories are leading a consortium to move  advanced battery storage technologies from the lab into domestic manufacturing. Dubbed the Battery Manufacturing Accelerator, or BattMan (no, really), the initiative aims to bring together a bunch of national laboratories, universities, and companies to overcome barriers in scaling up new battery technologies. Novel battery storage solutions are needed for applications in the electric grid, datacenters, and electric vehicles, as electrification gradually extends across industry. Batteries have a far lower energy density than fuels such as petroleum, so the hunt for better materials never stops. Researchers also want to improve on the safety of flammable lithium-ion cells, cut production costs, speed up charging, and reduce reliance on scarce raw materials. "The advances we make today in battery manufacturing technology will determine America's energy security and economic competitiveness for decades to come,” said Audrey Robertson, assistant secretary of Energy at the Department of Energy (DoE), which is overseeing the program. Researchers continue to develop new battery materials and chemistries, yet many promising technologies face obstacles on the way from the laboratory to commercial production. According to the DoE, BattMan will tackle that problem by combining super intelligence - known outside of the Trump administration as AI - with advanced manufacturing into a coordinated, data-driven innovation ecosystem. Pacific Northwest National Laboratory leads the consortium, along with Oak Ridge National Laboratory, Argonne National Laboratory and National Laboratory of the Rockies. Academic partners include the University of Washington, Binghamton University, University of Alabama, Arizona State University, and University of Michigan-Dearborn. The accelerator is also building ties with battery and materials manufacturers, and hopes to sidestep critical minerals supply problems by finding battery materials among more abundant, domestically sourced elements. ORNL is contributing its Battery Manufacturing Facility (BMF) R&D center and plans a smart test bed with advanced sensing, real-time monitoring, and data analysis to trial both wet and dry electrode manufacturing. BattMan will also introduce autonomous pilot lines that combine sensing, process monitoring and intelligent control systems to improve product quality and reduce variability in the manufacturing process. The goal is an AI-based manufacturing platform that spots production irregularities as they occur and adjusts parameters on its own to prevent defects. “The BattMan Accelerator is exactly the kind of coordinated national effort that turns scientific breakthroughs into economic and energy security advantages,” claimed distinguished R&D staff member Chins Chinnasamy, the consortium lead for ORNL. GSA left sensitive Google Drive files open to staff, contractors
2026-10-29

GSA left sensitive Google Drive files open to staff, contractors

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Auditors have flagged similar security failures since 2014 A major US federal agency has repeatedly left sensitive information in its Google Drive accessible to employees and contractors who had no legitimate business need to see it, and auditors aren’t happy. The General Services Administration (GSA), which handles contracting, procurement, property management, and other elements of basic operations for federal agencies, has faced repeated findings of inadequate protection for controlled unclassified information (CUI) since at least 2014, according to a report from the agency’s Office of Inspector General. Again. Controlled unclassified information, in the US federal government's parlance, is information that isn't classified but requires protection or restrictions on its distribution under applicable laws, regulations, or government-wide policies. Access is generally limited to individuals with a legitimate government need to know. According to the OIG report, that can include things like physical security info for government buildings, personally identifiable information (PII) of government employees, and proprietary business information, all of which the GSA has left exposed. According to the report published this week, employees and contractors have approximately 2.5 million documents stored in GSA's Google Drive. “During a limited search of GSA’s Google Drive, we found 116 files containing CUI that were improperly shared with all GSA users,” the report notes. That’s a limited search, mind you, and the OIG has plenty of reason to believe there’s more than just that sitting open for anyone with access to the GSA’s Google Drive instance to sneak a look at. The OIG said that GSA is failing to properly manage the third-party tool it has in place to prevent data loss and detect CUI exposures, with annual reviews of search terms incomplete or undocumented, many relevant search terms not implemented, and a failure to enable an available optical character recognition feature that could help detect CUI in scanned documents. Additionally, the GSA was apparently “unaware of the tool’s file size limitation of 15 megabytes,” meaning that even if the GSA was properly using that tool, some large files would still be skipped. “We also found that GSA lacks: (1) an approval process for properly sharing sensitive information and (2) a standard naming convention that could more effectively prevent exposures of CUI in GSA’s Google Drive,” the OIG added. As for what was exposed … it’s not great. According to the audit, site plans for buildings in Washington, DC were part of the improperly access-controlled files, with data that includes entrances and screening techniques, as well as the location of sensitive areas, like gun lockers. A progressive collapse analysis that "describes the building’s collapse vulnerabilities, defense vulnerabilities, and critical failure locations” was also exposed. PII of government individuals was also exposed, including social security numbers, addresses, dates of birth, and contact information. Proprietary business information was also exposed, “including proprietary research and vendor proposals with technical and pricing information.” This might not have been publicly accessible, but it’s still a shocking lapse of data control, especially given that contractors with access to the GSA’s Google Drive could potentially view proprietary information belonging to competing vendors, giving them an unfair advantage if they used it. To make matters worse, this isn’t the first time the GSA has been dinged by its own inspector general for such lousy data protection practices. According to the report, it was 2014 when the OIG first spotted such bad habits. “In August 2014, we first reported that GSA did not protect sensitive information on child care centers, courthouses, water sources, building automation systems, and security and fire alarm systems in its Google cloud computing environment,” the OIG noted. “We issued additional reports citing concerns regarding GSA’s ability to protect sensitive information in its Google environment between 2015 and 2017.” Auditors flagged another set of improperly shared Google Drive files in April 2025. While the GSA’s chief information officer agreed with the auditors’ seven recommendations to fix the problems, the report doesn't establish whether the corrective work has been completed. We reached out to the GSA and the OIG’s office for an update, but didn’t hear back from either. ® DoE-backed consortium hoping to scale, says orgs can apply to become, literally, part of BattMan Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: HPE Alletra MP X10000 cache offload delivers up to 20× faster time to first token in testing validated by Kamiwaza. Apparently, remembering things beats calculating them again. Oracle wants to bill you for every AI task, not just every seat
2026-10-29

Oracle wants to bill you for every AI task, not just every seat

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 Oracle expects to earn more from existing application customers as it embeds AI in its enterprise software suite. Speaking at Big Red's investor day on Wednesday, Chris Leone, executive veep of application development, said Oracle will layer usage-based revenue on top of its subscription model. “The more work that gets done by AI in our system, the more revenue we will see because they're consuming AI units as that work is getting done. We don't have to add more seats in order for us to now generate more revenue,” he said. Earlier this month, Oracle revealed its Fusion Claw runtime, designed to help customers control AI agents that automate business processes. It targets ERP, finance, and supply chain management in cloud-based Fusion applications running on Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (OCI). Customers will pay for more work done inside Oracle applications, and Leone said Fusion Claw will also automate work done outside of Fusion. The promise of more revenue for Oracle, and higher bills for customers, came as the company sought to reassure investors about its spending, borrowing and revenue as it chases a share of the AI market. Oracle is funding a datacenter spending spree with negative cashflow and borrowing. OCI is set to host large language model builders, notably OpenAI,  alongside its own customers' workloads. Oracle spent $55.7 billion on datacenters and cloud infrastructure in fiscal year 2026, which ended in May, and plans to spend between $90 billion and $95 billion in fiscal 2027. That will require more debt. In 2027, Oracle hopes to raise around $40 billion in debt and equity, on top of the $18 billion in debt it raised in September 2025. In a notice to investors this week, Oracle said it expects fiscal 2030 revenue of $225 billion, a compound annual growth rate of 35 percent from 2026. It expects positive cash flow by the second half of fiscal 2028, with capital spending falling the following financial year. Investors reacted positively. Oracle co-CEO Mike Sicilia also told shareholders the company will use AI to help implement its software, a plan he called as important as putting AI in the software itself. He argued AI will cut implementation time, bringing existing and new customers to OCI faster and boosting revenue. "On-premises customers can now move far more easily to the cloud by leveraging the AI tool sets to automate migrations and decrease the cost and time to implement. They don't have to be Oracle customers; they can be competitive customers." Sicilia said enterprise application software users' reluctance to change had been a major "headwind" for Oracle, one he hopes AI will ease. "Our large remaining base of on-premises customers are going to start to adopt this technology quite quickly. When we move customers from on-premises to cloud, we see a multiple increase in revenue," he said. ® DoE-backed consortium hoping to scale, says orgs can apply to become, literally, part of BattMan Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: HPE Alletra MP X10000 cache offload delivers up to 20× faster time to first token in testing validated by Kamiwaza. www.theregister.com - Articles
Built to bend: Postgres’s inventor and a steward on what AI really needs
2026-10-29

Built to bend: Postgres’s inventor and a steward on what AI really needs

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Michael Stonebraker introduced Postgres® at Berkeley in 1986. Forty years later, the database he invented is a leading choice for a generation of AI projects, and he is among the sharpest skeptics of how those projects are being built. Bruce Momjian is cofounder and core team member of the PostgreSQL Global Development Group, and he works at EnterpriseDB (EDB). In upcoming episodes of AI & Data Horizons by EnterpriseDB (EDB), the Turing Award winner and MIT professor and the Postgres community advocate both make the case that the future belongs to systems that bend without breaking, and to communities that no single company controls. Stonebraker’s first exhibit is text-to-SQL, the promise that a business user can ask a question in plain English and get an answer from the warehouse. Public benchmarks such as Spider and BIRD report accuracy above 80 percent. His team tried the same thing on MIT’s own data warehouse—1,400 tables of student, faculty, building, and salary information—using real queries from its SQL log. “We got zero-percent accuracy. Not 80, not 70, not 40—zero,” he said. Adding agentic AI, retrieval, and prompt engineering lifted that level into the teens. Telling the model every table and join term reached the 30s. “Still not ready for prime time.” The reasons are the stuff of every enterprise estate. There is no training data, because the warehouse is not on the public internet. The data is idiosyncratic: among other things, MIT buildings have numbers, not names. The schema has rotted into cryptic table and column names and overlapping views. And real queries are harder than benchmark ones. His team has published its own benchmark, Beaver, and says nobody has broken out of accuracy levels in the 30-percent range. The deeper problem, Stonebraker argues, is architectural. Large language models are good with text, but “what they are no good at is doing joins of structured data,” which is most of what an enterprise holds. Working with the city of Munich’s transport department, Stonebraker’s team found that answering a single citizen complaint about crosswalk timing meant combining regulations, CAD drawings, trolley schedules, and school-zone maps. Orchestrating that with a language model at the center was, he said, “ridiculously expensive.” The team’s answer, a system called Rubicon, uses models only on text, then casts the results into tables and hands the rest to a tabular engine. The orchestrator “looks like a query optimizer.” Stonebraker says it is cheaper by a factor of 10 and considerably more accurate than agent frameworks built around a model. He expects agentic AI to slide from the top of the hype cycle into the valley of despair before a more balanced view emerges. Enterprises hate non-determinism: Approve a loan and reverse it on review, and “that’s called a lawsuit.” Organizations also need protection from rogue agents. The guardrails inside the database, he notes, largely exist already: decades of access control, complete logs, and triggers that can stop a transaction in its tracks. Data-heavy agents, he suggests, belong inside the data layer as user-defined functions, if the tooling to debug and version them improves. Stonebraker is equally blunt about the frontier. The models have already used the real training data on the internet, and he knows of no enterprise that will let a model train on its proprietary data. AGI, he said, is “basically marketing hype” from foundation model vendors justifying their valuations. Why Postgres? “Postgres is the intergalactic answer to open source,” Stonebraker said. Its unique feature is that “it’s not controlled by any one company.” EDB, Microsoft, Google, and others each contribute committers, and nobody steers. He dates its rise to Oracle buying MySQL, when “everybody said: vendor control.” For enterprises, that independence is the foundation of sovereignty: retaining control over how and where their data systems operate. Momjian describes the same structure from the inside. Postgres has no office, no assets, and no legal entity, yet it produces “software better than billion-dollar companies.” Its forecasting tool is the community itself, which spots needs early from China, Russia, and the United States. “We only need one person in the world to be supersmart, and that person to tell us what they think.” Both experts describe a design philosophy that favors flexibility over the last few percent of speed. Stonebraker built Postgres to be extended, and that is why it absorbed JSON, full-text search, GIS, and now AI without a rewrite. Momjian adds a second call from the 1990s: Lean on the operating system rather than absorb it, which made Postgres a natural fit for the cloud. Momjian resists optimizations that buy 5 or 10 percent at the cost of rigidity, since a mistuned system can lose 40 percent. “If you take a Formula One car to the supermarket, there’s no place for the groceries.” The project plans on a horizon few vendors can match. “We’re looking at what would be the impact 5 years from now, 10 years from now.” The new release puts that discipline on show. Several previewed features, including SQL property graph queries and partition merging and splitting, were reverted, because reliability comes first. AI-assisted review is finding problems quickly; the August 2026 patch release for PostgreSQL 18 addressed 28 CVEs. What remains is practical: REPACK CONCURRENTLY for busy tables, smarter parallel autovacuum, and sequence synchronization for logical replication. Plan Advice, from PostgreSQL major contributor and committer Robert Haas, is the community’s answer to Oracle-style optimizer hints it rejected for years. “Somebody found a way of doing it that didn’t have the downsides,” Momjian said. The two diverge on how fast AI will write SQL. Momjian expects agents to create databases and generate much of the code. Stonebraker says that will not happen inside the enterprise until data is documented, silos are opened, and the politics change. They agree on the destination. AI needs a relational system to give it permanence and memory, and, in Momjian’s words, that is “a game-changer.” Forty years on, the inventor and the steward are betting on the same database. Belgium's tax agency confirms cyberattack, pulls services offline
2026-10-29

Belgium's tax agency confirms cyberattack, pulls services offline

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Officials confirm this week’s tax deadlines extended to November as a result Belgium's finance and tax agency has pulled "several" digital services offline after a cyberattack on the organization. FOD Financiën confirmed the attack on Wednesday, saying that MyMinfin, the agency's main online tax portal, as well as other online services, are temporarily unavailable while they start the initial investigations. "This measure helps limit the risks and makes it possible to carry out the necessary technical interventions to ensure the security of our systems," it said in a public announcement. FOD Financiën added that "this has consequences for tax deadlines in the near future," referring to the October 31 extended deadline to submit tax filings for specific types of income, such as sole traders and earnings from foreign companies. The agency confirmed the deadline will be extended to November 16, by which time MyMinfin, the platform through which such filings would normally be submitted, should be back up and running. The detail offered up in the public statement reflects the early stage of the investigation; not much is known, although there is nothing to suggest data was compromised. "Data protection is a top priority for the FOD Financiën," it said. "At present, there are no indications that a data breach has occurred or that data has been compromised. This aspect is also being closely investigated and followed up. "In the meantime, a large part of our other applications remains available with full or partial functionality. We are closely monitoring the situation and doing everything we can to make the affected services available again as soon as possible." Spokesperson Francis Adyns told local news outlets that the attack was thwarted within hours, and that it was unable to gain access to applications or data. "The attack could not be sufficiently expanded within our environment to take over applications or gain access to data," he told Nieuwsblad. "Elements that were potentially infected were isolated. As a result, MyMinfin, Tax-on-web, and Biztax went offline, but the hacking was successfully stopped. "The various services must be restored in an isolated environment. This requires considerable time to realize, with the necessary caution to rule out new risks." ® DoE-backed consortium hoping to scale, says orgs can apply to become, literally, part of BattMan Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: HPE Alletra MP X10000 cache offload delivers up to 20× faster time to first token in testing validated by Kamiwaza. Systemd adds support for Rust
2026-10-29

Systemd adds support for Rust

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
What's even better than Rust in the Linux kernel? Rust in systemd! Everyone's favorite Unix init system is adding support for the Unix world's favorite new programming language. What's the worst that could happen? Don't worry, this isn't here just yet. Agent P and his trusty team released systemd version 262 about a month ago. That means that systemd 263 is now under construction, and developer Christian Brauner announced on Mastodon that his new feature has been merged. The previous effort, which was pull request #44144, has now been split into five separate parts. Part 1 contains keyring-util and voa-util, described as "helpers for enrolling certificates into kernel keyrings." Part 2, support programs written in Rust, is the banner item. Then come a few more: bind only the headers Rust code uses, wrap the libsystemd-shared helpers systemd-keyring-setup needs, and finally, add systemd-keyring-setup. There are several important riders to the announcement, and these are spelled out in the second PR: "Rust is only used for new leaf components. Existing C code isn't ported and nothing that other components depend on is written in Rust." In other words, before anyone gets too excited, this does not indicate the start of an effort to rewrite systemd in Rust. All it means is that new systemd modules can be written in Rust. The second bullet point will be more significant for existing Rust developers: "No cargo. Everything is built with meson." Cargo is the Rust language’s package manager and build system. It's the normal way to build Rust applications, as well as to distribute modules of Rust code for other Rust developers to use. However, systemd already has a modern build system. It's called Meson and it's not unique to systemd – it is also used extensively by the GNOME project and the Mesa 3D graphics library. Meson itself is written in Python and uses Ninja to manage the compilation process. Ninja was designed to build Chrome faster, and it's also used for Android. The third stipulation is that any Rust code must not use the standard library. For those more familiar with C, this may sound extreme, but it's normal for embedded Rust programming. The project documents the system requirements. It needs Rust version 1.85 or later. That's modest: that version was released in February 2025, and version 1.99.1 appeared earlier this month. It also calls for bindgen version 0.71.1 (from December 2024) or newer, and Meson 1.4.0 (from March 2024). These are relatively old versions – for instance, it's the Meson version included in Debian 13). Brauner added an interesting little coda in a second Masto post: saying that the team would have liked to support Rust in the core of systemd, but currently, Rust’s support for dynamic libraries isn't quite there yet. It's perhaps worth noting that writing systemd modules in Rust isn't the same thing at all as calling systemd from Rust code. There are several tools for doing that, and they have been around for years: for example, the systemd crate first appeared in 2015. ® DoE-backed consortium hoping to scale, says orgs can apply to become, literally, part of BattMan Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 PARTNER CONTENT: HPE Alletra MP X10000 cache offload delivers up to 20× faster time to first token in testing validated by Kamiwaza. Apparently, remembering things beats calculating them again. This open-source project lets you sync Withings data to other apps without a subscription
2026-10-29

This open-source project lets you sync Withings data to other apps without a subscription

The Verge | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
In response to Withings charging new users to sync data with third-party apps, YourOwnWeigh will do it for free. In response to Withings charging new users to sync data with third-party apps, YourOwnWeigh will do it for free. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. When it was revealed earlier this month that Withings would require new users to subscribe to a Withings Plus subscription to sync heath data from the company’s devices to third-party apps, Tom Anthony, who was already fed up with the Withings mobile app which they describe as a “bloated mess,” became motivated to “free” their scales. Anthony’s solution was to create an open-source project called YourOwnWeigh that reroutes your data away from Withings’ servers so it can be wirelessly synced to other apps and platforms for free. You can read more about the project on GitHub, where you’ll also find quick-start guides on how to get YourOwnWeigh set up, but it mostly consists of a web app running on a server of your choice. If you don’t already operate your own, Anthony says YourOwnWeigh can run entirely on a free CloudFlare account given how little data it’s moving around. The YourOwnWeigh web app handles several tasks, including using Bluetooth to update Withings scales with alternate firmware so they upload data to a non-Withings server you’ve configured. Instead of hosting a copy of Withings’ firmware, it’s downloaded and patched as needed. The web app also imports the scale’s current settings and existing user profiles, and downloads the health data generated by regular weigh-ins. To get that data into Apple Health, Anthony used Apple Shortcuts to create a scheduled automation that pulls the Withings data off the alternate server and imports it into the app, but they’re hoping the community can come up with ways to expand the compatibility to other apps, including Google Health. YourOwnWeigh is also currently limited to just two older models of Withings’ scales. But assuming the firmware on newer models can be patched in a similar manner, expanding the project’s compatibility should be possible. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Microsoft’s AI future has fewer jobs
2026-10-29

Microsoft’s AI future has fewer jobs

The Verge | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
It’s also time to name the next Elder Scrolls game, and Xbox’s architect of Forza Motorsport is leaving. It’s also time to name the next Elder Scrolls game, and Xbox’s architect of Forza Motorsport is leaving. To anyone listening closely to what Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella has been saying over the past couple of years, this week felt like the clearest sign yet that AI is about to fundamentally change the size of Microsoft’s workforce. Microsoft is now offering voluntary buyouts to around 60,000 employees across the US, UK, Canada, and India. It’s the first time the company has presented such an offer to so many employees, expanding greatly upon the retirement offer that was limited to around 9,000 employees earlier this year. It’s all part of an ongoing effort to avoid reducing investment in AI infrastructure by lowering Microsoft’s operating costs and headcount. “Our total people costs will go down, our cost per head will go up, and my GPU per researcher will go up,” Nadella said on a podcast almost two years ago, responding to a question about AI’s impact on headcount. That’s a financial way of saying Microsoft will have fewer people, but it will offer higher salaries and more tools and AI power to those who remain. It also helps explain the continued layoffs we’ve seen at Microsoft over the past couple of years. Microsoft has been performing layoffs regularly over the past decade, but over the past couple of years, the layoffs and executive departures have been significant. These aren’t the annual layoffs of old; they’re now happening every few months. This week’s voluntary exit offer arrived just months after Microsoft laid off 4,800 employees, cuts that impacted its Xbox and commercial sales teams the most. For the first time in a decade, Microsoft’s overall headcount has also gone down year over year. Microsoft now employs around 223,000 people as of June 30th, 5,000 fewer than the 228,000 people it employed in 2025. Microsoft laid off 4 percent of its workforce in July last year, and it’s now offering voluntary buyout offers to around a quarter of its employees. More than 30 percent of the 9,000 eligible employees accepted a retirement offer earlier this year, but that number will likely be far less for the company’s latest Voluntary Exit Offer. That’s largely because, according to sources familiar, the offer is practically the same as regular severance terms at Microsoft — cash based on time at the company, accelerated stock vesting, some additional subsidized health coverage, and several months of career services. But what makes this unusual is the scale of the amount of employees who have been offered this exit. Even if only 5 percent accept the offer, that’s still 3,000 employees. I understand eligible employees have until December to decide, so we won’t know the impact of this offer for a while yet. With rumors of more layoffs in November and January, the amount of employees who do accept will surely inform Microsoft’s numbers for its next round of cuts. There are some, both inside and outside the company, who think Microsoft needs these cuts. “Microsoft needs fewer people,” says Merill Fernando, a former Microsoft Entra product manager. “I’ve been on the inside. I’ve seen how things get built. Lots of small orgs. Layers on layers. Silos everywhere.” Fernando paints a picture of a Microsoft where every organization “had its own priorities and its own decision makers,” so it’s hard to get the company to do anything collectively. It sounds a little like the meme where each organization in Microsoft is pointing guns at each other, competing instead of collaborating. Nadella even addressed that meme last year in a podcast with Stripe co-founder John Collison, acknowledging that the comic has defined Microsoft’s cultural narrative “more so than reality,” but that it had signaled some very real tensions inside the company. “In some sense, some of these divisional tensions are real issues that need to have tension. Social cohesion is not a goal, winning in the marketplace is a goal,” said Nadella. The question now is whether Nadella can reshape Microsoft for the AI era without undoing the cultural transformation that helped define his leadership. A company that has shifted to be built around empathy is now asking its employees to reckon with an AI future that could make many of their jobs unnecessary. How Nadella handles that transition will define his legacy as much as the growth and success he’s achieved over the past decade. A few weeks ago I started hearing from Xbox employees that Dan Greenawalt, the longtime creative force behind Forza Motorsport, is leaving after almost 27 years. Greenawalt is departing as the head of Turn 10 Studios, the Forza Motorsport creator that merged with Forza Horizon developer Playground Games. Playground Games studio head Trevor Williams revealed the news in a recent internal memo, seen by Notepad. “Dan Greenawalt has decided that this is the right time for him to retire from Microsoft,” says Williams. Greenawalt will stay on as an adviser to Williams and the newly appointed Playground Games chief operating officer, Michel Bastien, “while he transitions to his next phase.” Here’s part of Williams’ memo about Greenawalt: It’s hard to overstate Dan’s impact over the past 27 years. From shipping Project Gotham Racing on the original XBOX in 2001 to leading the creation of Forza, founding Turn 10 Studios and appearing so many times on our E3 stage, usually making a dramatic entrance with a supercar in tow – and so much more – we owe tremendous thanks and gratitude to Dan. On behalf of both teams, I would like to thank Dan for his leadership, his support, and his partnership. I wish him well for whatever comes next. Microsoft revealed last month that it was merging its two Forza developers into a single studio as part of a massive restructuring of Xbox. Both Playground Games and Turn 10 Studios have merged into just Playground Games. The studio now has two locations: Playground Games Leamington Spa and Playground Games Redmond. With Greenawalt retiring and Turn 10 merged into Playground Games, it’s not clear if we’ll ever see Microsoft return to Forza Motorsport. More than 70 people at Turn 10 Studios were laid off last year, in a move that hit the vast majority of the studio. Over the past year, Turn 10 Studios has essentially been maintaining Forza Motorsport, and now the remaining employees have moved to Playground Games to work on Forza Horizon and Fable. It’s been more than eight years since Bethesda officially announced the existence of the sixth game in its Elder Scrolls series. While there have been some debates around platform exclusivity for The Elder Scrolls VI, we’ve heard very little detail about the game that Bethesda has been working on all this time. Sources familiar with Bethesda’s plans tell me that the Xbox-owned publisher is about to announce that the next Elder Scrolls game will officially be called The Elder Scrolls VI: Sentinel. The announcement could come as soon as next month, during a period that will mark 15 years since The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim was released. Bethesda has been showing off Sentinel gameplay in recent months, I’m told. Xbox CEO Asha Sharma got a live playthrough of Sentinel in August, and she praised the scale of the game and said “the story is even greater.” While I’m only expecting a name reveal next month and nothing more, I’ve been hearing some additional details about the game. If you want to totally avoid spoilers about the game, then don’t read the next paragraph. I understand that Sentinel will be set in Hammerfell, a province in the Elder Scrolls universe that’s appeared in The Elder Scrolls Adventures: Redguard and The Elder Scrolls Online. Sentinel will be the first game about the history of the Elder Scrolls, and help uncover the secrets of these mysterious ancient artifacts.
