The NYT Cooking app is testing AI recipe modifications
2026-10-29

The NYT Cooking app is testing AI recipe modifications

The Verge | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Some users aren’t exactly thrilled with The New York Times’ recipe-tweaking AI tool. Some users aren’t exactly thrilled with The New York Times’ recipe-tweaking AI tool. The New York Times is testing a new AI feature for its Cooking app that helps users to modify recipes, and it has sparked some drama among users. Last Friday, NYT Cooking senior editor Genevieve Ko announced the feature, which can be used to recommend substitutions for ingredients and cooking equipment, scale recipes to any number of servings, and “generally make our recipes work for what a cook needs at that moment.” One of the examples given in the article shows a user asking to use baby spinach instead of kale, with the Cooking app feature advising that because baby spinach wilts faster, it should be added during a later step of the recipe. Ko says it can also change the recipes around different pans and tools. Customized recipes are private and can’t be published or shared through the app. The AI modification feature is being rolled out gradually while the NYT monitors how it performs — it’s currently available to all Cooking subscribers on the iOS app, and it will be more broadly available “soon.” Some NYT readers have pushed back against the feature, citing concerns in the article’s comment section around the environmental impact of widespread AI adoption and the general impact that AI features may have on the user experience. When one user asked if there will be a way to disable the AI feature, Ko responded with, “No, not at this time,” and noted that the feature is “completely optional to use.” “Unbelievably disappointing to see,” said another NYT user. “I’ve always loved NYT Cooking and it pains me that the AI mandate has reached what used to be a wonderful and simple collection of recipes and notes from all sorts of different people.” This isn’t the first time that AI has been used by the NYT Cooking app. Ko says it was used to convert imperial volume measurements to metric weight measurements in recipes two years ago, and a feature was added last year that converts recipes to halve or double the original serving size. The comments on the new AI feature announcement post have since been closed, but NYT spokesperson Jordan Cohen tells The Verge that this is standard practice: “Comments on this article remained open for four days, with editors from Cooking taking care to answer questions, address concerns and engage directly with readers. We kept that conversation open to give readers a meaningful opportunity to share feedback and hear from the team. Closing comments on older articles is standard practice at The Times so that our moderators can focus on new discussions. We’re pleased to see that many members of our Cooking community are interested in exploring this tool and are already trying it out on our iOS app; they’re reporting good results.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Anthropic is asking Wall Street to believe in AGI

Anthropic is asking Wall Street to believe in AGI

The Verge | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
SemiAnalysis’ Joey Brookhart helps break down the Anthropic S-1 and why he thinks the company could be worth $10 trillion. Today on Decoder, I’m joined by Joey Brookhart, an analyst at the chip and AI research firm SemiAnalysis, to help me dig into the story inside the numbers of the Anthropic IPO filing — a document known as an S-1. Anthropic just filed its S-1 to go public on the Nasdaq, and it could start trading as soon as next month at a valuation of anywhere between $1.5 trillion and $2 trillion. That would immediately make it one of the most valuable companies on the planet. But the price tag really isn’t the whole story here. The S-1 document published earlier this week is hundreds upon hundreds of pages filled with details that finally paint a picture of the economics of a frontier AI lab. As you might imagine, that picture is both really complicated and built on a series of very, very ambitious assumptions about the future and what AI will do to it. So I wanted to have Joey on the show to help break down what this document tells us, both about Anthropic’s finances and the broader AI economy. Joey and his colleagues at SemiAnalysis actually built a model of Anthropic’s finances from the outside back in July, so he’s been waiting for this filing about as eagerly as anyone, and I was excited to hear his take on what we’ve learned and, importantly, what we still don’t know. Okay: SemiAnalysis’ Joey Brookhart on the Anthropic S-1 and the costs and risks of building frontier AI. Here we go. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Joey Brookhart, you are an analyst at SemiAnalysis. You’re also co-lead on SemiAnalysis’ Tokenomics model, which is going to come up a lot in this episode. Welcome to Decoder. I am very excited to talk to you. We’re talking just a couple days after the big Anthropic S-1 was filed. It’s our first look inside of the finances of one of the leading frontier models. What’s your take? What’s your read into it? It was an awesome S-1. It’s been a rough three weeks since the investor day. Some people were somewhat disappointed in the September [annualized recurring revenue] numbers, but what we saw here was a company that has efficiently scaled to an amazing level, with a $1 trillion-plus valuation, $70 billion-plus of ARR in a very short amount of time. In gross margins, compute margins, and free cash flow, everything is what you’d want to see. So I don’t think there was much to nitpick in this filing. It’s the reason you’ve seen a lot of the semiconductor complex, and the neoclouds and compute stocks, really rally again in the last two days. It’s funny, I don’t think I can nitpick the S-1 itself. I want to get into some of the details because there are a lot of numbers in there that are worth thinking about. I actually just want to start at the really big picture. You run a thing called Tokenomics. I’m going to explain what Tokenomics is to people. I look at Anthropic and where it could go. I look at Dario Amodei saying that he can see his way to $1 trillion in revenue. Where does that money come from? Because so much of these businesses are about eating every other business and it feels like someone else has to make some money to pay Anthropic to generate these revenues. I haven’t actually seen that laid out very clearly. Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here. It’s so interesting. Today, because even the scale we’re at is $120 billion of ARR between the two leading labs, it’s still an extremely narrow market. The top 1 percent of customers are the ones that are spending $50,000 to $100,000 per employee. Your broader Fortune 500 or Global 2000, which typically do a lot of IT spend, isn’t spending much. A lot of the spending, especially at Anthropic, is coding-specific. It’s almost a single vertical company. We look in the future to 2027, you think to this broader adoption and white collar work. This typical enterprise Q4 budgeting season, we actually haven’t gone through with one of these labs yet in any meaningful way because their ARR did really start to accelerate until the beginning of the year. We think of other cases like cyber, which can be similar to coding, where these are eight-figure-plus contracts with leading enterprises that can scale very quickly due to all the cybersecurity incidents we’re seeing. You get to other verticals like bio. To get to $1 trillion, we do have to move to some of that white collar work, where it goes from AI augmentation to labor saving or replacement in some areas. You look at things like Microsoft’s buyout offers to employees as an example of this, but there’s no wealth of other verticals that they can get into and really start to move the needle. This is the single biggest question I have, not just about the S-1, but the entire industry. I would understand that if there are one or two leading labs. There are two big companies that have a technology that can automate a whole bunch of things. We will see how well that automation goes sector by sector, but there are not just two leading labs. There are quite a few of them. There are open weight models. This isn’t a traditional software business like ServiceNow or Workday, where you have just an incredible logo or gross retention of 95 percent to 98 percent and customers sign these nice three-year contracts, they never churn, they add some seats, and maybe they adopt another product that is being upsold. This is purely consumption. The other portion was the 20 percent drop in the last two weeks of the year versus the prior two weeks last year in token spend, because of the holiday season. You’re not going to do work code stuff, you’re not going to be coding for work during the holidays. This is extremely, extremely pure consumption business, even different from how we think about Snowflake or Datadog today in public markets. Obviously, Anthropic is not the only company that’s going to have an IPO. OpenAI is right next to them. They’ve delayed theirs. You mentioned earlier Anthropic is much more enterprise focused. I think OpenAI for a long time thought that Google wouldn’t react to them and they would just take the search business away and everything would be fine. Google obviously reacted for better or worse. Search is now AI Overviews. Looking at the Anthropic S-1, what are you looking ahead to with OpenAI? Because they are a more consumer facing company. OpenAI was 60-40 consumer, 60 percent consumer and 40 percent enterprise at the end of Q1. That didn’t flip for the first time until January or August to be enterprise focused. What they’ll tell you is enterprise business — because it was so consumer and subscription focused, this consumption business that’s 80 percent of Anthropic’s revenue — didn’t take off until a release of 5.6 and then with Astra. Those are really great models. OpenAI took the majority of actual net new ARR in Q3, but it’s going to take some time because what’s been in the Wall Street Journal, like negative 100 percent EBITA margins versus we’re seeing positive 11 percent here. There are some differences, some pretty big differences, in the business model where they just don’t monetize at the same rate that Anthropic does. That’s a little bit because the business makeup — the verticals, that consumer-versus-enterprise split — and that’s changing. What they’ll tell you is they see a really good Q4 and they have a much stronger enterprise sales function than Anthropic has. I expect them to come up, but I don’t think the financials are going to look as good as this even when they drop their S-1 maybe in early 2027. Do you think that’s why they delayed it? To get their financials looking better? Yeah, I think OpenAI had a really strong Q3. Enterprise grew 100 percent quarter-over-quarter versus the total business which was 77 percent. You get another strong quarter of enterprise here. If Opus 5.5 didn’t take a ton of share, that would put OpenAI in a much better position to tell the story. There are some other turns here between now and when Anthropic actually goes public. They’re going to do a roadshow. There’ll be some pricing events. What are you looking for from Anthropic? They did an investor day two or three weeks ago where they did give some more color. I think they’ll walk through why Q3 was so weak. Meta was a very, very large customer, probably over 10 percent. What’s been publicly reported is they went from 60,000 Claude Code seats down to 30,000. These token volumes didn’t disappear, they just shifted into Meta’s internal models. Anthropic started cracking down on anti-distillation, and that was 5 percent of revenue prior. I think they said 5 percent to 7 percent of revenue in the S-1 and stuff against their terms of service. So what’s here on the roadshows is a lot of this October acceleration after Opus 5.5. We believe that they’re back on track. If they were doing $10 billion of net new ARR per month in Q2 and that fell down to $4 billion in Q3, that they’re getting back on that $8 billion to $10 billion pace here in Q4. People want to hear about these new verticals, specifically cyber. They’re doing some patching until they have that full cyber SKU rolled out, but they’re definitely monetizing cyber today for vulnerability code scanning. These are not small contracts, I should say, with existing customers. I think they’ll start to message what the 2027 ARR growth path looks like. The other thing too is expectations management, keeping numbers low enough, not talking about AGI as much. [Laughs] It’s hard to do expectation management when your risk factors are like, “We could pose an existential risk to all of humanity.” [Laughs] Right. And so saying you do that — you’re going to achieve AGI at some point, you’re going to replace everyone’s jobs — but then trying to keep numbers low enough for Q4 and to have this massive beat the first quarter you become public and have a decent guide for 2027, it’ll be a nice mix of seesawing between that long-term vision and short-term expectations management. MIT Technology Review
We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time
2026-10-29

We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This summer, hundreds of OpenAI agents tried to cheat on a cybersecurity test. They broke out of their test environment and successfully hacked Hugging Face, a site that they believed held clues about how they’d be graded. Around the same time, another OpenAI agent accessed private files from an Australian government website. It’s a problem that’s by no means confined to OpenAI. In the months since, Anthropic, Google, and Meta have also disclosed incidents in which their agents have gone off track. And worryingly, in many cases the agents eluded the very researchers and safety teams tasked with monitoring them. AI agents are taking unauthorized actions, and the companies building and running them are mostly relying on luck and hindsight to mitigate any harmful impacts. They must do more to ensure safety. That’s why EU regulators and US agencies are rightfully calling for agent developers and deployers to monitor agents in real time. But these rules assume that companies can see what their agents are doing and why—when in reality, they lack sufficient visibility. As agents roll out across critical sectors, from finance to government to medicine, we must be able to monitor them in real time to prevent wide-ranging and potentially devastating consequences.  And while safety depends on monitoring, monitoring requires observability: the capacity to capture what the system did, process it into signals, and present those to humans or automated oversight systems. In other words, we need to know what the agent did and why it did it. And as our recent research shows, current agent frameworks—the software that runs agents— do not reliably generate the crucial observability records we need to ensure that agents are deployed safely. Agents present new kinds of problems that generative AI alone does not. They inherit the unpredictability of the LLMs they are built on, but where a generative AI system produces a single output for a human to act on, an agent performs longer tasks—it plans, picks tools, spawns sub-agents, and takes actions. So an agent’s course can take shape in a multitude of different ways, and no tests before launch can fully anticipate the risks. This makes observing the agents while they work even more challenging, and even more important. With colleagues from academia, civil society, and industry, we studied the four most widely used agent frameworks to see what signals they actually emit. We found major gaps that made it hard to achieve key safety goals, like noticing policy violations, unauthorized actions, and even human oversight failures. Key observability requirements were missing—like Agent IDs that persist over time, and records about which humans approved agent tasks. This made it hard to tell which agents did what or assess whether they were taking unauthorized actions. The good news is that these gaps, once identified, can be closed. Doing so starts with capturing two types of records: those that reveal what happened in an agent’s run and how the agent made its decisions. The “what happened” records identify the agent (while preserving privacy) and log how humans intervened and whether agent permissions or “memory” shifted, while the “decision” records show an agent’s reasoning and levels of confidence in the actions it’s taken. Take, for example, agents working to process a bank’s mortgage loans. The system examines an application and recommends denying it, and then a human loan officer signs off on the decision. In a later audit, or perhaps even in real time, a bank or regulator needs to be able to reconstruct which agent made the call, which officer approved it, and how long they took. And the bank must also analyze records of how the agent arrived at its refusal decision in order to ensure it did not use off-limits information, like details about the neighborhood the applicant is from. Recording “what happened” and “how the agent decided” require different approaches, but both are feasible. The current lack of “what happened” records is mostly a software plumbing problem that can be addressed through OpenTelemetry, an open observability standard maintained by engineers across the industry and by the agent frameworks adopting it. OpenTelemetry has launched a working group drafting shared formats for tracing agents, but they have yet to be finalized. The group should urgently incorporate standards for maintaining persistent agent identity and for recording human interventions and changes to agents’ permission or memory. And it should be done before these systems can scale, all the while attending to the privacy implications of agent ID that links to real people. Preserving “how agents decided” records is more challenging. The issue raises open research questions for model providers and researchers: Does the step-by-step reasoning a model displays, known as chain of thought, reflect how the model actually made a decision? And do we have reliable ways to measure how confident the agent is in that decision? These questions need answers before anyone sets standards for how such observability records are used. No single organization or sector can close the observability gaps on their own, but together, they can support the infrastructure required to prevent dangerous incidents and support safety. AI developers building agent frameworks, like OpenAI, Anthropic, CrewAI, and LangChain, must integrate telemetry signals recording an agent’s persistent identity, any human interventions, and any changes to an agent’s permissions and memory. Meanwhile, enterprises adopting these agent frameworks have an influential role to play; enterprises in regulated industries, like banks and hospitals, can put pressure on the agent developers to supply these missing observability records where standards do not yet exist. Enterprises must also define policies, permissions, and roles that set out what agents can safely do, since telemetry alone cannot assess whether agents broke specific rules. Finally, regulators and AI standards institutes must specify the observability records required for high-stakes AI deployments while leaving the technical details to industry. We have already seen initial momentum here: NIST’s agent standards initiative in the US is working on agent identity, one of the most salient gaps we found in our research. No bank would let a thousand unbadged employees loose on customer accounts and hope to piece together what happened when something goes wrong. Yet that’s how we’re deploying AI agents. Making their behavior observable is the first step toward fixing this. And by building the regulatory and policy ecosystem that makes it possible to act on what we see, we can hold organizations accountable. Until then, every task we hand to an agent hands over our agency, too. Madhulika Srikumar is the Head of the AI Safety Program at the Partnership on AI, a global non-profit partnership of leading industry, academic, and civil society organizations, advancing best practices in AI governance. Claire Leibowicz is the Director of AI, Trust, and Society at the Partnership on AI and a doctoral candidate at Oxford University. Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot
2026-10-29

Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Climate change itself is increasingly fueling climate change. Rising concentrations of greenhouse gases are driving up global temperatures, setting off dangerous feedback loops that we have not fully accounted for in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming in various ways, but a growing number of observations and studies are at last providing a sharper sense of just how powerful these cascading effects could be. And, by extension, they’re showing how much faster the planet may warm in this century. A study in Environmental Research Letters in September, synthesizing the results of previous papers, found that the additional carbon dioxide and methane released from thawing permafrost, intensifying wildfires, hotter lakes and reservoirs, and wetter, warmer wetlands alone could drive global average temperatures up 0.2 °C to 0.4 °C this century. That would come on top of the 2.6°C of warming above preindustrial levels that modelers are already expecting through 2100. The additional heat could subject many of the world’s cities to monthslong heat waves and expose hundreds of millions of people to flooding. None of the models that inform the major UN climate reports, which assess how much the planet will likely warm under various emissions scenarios, account for all these feedback effects. Many of them don’t incorporate any. That leaves a huge blind spot in our understanding of how much the world could actually warm in the coming decades—and how rapidly nations will need to slash emissions to stay within critical boundaries. A separate analysis published in One Earth in March, which examined what the feedback problem means for our international climate goals, concluded that the world may be much further off track than is widely believed. It found that just three regional sources of warming-driven emissions—thawing permafrost, increasing fires in boreal forests, and warming tropical wetlands—may shorten by as much as 25% the time before the planet consistently breaches the 1.5 °C and 2 °C warming thresholds. Our climate models and the UN’s reports clearly need to be updated to better represent these effects. Nations must also step up their efforts to reduce emissions to reflect this improving understanding of the dangers we face, says Sam Abernethy, the lead author of the paper and a climate scientist at Spark Climate Solutions, a San Francisco–based nonprofit research group. At least some of this is starting to happen. Spark kick-started an initiative, called the Warming-Induced Greenhouse Gas Emissions Model Intercomparison Project, through which more than 20 international teams are running the same set of analyses on different models to develop greater understanding of feedback effects’ potential power. Researchers involved hope that the UN will incorporate those results into its next major climate assessment, due out in 2029. That report will update estimates of the remaining emissions that can be released before the world crosses key temperature thresholds. But what is already clear is that the planet is rapidly warming and that nations haven’t done nearly enough to cut fossil-fuel pollution. And even if we did manage to halt it, warming-driven emissions could continue. So a number of the research groups involved in the recent studies have started to explore what nations could do to get feedback emissions under control. Scientists at the Woodwell Climate Research Center in Massachusetts are, for example, investigating the possibility of preventing fires or rapidly detecting them and putting them out when they threaten to burn the vegetation and soil that help insulate particularly carbon-rich wedges of permafrost (a subject I explored at length in a recent feature story). In addition, the Environmental Defense Fund’s Climate Innovation Initiative has backed lab research on the potential of adding sulfate, iron, or other compounds to wetlands to counteract fast-rising methane emissions. Several startups are also exploring possibilities. They’re looking into suppressing the lightning strikes that are igniting more and more fires in high latitudes, placing synthetic mats above permafrost to prevent it from melting, and deploying autonomous drones to quickly suppress potentially high-emissions fires before they burn out of control. Investigating all these ideas is worthwhile. But researchers are only in the earliest stages of figuring out how big a dent in emissions any such efforts could achieve across sprawling global ecosystems undergoing massive transformations. The surest path to reducing the effect of warming-driven warming remains the one we’ve known all along: breaking society’s addiction to fossil fuels. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
Creating AI value with enterprise context
2026-10-28

Creating AI value with enterprise context

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Across a business, any number of AI platforms, systems, and tools may be at work simultaneously—for example, AI-powered customer platforms, conversational assistants, and workflow and process automation tools. Each one is sophisticated, advanced, and quick to respond. But, when set to work, the outcomes and actions that emerge may be wrong, confused, or lacking a proper understanding of the business. Many organizations find themselves in this situation—and it could be one of key reasons they struggle to extract returns on their AI investments. An MIT Technology Review Insights poll finds that while every organization surveyed is using AI today to some degree, fewer than one in five (16%) are extracting transformative value from their deployments. The largest percentage of respondents (40%) say they are seeing moderate gains in specific tasks or teams, while 21% report meaningful gains, leaving 23% that are seeing minimal returns or none at all. Companies are mostly still piloting AI use cases (according to 38% of respondents) or using it to assist employees’ work with tools like co-pilots or chat assistants (35%). Just 12% of enterprises have AI agents in production and 3% say they are using AI to do most of the work in key processes. “There’s a big gap between what the models are capable of and how the models are actually being useful inside organizations,” says Prukalpa Sankar, co-founder of Atlan. “For every real-life intent and purpose, artificial general intelligence is already here. It’s just not really useful in most companies today.” The missing layer, Sankar says, is context, or how well AI understands a business. A context layer is the architectural infrastructure that sits between an organization’s business systems (for example, systems of record and data, and its AI platforms). It translates enterprise intelligence into governed, machine-usable context for AI agents or models and helps knowledge compound. “Without a context layer for AI, it’s almost like you’re hiring the smartest intern in the world, blindfolding them, and throwing them into your organization,” Sankar says. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. MIT Technology Review
Pairing industrial AI autonomy and human control
2026-10-28

Pairing industrial AI autonomy and human control

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Living digital twins are helping industrial plants run more like living systems, and setting the stage for industries that work in sync. Most people associate industrial environments with cold steel and clockwork machinery. Functionally, though, a plant or factory is more like a living thing: It has a pulse, even a metabolism. It goes through wear and tear as it ages, and signs of deteriorating health often show up as barely perceptible irregularities. Digital twin technology has long aimed to mirror that dynamism and model anomalies that may be symptomatic of larger problems. But earlier implementations were a few beats behind physical reality; they were incapable of real-time analysis and unable to proactively address any issues detected. They focused more on simulation, observation and what-if scenario planning, rather than closed-loop execution. Rather than serving as a digital mirror of reality that can spot anomalies or suggest optimizations, a living digital twin combines a dynamic, high-fidelity digital representation of complex operations with the capacity to intervene in the physical world through operations and control systems. AI is at the center of this change. Rob McGreevy, chief product officer at AVEVA, likens newer, AI-infused digital twins to organisms that grow and evolve. These “living” versions are adaptive, evergreen models of a plant’s assets and processes. Because they keep pace with live conditions and connect systems that once ran in isolation, they provide a previously missing layer of visibility and orchestration that better reflects the organic rhythms of the facility itself. Now, agentic AI is also enabling industrial systems to move beyond diagnostics or suggestions and act of their own accord. As the technology progresses toward greater autonomy, it’s starting to develop the reflexes for a kind of industrial homeostasis, in which small, constant adjustments keep a plant or factory healthy and humming. All that said, human judgment and operator oversight still play an instrumental role by defining what healthy looks like to begin with—and flagging when AI systems are falling out of alignment. Living digital twins provide the means for human engineers to monitor what AI is doing and ensure execution is on track. Living digital twins link engineering and design information with real-time operational data through a digital thread, or a connected record of an asset that follows it through its lifecycle. A couple of notable technological shifts have helped string this thread together: Sensors (which have gotten more affordable in recent years) now stream telemetry from IoT devices and industrial control equipment, and software platforms bring that information into user-friendly interfaces. Predictive and physics-based models can also provide AI-enriched insights into how a machine or process might behave. The lenses through which operators view digital twins have evolved in turn. “You can look at the digital twin through the lens of sustainability: What are my emissions?” says McGreevy. “Or, through the lens of maintenance: Is this asset performing in a healthy way? Or, through a performance metric: Is it producing the number of widgets I need?” The next phase is letting AI act on select insights those lenses illuminate. For their part, manufacturers are eager for this chapter: In a 2026 Manufacturing Leadership Council survey of manufacturers (opens in new tab), 66% said they are using or plan to use agentic AI in their operations, and 40% expect some degree of factory autonomy by 2030. At the same time, they’re wary of the potential risks: In Deloitte’s 2025 smart manufacturing survey (opens in new tab), 65% of executives ranked operational risk, including the potential for business disruption, among their top concerns. Source: MIT Technology Review Insights with data from Manufacturing Leadership Council (opens in new tab), 2026 “There is a lot more refinement to be done, a lot more proof points, testing, and validation needed,” McGreevy says about the ongoing process of making AI autonomous. In the near term, McGreevy expects agentic AI to follow the path seen in other industrial technologies like SCADA systems. “The ‘S’ in SCADA means supervisory, and supervisory means there’s a human in the middle,” he says. He sees AI working mostly in that advisory mode for the foreseeable future, with full autonomy limited to narrow, highly repetitive tasks. As for the role human operators play, the day-to-day is evolving. Workers who once specialized in a single process line or maintenance task can now take on multiple disciplines. They can spend less time and effort approving routine actions one by one, and more on defining acceptable operating limits. Because AI can reinforce patterns in the data it receives, catching missteps early is a core part of the job. Human knowledge and experience in interpreting what AI is seeing and doing is instrumental. Governance on a company- and industry-wide scale, too, needs to adapt to the times. Organizations must establish clear guardrails for which decisions AI can make on its own and how its actions are monitored and audited. Still, many have yet to set up comprehensive safeguards: In a recent study from AVEVA and IMD (opens in new tab), 43% of organizations said AI has changed decision-making and accountability across ecosystem partners only slightly or not at all, while just 23% reported a significant or fundamental change. (Only 27% reported sharing data substantially or extensively with partners.) In the meantime, regulators are starting to define some of the rules. The EU AI Act classifies certain AI safety components used in critical infrastructure as high-risk and imposes requirements for human oversight. Companies are setting up their own structures as well; AVEVA, for one, has a responsible AI governance board and a published set of principles for how AI is developed and deployed. Inside the semi-autonomous systems already running, that oversight looks different from one deployment to another. In some cases, an operator approves every action; in others, AI acts on its own while people oversee and define the boundaries it can operate within. McGreevy cites several examples of what machine autonomy and human-plus-machine collaboration looks like among AVEVA customers: The same synchronization that was once missing from individual plants’ technology systems has also been lacking on a larger scale. “Manufacturing and critical infrastructure have operated as siloed systems for the last 50 years,” says McGreevy. But certain emerging technologies, he suggests, are creating opportunities for more holistic coordination. On an enterprise level, this might involve the integration of physical AI: systems that pair robotics and sensing technologies with AI-powered decision-making. Drones that fly over pipelines and power lines, for instance, can feed visual inspection data to algorithms that spot gas leaks and other hazards. Not only does this create a more accurate and interconnected flow of information, but it spares humans from needing to physically enter potentially dangerous environments. “The robotics-meets-software convergence will mean fewer incidents, fewer outages, and a more sustainable way of operating,” McGreevy predicts. A bigger-picture version extends beyond any one plant or process to entire industrial networks, something McGreevy dubs the “integrated value chain.” “Living digital twins make digital manifestations of the entire value chain possible,” he says. “This allows companies to react quickly to supply chain changes, demand changes, tariffs, and material availability.” Amid today’s realities of turbulent geopolitics and shifting regulatory demands, such resilience will be “an absolute necessity,” adds McGreevy. “Without it, it will be very hard to run an effective business.”

No US stock trades under X since Nippon Steel bought US Steel last year. With SpaceX and Tesla exploring a merger, investors say Musk could claim the symbol, though he has not said so. Only five single-letter US tickers are free. The stock ticker X is free, and some investors think Elon Musk could claim it if SpaceX and Tesla merge, Bloomberg reported. The symbol has been unused since Nippon Steel bought United States Steel last year. Musk has long loved the letter. Tesla sells the Model X, SpaceX carries it in its name, Twitter became X after he bought it in 2022, and one of his sons is called X Æ A-Xii. Even his early online bank, X.com, later became part of PayPal, and he bought the domain back in 2017. “The merger has a ways to go, but if it closes, nobody should be surprised to see X back on the tape,” said Dave Mazza, chief executive of Roundhill Investments. “It’s entirely on brand.” Musk, who runs both companies, said last week that special committees at SpaceX and Tesla are exploring a merger. He called it “a good idea” but said no terms had been agreed and a deal may never happen. He has not said he wants the ticker. Single-letter tickers are rare, and only five, I, K, N, X and Y, are free in the US, according to Bloomberg. Tickers do change hands. SpaceX listed on Nasdaq as SPCX, a symbol that a Tuttle Capital fund used until April. Some investors think a merged company would need a new name, too. “Tesla still makes people think cars, and SpaceX makes people think rockets,” said Haris Khurshid, chief investment officer at Karobaar Capital. “X will probably mark that spot,” said Max Gokhman of Franklin Templeton Investment Solutions. “That would be the most Musk thing ever.” Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Apple’s touchscreen MacBook Pro keeps macOS familiar, Bloomberg finds

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
A person uses the touchscreen on Apple’s MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display, in a press image released on 28 October 2026. Bloomberg’s Chris Welch tried Apple’s first touchscreen MacBook Pro and found Apple took a measured approach, adding touch without reshaping macOS. Gestures felt smooth, but the tiny window buttons are awkward to tap. The laptop ships on 6 November from $2,899. Apple’s first touchscreen MacBook Pro does not turn the Mac into an iPad, according to a hands-on by Bloomberg’s Chris Welch. He tried the laptop after Apple’s launch event in Los Angeles on Wednesday. It is a big shift for a company whose design chief once said a touchscreen Mac would not be useful. The machine, which Apple unveiled on Wednesday from $2,899, ships on 6 November. Welch found that Apple took a “measured approach” to touch, with no oversized buttons or sweeping changes to macOS. Pinch-to-zoom and drag and drop felt smooth and quick, he wrote. The touch sensor is built into the OLED panel itself, rather than layered on top as on touchscreen Windows laptops. Small changes appear only when you touch the screen. Menus spread their items further apart so they are easier to tap, then look normal again when you switch to the trackpad or a mouse. The weak spot is the three tiny window buttons in the top left corner. Apple briefly enlarges them for fingers, but Welch found the animation “a bit sluggish” and not very graceful. Apple’s last touch experiment on the MacBook Pro, the Touch Bar, was phased out from 2021 after years of complaints. Touch makes iPhone Mirroring far better, since users can tap and swipe as they would on the phone. Developers can also test their iPhone and iPad apps in a way that feels closer to the real thing. Some people on social media have accused Apple of adding touch to justify a $400 price premium. Welch argues that most of the extra cost likely comes from the dual-layer OLED screen, which reaches 2,000 nits and is calibrated panel by panel. He still wishes Apple had added Face ID and mobile data. An Apple employee told reporters the company is only getting started on tuning macOS for touch. Buyers who want none of it can pick the M6 MacBook Pro, which keeps a regular screen. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. ServiceNow shares fall a record 25% after its bookings outlook misses
2026-10-29

ServiceNow shares fall a record 25% after its bookings outlook misses

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
ServiceNow chairman and chief executive Bill McDermott ServiceNow shares fell as much as 25% to $112.37 after markets opened in New York on Thursday. It was the biggest intraday drop since the company went public in 2012, Brody Ford reported for Bloomberg. The stock was on course for its worst day on record, according to Dow Jones Market Data cited by MarketWatch. The fall followed ServiceNow’s third-quarter results, released after the market closed on Wednesday. The shares had dropped 14% in extended trading that evening, Bloomberg reported. “This was a little bit uncharacteristic for ServiceNow standards, just in terms of the lack of upside,” Citi analyst Tyler Radke told CNBC. Subscription revenue rose 21% to $3.988bn in the quarter to 30 September. Analysts had expected $3.98bn, according to Bloomberg. Total revenue grew 20.5% to $4.106bn, against a $4.10bn estimate from analysts polled by LSEG, CNBC reported. Adjusted earnings were $1.00 a share. That compares with analyst estimates of $1.03, according to Bloomberg, and $1.02, according to LSEG. Net income under standard accounting rules (GAAP) fell to $277m, from $502m a year earlier. Current remaining performance obligations (cRPO) rose 20.5% to $13.65bn. The measure covers contracted revenue that ServiceNow expects to recognise over the next 12 months. Analysts had expected $13.56bn, MarketWatch reported. For the fourth quarter, ServiceNow forecast subscription revenue of $4.245bn to $4.255bn, growth of 22.5% to 23%. Bloomberg reported that this was about in line with analysts’ estimates. The company expects cRPO growth of 18.5% in the December quarter. Analysts had projected 19% on average, according to data compiled by Bloomberg. ServiceNow raised its full-year subscription revenue forecast to $15.78bn to $15.79bn. It also raised its free cash flow margin target by one percentage point to 36%. The company raised its target for annual contract value from AI products to $1.7bn by the end of 2026. Three months ago the target was $1.5bn, and at the start of the year it was $1bn. “Customers are also rapidly increasing their use of agentic AI, with year-to-date consumption in production up more than 13x year-over-year,” chief financial officer Gina Mastantuono said in the results statement. Analysts said the cRPO outlook was the biggest issue in the report, Nora Redmond and Hannah Pedone reported for MarketWatch. William Blair analyst Arjun Bhatia wrote that early contract renewals likely lifted the September quarter. That came at the expense of the December quarter, he wrote. Bhatia also cited public-sector customers moving to on-premises deals. Those are recognised as revenue upfront, not as cRPO. He added that more deals include termination-for-convenience clauses, which let customers opt out. BMO analyst Keith Bachman wrote that even if the outlook is conservative, it “still points to deceleration”. Derrick Wood of TD Cowen wrote that there are “lots of mechanical caveats to help explain away” aspects of the forecast. “But we think it could re-raise AI disruption fears,” Wood wrote. Analysts at JPMorgan, led by Samik Chatterjee, called the results “a bit noisy”. They wrote that the gap between cRPO and revenue growth is likely to reverse over time. Radke told CNBC that investors had not yet seen a payoff from ServiceNow’s new forward-deployed engineering team. The team works inside customers’ businesses to build AI tools. “I think you got to wait until next year to really see that inflection,” Radke said. The stock had gained 36% in the three months before the report, Bloomberg reported. In May, ServiceNow lined up a $4bn bond sale to refinance its Armis acquisition debt. On Thursday, the day of the share fall, Nasdaq futures fell after results from Alphabet and Microsoft. On the day of the results, ServiceNow also expanded its partnership with OpenAI. It launched a Control Tower Alliance for AI governance too. Its founding members include Anthropic, Amazon Web Services, Nvidia, Okta and OpenAI. CNBC reported that the results did little to overturn “SaaSpocalypse” fears, the concern that AI will upend traditional software business models. In September, Salesforce chief executive Marc Benioff dismissed the SaaSpocalypse as “crazy nonsense”. “This was more than another beat-and-raise quarter for ServiceNow, this was the inflection point for ServiceNow as the Super Intelligence Control Tower,” chief executive Bill McDermott said in the results statement. Leak exposes 41,000 Homeland Security staff, including ICE and CBP agents
2026-10-29

Leak exposes 41,000 Homeland Security staff, including ICE and CBP agents

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Records on more than 41,000 employees and contractors of the US Department of Homeland Security (DHS) are now online. They include names, email addresses, phone numbers, job titles and office locations. The staff work for Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE), Customs and Border Protection (CBP) and other DHS agencies. WIRED, which was given a preview of the data, reported the leak on Wednesday in a story by Dhruv Mehrotra, Maddy Varner and Dell Cameron. The transparency nonprofit Distributed Denial of Secrets (DDoSecrets) published the archive, which it calls “Icepick”, the same day. DDoSecrets says the data came from a hacktivist group calling itself the Department of Peace, and that it was taken from the Okta single sign-on system DHS uses. Reuters could not immediately corroborate the Okta claim. “We are aware of this report. Okta is secure, and we have no evidence our data or systems were compromised,” an Okta spokesperson told WIRED. “DHS is aware of a potential breach and is actively working to address the situation swiftly and effectively,” a DHS spokesperson told WIRED. The records run to 19 August 2026, the date of their last update. They cover about a seventh of the DHS workforce, according to WIRED’s analysis. CBP staff account for more than half. WIRED counted about 10,000 ICE records, roughly a third of them in Homeland Security Investigations (HSI), ICE’s investigative arm. BBC News counted more than 21,000 CBP staff and nearly 6,600 at ICE. Another 6,200 records belong to US Citizenship and Immigration Services, WIRED reported. Nearly 1,000 belong to the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), more than half of them contractors. WIRED found more than 55,000 permission groups in the records, which control access to internal systems. At HSI, 39 employees belong to groups whose names include “CUC”. Government court filings say that stands for “certified undercover operation”. Other group names reference a wiretap programme and appear to reference tools such as Palantir’s Falcon platform and PenLink, which analyses phone records and social media. Some groups reference AI tools, including OpenAI’s ChatGPT, Google’s Gemini and Microsoft’s Copilot. One, called “Grok.com”, includes eight DHS technology officials. More than 200 CBP accounts sit in a group referencing Mobile Fortify, a face recognition app that immigration agents use to identify people. Reuters could not immediately verify the entire trove, Raphael Satter and AJ Vicens reported. It matched personal information on six named employees to credit bureau records and breach data. It also called three employees and confirmed their phone numbers and other details. One said it was the first they had heard of a breach. Nextgov/FCW corroborated parts of the data, including a work phone number its reporter already had, David DiMolfetta reported. It also found that some records appear to be historical, naming CISA employees who have since left. In a note with the files, the hackers wrote: “They have our information, so it’s only fair that we have theirs.” A Department of Peace spokesperson linked the leak to a remark by DHS Secretary Markwayne Mullin, in a statement released by DDoSecrets. “On July 17th, Markwayne Mullin said he was ‘turning up the heat.’ This is what happens to ICE when you do that,” the spokesperson said. Swiss researcher and journalist maia arson crimew, who built a search tool for the data, said the dataset provides “FOIA material for years to come”. She said on X that she was not involved in obtaining the data. Alexander Leslie, a national security expert at the threat intelligence company Recorded Future, told WIRED the data could help adversaries. “If authentic and current, this is an extraordinarily valuable intelligence resource for those seeking to penetrate DHS,” Leslie said. Adam Steinbaugh, a senior attorney at the civil liberties group FIRE, spoke to WIRED about the names and basic work details in the archive. “This is information that would be on a business card,” Steinbaugh said. “That’s not something the government can criminalize.” On Thursday, crimew’s search site stopped loading after its registrar put the domain on hold, Zack Whittaker reported for TechCrunch. DHS had filed a takedown notice through ZeroFox, a security company authorised to seek the removal of data on its behalf until 2028. TechCrunch saw a copy of the notice. CentralNic, which holds the domain, did not tell TechCrunch whether it acted on a government demand. The notice asked the registrar to “stop serving the personal contact and location data of individuals who did not consent to its publication”, according to TechCrunch. Bluesky is blocking links to the DDoSecrets archive and labelling them “malicious”. The company told WIRED that the archive contains people’s contact information, shared without their authorisation. DDoSecrets co-founder Emma Best told TechCrunch that the dataset contains no home addresses or personal contact information. TechCrunch described the phone numbers in the data as work-issued. The leak follows ShinyHunters’ claims about FBI data in September. Earlier this month, the FBI removed an Accenture contractor after a separate breach. This week, a suspected hacking attempt took the Federal Election Commission’s filing system offline. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Google’s Gemini 4 Carbon tops AI rankings, but only cyber defenders get it
2026-10-29

Google’s Gemini 4 Carbon tops AI rankings, but only cyber defenders get it

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Google has released Gemini 4 Carbon, its newest frontier AI model, to a small group of cyber defenders. It arrives two weeks after Gemini 4 Argon reached paying users. Koray Kavukcuoglu, senior vice president at Google DeepMind and Google’s chief AI architect, announced it in a Google blog post on Wednesday. Carbon ranks first on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v5.0 with a score of 59. The Artificial Analysis index puts Anthropic’s Claude Opus 5.5 second with 58, and OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra third with 57. “Carbon illustrates the pace at which we’re pushing the frontier forward,” Kavukcuoglu wrote. Google says Carbon leads three outside cybersecurity tests, which it ran without guardrails. It scored 88.2% on SEC-bench Pro, which measures finding real vulnerabilities. It scored 54.8% on ExploitGym, which tests whether a flaw can be exploited. On CWE-bench, which measures patching, it scored 71.0%. On Google’s internal tests, Carbon was more than twice as successful as Argon at writing secure code. Google’s Chrome team found it more than 10 times better than another lab’s large frontier model at writing high-quality patches. Google did not name the lab. Google says its teams have seen Carbon run for days on long engineering tasks. These include tracking down and patching subtle bugs across large codebases. Carbon also climbed 89 points to fourth place on WebDev Arena, a leaderboard for AI-built websites. Google reports that Carbon makes about half as many unsupported claims as Argon. In its internal coding environments, high-severity misalignment alerts fell by almost 80%. Misalignment means the model acting in ways its developers did not intend. On Gray Swan’s prompt injection benchmark, no evaluated attack succeeded against Carbon. Argon’s attack success rate was 0.7%. On Terminal-Bench 4.0, Carbon’s rate of successful cheating was 0.0%. Google’s own comparison table shows two tests where Carbon does not come first. GPT-6 Astra leads on FrontierSWE 2, a software engineering test, with 65.5% against Carbon’s 61.2%. Claude Opus 5.5 leads on OSWorld, which measures computer use, with 87.2% against 81.5%. Artificial Analysis ranks Carbon second on its Coding Agent Index, behind Claude Code running Opus 5.5. On its knowledge test, Carbon answered 58% of questions correctly, up from 48% for Argon, Carl Franzen reported for VentureBeat. Its hallucination rate on the same test rose from 4% to 11%. That rate counts wrong answers among those Carbon did not get fully right. Google has not published a technical report for Carbon yet, VentureBeat noted. Starting this week, Carbon goes to a subset of cyber defenders. Google will add more trusted partners through its Fairwind Program. It is also taking part in the US government’s voluntary process for testing models before release. Developers, businesses and consumers will get Carbon “as soon as possible”, Google says. Argon took the same path. Google gave cyber defenders Argon first on 30 September, then opened it to everyone who pays on 13 October. Carbon will launch at Argon’s introductory price of $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens. A footnote in Google’s post says prices will later rise to $4 and $20. Google declined to say when, Ryan Whitwam reported for Ars Technica. Google announced Carbon on the day Alphabet reported its third-quarter results, a day after DeepMind released the Genie 4 world model. Sundar Pichai, chief executive of Google and Alphabet, brought up Carbon in his earnings call remarks. “With Gemini 4 Argon and now Carbon, which we announced today, we’re excited to be leading at the frontier,” Pichai said. Orange Cyberdefense joins Mistral design partner programme for Large 4
2026-10-29

Orange Cyberdefense joins Mistral design partner programme for Large 4

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Orange Cyberdefense will use Mistral AI’s latest model to help test clients’ systems for weaknesses, investigate breaches and speed up responses to cyberattacks. The French security firm joined the design partner programme for Mistral Large 4 on Thursday, becoming the first European managed security services provider to take part. The Orange unit plans to introduce the model gradually across its services. Its first uses will include technical audits and penetration testing, in which security specialists try to break into a client’s systems to uncover vulnerabilities. The model will also help teams assess how attackers might gain access to those systems and investigate incidents when a breach occurs. The aim is to help security staff understand what happened and respond more quickly. Mistral launched Large 4 in early October, a model with one trillion parameters, and the company has since made its weights available for download, allowing developers to run and adapt it themselves. A cybersecurity-focused version is now being tested by a small group of partners, which are restricted to using it on systems they own or have permission to assess. Orange Cyberdefense will run its security models on computers in Mistral’s European data centres. The company says this arrangement will help clients retain control over their data, an increasingly important consideration when using AI for sensitive security work. Mistral has made data control and deployment flexibility part of its argument for using open models rather than relying entirely on closed AI systems. “This alliance between Orange Cyberdefense and Mistral AI strengthens the foundation of cybersecurity augmented by trustworthy AI,” said Anis Trabelsi, head of innovation and AI at Orange Cyberdefense. The partnership builds on the security firm’s existing use of AI. Since April 2025, it has used a tool from Qevlar AI in its security operations centres, where staff monitor clients’ systems for threats. The companies did not disclose the financial terms of the agreement, and the rollout will initially target business and public-sector clients.
